#3144. The S&P 500 Fell in Both September and October for Only the 17th Time Since 1950. Here's What Usually Followed.

It's happened in around one year in five since 1950. How did the next two months usually go?

The S&P 500 (^GSPC +0.36%) closed September at 7,651.54 and October at 7,580.07. That's two monthly losses in a row, of around 0.5% and 0.9%, and the first time since 2023 that the index has dipped in both months.

October's loss came late. Heading into its last session, the index was up around 0.9% for the month, and a fall of about 1.8% on Oct. 30 sent it under September's close. Yet the S&P 500 has risen around 11% this year and finished October within about 3% of the record it set on Oct. 6.

I wanted to see what has usually come next. With month-end closes (price only, no dividends), I found 16 past years since 1950 when the index dropped in both months. That comes to around one year in five.

Most of these years finished well. The index rose over November and December in 12 of the 16, and the median two-month gain was around 3.7%. The four that didn't were 1957, 1987, 2000, and 2008.

The next 12 months looked even better. A year after the end of October, the S&P 500 was higher in 14 of the 16, and the typical gain was around 13%. Of course, two of these gains hardly counted, at about 0.1% after 1952 and 0.9% after 1977.

In 1989, the index had already climbed around 23% for the year through October. The next 12 months ran into a recession that started in August 1990, according to the National Bureau of Economic Research, and the S&P 500 finished that stretch down about 11%. In 2000, the index had peaked back in March, early in the dot-com bust, and it dropped around 26% over the next year.

This record looks a lot like a normal year's.

From 1950 through 2025, the index gained over November and December in 59 of 76 years, or around 78% of the time, and the typical rise was about 3.6%. After a down September and October, the rate was 75%, with a typical rise of around 3.7%. So two losing months haven't said much about how the year finishes.

The 12-month results look a bit more favorable -- 14 of 16, versus 60 of the 75 years from 1950 through 2024. But with just 16 cases, a gap that small could easily come down to luck.

And the starting year matters. Yahoo Finance's data for the index goes back to the end of 1927, and the years before 1950 have eight more September-October drops, mostly in the Great Depression.

Those did worse. The index gained over November and December in only three of the eight, and it was higher a year later in just four. I left them out because a market recovering from the 1929 crash arguably has little in common with today's.

By size, this year's dip was one of the smallest. Combined, September and October knocked around 1.4% off the index, the second-smallest two-month loss of the 17 cases, behind 1984's 0.4%.

The other years with two-month losses of around 3% or less didn't agree on much. The index climbed about 7% over the year after October 1951, 14% after 1984, and 21% after 2016. But it was flat after 1952 and lost about 11% after 1989.

Most of the big rebounds followed deeper fall declines. In 1990, 2020, and 2023, the index lost about 6% to 7% over September and October, then rose 29% to 41% over the next 12 months.

Just two of the 16 earlier years had a record close during September or October, like 2026 did: 1989 (on Oct. 9) and 2020 (on Sept. 2). The year after October 2020 saw the group's largest gain, but 1989 led to one of its two losses.

The index's valuation is another matter. Its cyclically adjusted price-to-earnings ratio, a measure that sets the price against a decade of inflation-adjusted profits, began October at around 41. Of the 16 earlier years, just 2000 got close, at about 39. In 1984 and 2016, it was around 10 and 27.

Overall, what usually followed was a gain, at about the same rate as in any other year. I think the two-month dip says little by itself, in either direction.

For buy-and-hold investors adding to an index fund like the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF on a regular schedule, I don't see a reason in this history to stop or speed up. I'd just keep my expectations modest, given where the valuation stands.

Daniel Sparks and his clients do not have positions in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Vanguard S&P 500 ETF. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3142. This clever banking scam keeps phishing by faking its own demise – how it works

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

You won't see the 'suspended' sign because it's not meant for you.

KELA researchers have exposed an active financial-scam infrastructure that hosts hundreds of fraudulent websites capable of faking their own suspensions to stay online. As of Oct. 25, 2026, 248 financial scam and phishing domains tied to 189 major US banks and financial services have been linked to the same infrastructure.

According to the cybersecurity firm’s report, published on Tuesday, the “Suspended” infrastructure operation runs websites that have cloned the official domains of victim organizations, with a particular focus on business banking. Many domains use the [keyword]-[bank].com format, slotting in keywords such as “secure,” “disable,” “protect,” and “cancel.”

Also: Beware of Bluetooth sniffers: How your favorite gadgets could be exposing your data

A handful of servers spread across two shared hosting providers run the backend of Suspended infrastructure, which has also been tied to phishing campaigns targeting cryptocurrency exchanges and government services in the UK, the Netherlands, Australia, and New Zealand. As a result, the researchers believe that either a single operator is behind the setup or that the server infrastructure is part of a wider phishing-as-a-service platform.

This phishing campaign stands out because of how the cybercriminals behind the scam are protecting it: by issuing takedown notices.

When you visit a website and see a message that it has been taken down or suspended, it’s usually because the domain has been associated with malicious or illegal activity, including phishing and scams. Law enforcement agencies, often with help from technology firms, find these websites, obtain the necessary court orders, and seize the infrastructure or accounts behind them to force a suspension through a registrar or hosting provider.

You see the notice, you move on — and this also goes for scanners and security teams.

However, in this case, the scam artists created a mechanism to show it themselves, to avoid being flagged and taken down. But it’s when they show it that matters.

The false suspension notice appears deceptively simple at first glance. A phishing kit embedded in the domain registers the IP address of each visitor, and on their second visit, they are shown a fake “account suspended” page. The kit is disguised as a challenge page similar to those legitimately used by Cloudflare.

Researchers also detected other decoys, including “Sorry, you’ve been blocked” notices, blank pages, and redirections to Google. Some pages also have reCAPTCHAs, and the researchers detected scripts that block browser developer tools, likely to slow analysis.

Also: Don’t use Muse? Meta’s AI agent can still access your private messages – here’s how

But why show fake suspension notices in the first place? To scanners and cybersecurity teams, it looks like someone else has already dealt with the malicious domain, and they can move on to the next suspicious website.

“That design targets exactly the people who could shut a site down,” KELA says. “A victim typically follows a link once. A scanner, a security team or a takedown provider checks a reported site and then checks it again to confirm, and the second check shows what looks like a completed takedown.”

As shown in the image below, while scanners would detect a suspension notice, victims would still see the cloned website, complete with lures to entice them into entering their information, leading to financial fraud and potentially identity theft. After all, one visit is enough.

As of this writing, there are 248 live domains, plus those taken offline after the researchers disclosed their findings to law enforcement; over 300 scam domains have been hosted on the same infrastructure since May this year. Unfortunately, this phishing campaign remains active, with new domains appearing every day.

One lead could reveal the operator: a remote desktop connection to the malicious infrastructure through a standard UK home broadband connection, but it has not been verified.

Use a phishing link checker to verify whether a URL is trustworthy, and remember that any email or message claiming to be from your bank may be fraudulent.

Typical signs of a bank phishing email include:

When it comes to organizations, KELA has released a free Bank Phishing Check tool that compares official domains and organizations against the websites uncovered during the firm’s research.

The Blaugrana host Unai Emery's improving Villains.

#3140. Sysco: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Sysco's margin story hinges on whether its fiscal 2029 efficiency targets hold as restaurant traffic continues to fall.

On its first-quarter fiscal year 2027 earnings call, held on Nov. 3, 2026, Sysco (SYY -9.33%) reported adjusted earnings per share of $1.19 alongside an acceleration in profit growth planned for the back half of the year. The foodservice distributor is pushing through near-term food cost deflation and higher fuel expenses by deploying artificial intelligence technology and acquiring cash-and-carry operator Jetro Restaurant Depot. Three takeaways from the call highlight where the business is headed.

The company is rolling out an enterprise artificial intelligence (AI) program with 30 distinct initiatives to streamline operations and logistics. Management expects these efficiency gains to generate $100 million in net cost savings in fiscal 2027 and $500 million by fiscal 2029.

Sysco is expanding its distribution model by acquiring Jetro Restaurant Depot (JRD), a cash-and-carry wholesaler serving restaurant operators. The transaction expands Sysco's reach into self-service store locations and remains on schedule to be completed by the third quarter of fiscal 2027.

Local case volume in the U.S. Foodservice (USFS) core segment grew 2.7% during the quarter despite broader restaurant industry traffic falling 2%. Productivity gains and the expanded adoption of AI360, Sysco's proprietary sales enablement tool, helped drive the volume acceleration.

Operating momentum continues to build across distribution networks, supported by multiyear efficiency programs and stable sales consultant retention. Management is watching fuel price fluctuations and commodity inflation trends while relying on customer surcharges and route optimization to protect operating margins.

Combining core operational execution with pending strategic acquisitions sets the stage for margin expansion in the second half of the year.

The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Sysco. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3138. Roku is taking away a fan-favorite screensaver – but you have alternatives

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

One of Roku's most popular screensavers is about to make its last splash. Here’s how to switch before Dec. 1.

A fan-favorite Roku screensaver is going away, and users aren’t happy.

Over the last several days, users with the Aquatic Life aquarium screensaver set as the default have noticed a message appearing when the screensaver opens. “Starting Dec. 1, 2026,” the message reads, “the Aquatic Life screensaver will no longer be available, and your screensaver will switch to Roku City.”

Also: The free Roku City screensaver game has me hooked

The screensaver, which displays a digital aquarium with tropical fish swimming back and forth, was available as a downloadable theme pack and was a popular pick for many years.

Users on Reddit quickly lamented the loss in many posts, with dozens noting that the aquarium was part of their daily routine. Roku responded to one post, saying, “That makes sense, and we hear you. Aquatic Life has a lot of loyal fans, and we’re passing your feedback along to the team.” The post noted that when “the fish swim off,” Roku devices with Aquatic Life will switch to Roku City.

You can browse to Settings > Theme > Screensaver to find another theme.

Several online reports indicate that the removal is a licensing issue and that the creator of the screensaver posted on Reddit that he removed the license after Roku’s sale to Fox. That thread has now been deleted, so the comment is unavailable. Roku hasn’t issued any statement of its own about why, but I have reached out to ask. I’ve also reached out directly to the screensaver’s creator.

If you’re looking for the exact screensaver, it’s still available as a 2-minute clip on Roku’s YouTube channel. It takes a few extra steps, but if you want to keep the screensaver running on your TV, you can create a YouTube playlist with just that video, set it to loop, and use the YouTube app on Roku whenever you want to see it.

Even though the announcement of the screensaver’s departure happened less than 24 hours ago, the comments section of that video is packed with users begging Roku to keep Aquatic Life.

Also: I started clearing my Roku cache, and it fixed my biggest TV complaint

Alternatively, you could open one of the many YouTube videos that have aquarium displays and let one of them run for as long as you want.

There are also several dozen aquarium apps for Roku. I checked out several of them, and while they don’t look exactly like the Roku one, they do fill the void. Note that if you opt for one of these, most of them do have ads that play before the aquarium starts.

Lastly, you can download the original app for your phone or computer from SereneScreen.

#3137. Don't let inflation bite — how to hedge with TIPS, commodities and infrastructure funds

The Consumer Price Index hit 3.7% in September – the latest sign that inflation remains a problem that isn't going away.

It's a tough situation for investors looking for protection, but there are some good ways to hedge.

Long-term Treasury Inflation-Protected securities (TIPS) yield 2.9%, near multi-decade highs. Beyond TIPS, analysts suggest commodities, infrastructure, and other assets that can hedge against rising prices.

While inflation is down sharply from its 9.1% peak in 2022, it's still well above the Federal Reserve's 2% target. Headline CPI's 3.7% rate in September topped estimates for 3.6%. Core CPI, excluding food and energy, reached 2.6%, also higher than expected. Rising energy prices drove much of the increase. But the Personal Consumption Expenditures index, the Fed's preferred gauge, also remains elevated at a core 3% rate.

Americans' inflation concerns are also rising. The New York Fed's Survey of Consumer Expectations showed the one-year inflation outlook in September climbed to 3.9%, its highest level since May 2023.

Despite their name, TIPS haven't provided much inflation protection this year. The iShares TIPS Bond ETF, which tracks the ICE U.S. Treasury Inflation Linked Bond Index, has a total return of -1.16% year-to-date.

Like most other bonds, TIPS have been hit hard by rising rates. The 10-year Treasury note is up sharply this year and spiked Wednesday to 5.29%, near the highest levels since 2002. Bond prices and yields move inversely, and as rates climbed many TIPS funds suffered.

But higher yields have made TIPS more attractive. The 10-year TIPS' 2.9% yield is up from roughly 1.9% early in the year.

"Given where rates are, it's getting interesting," says Jason Draho, head of asset allocation Americas at UBS Global Wealth Management.

TIPS pay a fixed coupon, or interest rate, but as inflation moves higher, the principal value of the bond increases. The adjustments reflect changes in inflation, based on the non-seasonally adjusted CPI for All Urban Consumers, with a three-month lag.

The upshot is that TIPS investors earn interest on a higher principal value over time. That's the essence of the protection compared to traditional Treasuries.

TIPS' poor performance this year isn't because of a surge in inflation expectations. Almost all the increase has been due to rising real yields — the nominal yield minus expected inflation. Real yields are up because of factors like robust economic growth and a U.S. fiscal deficit around 6% of gross domestic product (requiring far more government borrowing).

If nominal rates were rising just due to inflation, then TIPS total return — a mix of yield and price — would be fine, said R.J. Gallo, chief investment officer of global fixed income at Federated Hermes. But that isn't the case.

"TIPS have produced losses in total return because their income accruals have been insufficient to offset the price decline produced by the rising underlying real yields," Gallo explained.

TIPS maturing in 10 years or more are highly sensitive to duration risk, Draho notes. "You're making a big call on rates not rising any further if you buy TIPS, especially longer maturities of 10-year plus," he said.

The bull case is that inflation comes down quicker than expected. That's not the market's expectation, based on short-term Treasury yields, notes Draho. But if inflation does ease next year, which he expects, the yield curve would adjust lower.

One way to manage duration risk is to own individual TIPS rather than a fund that has to maintain duration consistent with a benchmark index. Investors could create a TIPS bond ladder with varying maturities, pegged to their income needs over the next decade, for instance.

If you hold a TIPS to maturity you may experience price volatility, but you'll get the original principal or inflation-adjusted price, whichever is greater, when the bond matures.

Short-term TIPS provide more protection against rate increases and they look attractive, said PIMCO portfolio manager Greg Sharenow.

"While as a firm, our view is that inflation will ultimately come down, that view keeps on being pushed out as real world events unfold," he said. "We've seen over and over again the last five to seven years — where we're now in our third supply shock — that you end up actually underpricing versus realized inflation," he added.

Funds focusing on short-term TIPS include Vanguard Short-Term Inflation-Protected Securities ETF, iShares 0-5 Year TIPS Bond ETF and PIMCO 1-5 Year US TIPS Index ETF.

TIPS, of course, aren't the only way to go. Rick Rieder, BlackRock's chief investment officer for global fixed income, suggests a multi-pronged strategy, including equities, commodities, and alternative assets.

"I'd start layering in more income, keep interest-rate exposure moderate, and then what a lot of people are doing is use things like alternatives to to create more balance in a portfolio," he said. "That makes a lot of sense to me."

Broad inflation-protection funds include State Street Multi-Asset Real Return ETF, Fidelity Strategic Real Return Fund and PIMCO Inflation Response Multi-Asset Fund.

The PIMCO fund's holdings include TIPS, commodities, gold and real estate investment trusts.

"The idea is to have a number of different exposures so that we can have a more balanced return and not try to be so specific in getting the exact right asset class at any one moment," said Sharenow, a manager on the fund.

He adds that he sees "significant value" in TIPS, which have the heaviest weighting in the portfolio. Commodities are the next largest holding. They have price "pass-throughs," which is how much companies can shift costs to customers through price increases. That is beneficial to investors, he noted.

Separately, the fund holds about 10% each in gold and REITs.

Draho recommends commodities, based partly on long-term asset-allocation studies that include the 1970s when inflation was far higher. "Over a long period they can help reduce risk and improve diversification," he said. "Their benefits have been most pronounced when inflation was overshooting expectations."

Rather than picking individual commodities, he suggests owning a basket that's consistently rebalanced. Gold and oil, for instance, perform well in different economic and monetary policy scenarios, and it can be tricky to get the timing right.

Commodity ETFs to consider include the Invesco Optimum Yield Diversified Commodity Strategy ETF (PDBC) and the iShares S&P GSCI Commodity-Indexed Trust (GSG).

Another asset class Draho suggests is infrastructure — operators of roads, ports, bridges, and other hard assets. Companies in the space tend to be toll takers, collecting long-term revenue under contracts that include inflation riders or built-in price adjustments.

Investors can get exposure to infrastructure through ETFs such as the iShares Global Infrastructure ETF (IGF) and ProShares DJ Brookfield Global Infrastructure ETF (TOLZ).

Draho isn't sounding alarms on inflation but says it makes sense to add protection against another move higher.

"The risk now is there will be supply side shocks that could push up inflation again," he said.

— CNBC's Daren Fonda contributed reporting.

#3136. What’s Causing iPhone Duo Cracked Screens? A Teardown Finds a Possible Weakness

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

iFixit disassembled an iPhone Duo and found some surprises, including the fact that it's "the most repairable foldable" it's ever pulled apart.

The iPhone Duo‘s expansive inner screen and the hinge that folds it into a more compact closed shape have gotten a lot of attention during the first weeks it’s been available — and not the type Apple would prefer. From the get-go, reports of cracked displays popped up, not the thing you want to see on a phone you just dropped $2,000 (and up) to get.

We’re currently conducting our own testing on the screen, folding the iPhone Duo 900 times per hour with a custom-made machine to see how long its hinge will last. At the time of writing, it had passed 235,000 folds without issue, but other people with a Duo haven’t been so lucky. Most commonly, people have reported issues with display cracks and lines of faulty pixels appearing along the hinge, according to MacRumors and Reddit.

As quickly as the faults have come to light, repairability experts at iFixit think they might already have discovered the culprit. Using teardown footage, patents and CT scans it collected while breaking down the iPhone Duo to ascertain its repairability score, iFixit built a 3D hinge model to identify where things could be going wrong.

What it found was that the breaks seemed to be occurring in the same place: just below the cable that connects the two screens, in a spot where a bunch of different connectors all meet.

“All those edges meeting in this spot mean lots of possible culprits,” iFixit’s Elizabeth Chamberlain wrote in a lengthy blog post. “That center pivot looks different from the rest of the hinge. Could it be getting stuck? Could something else be getting stuck?

“Here’s one possible answer. In our closing simulation … the center support bar fails to drop far enough. The screen’s curved fold comes down onto it, creating a small stress hotspot near the reported break location.”

iFixit has created an interactive CT scan that allows you to play around with the Duo and see how the hinge supports, cable and nearby components fit beneath the display.

Apple didn’t immediately respond to a request for comment about the faults, but anyone experiencing issues with their iPhone Duo should be protected under warranty at this stage. Apple will charge $999 for inner screen replacement at a later date — whether that makes an AppleCare subscription a worthwhile investment is a choice only you can make.

Any reports of screen problems are obviously bad news for Apple, but the company does also have reason to be proud of itself.

iFixit has declared the iPhone Duo “the most repairable foldable smartphone we’ve ever taken apart.” The phone has scored a 5/10 repairability rating, which might not be as impressive as the iPhone 18 Pro and 18 Pro Max’s scores of 7/10, but still makes Apple the industry leader in terms of foldable repairability.

In its teardown, iFixit called out the trim surrounding the Duo’s screen as being particularly easy to pull away in contrast to the Samsung Galaxy Z Fold 8’s trim, which “crumbled” as it was removed. The flat layout of the Duo’s components also garnered praise from iFixit. Being able to access components like the cameras and logic board without taking the whole device apart makes repairs simpler if any of those components require replacement.

iFixit also praised Apple’s electrically releasable adhesive, which can be used to easily remove the battery by passing a low voltage through it. This is an innovative repair technology that Apple pioneered and is also used across its other devices. It allows the cells to be removed without any bending or prying that could result in a puncture risk to the battery.

Finally, a key part of making tech more repairable is ensuring that the companies making the products also make instruction manuals and official parts accessible to people — whether they’re doing the repairs themselves at home, or taking them to a third-party repair shop. iFixit is still awaiting official parts from Apple, but it praised the company for publishing its repair manual so quickly.

Major points were deducted for the hinge and inner display — both of which would be difficult and time-consuming to repair.

“For a first attempt, Apple has done well,” Chamberlain wrote. “The easy battery removal alone is something we’d like to see on every foldable. But of course we’ll hold Apple accountable for making replacement parts available, and here’s hoping that the next generation of the Duo features a more affordable inner display and a more accessible hinge repair.”

#3135. Why DuPont Stock Trounced the Market on Tuesday

The cyclical slump in the chemical industry didn't affect one of its top names in the third quarter.

The chemical industry is in a prolonged global slump these days, so it was heartening to learn that DuPont (DD +8.61%) had a beat-and-raise third quarter despite that considerable challenge. The veteran American chemical company posted its third-quarter results Tuesday morning, and the reaction to them was strongly positive -- so much so that its stock closed the day almost 9% higher.

DuPont also posted strong growth in key fundamentals. Its net sales for the quarter rose by 4% year over year to just under $1.87 billion, while net income from continuing operations under generally accepted accounting principles (GAAP) surged by 68% to $171 million. Non-GAAP (adjusted), per-share profitability also bounced, improving by 62% to $2.09.

With those results, the company edged past the average analyst estimate of $1.84 billion for revenue. It notched a more convincing bottom-line beat, as those pundits were collectively modeling an adjusted earnings per share (EPS) figure of $1.86.

Compounding the two beats, DuPont also substantially raised its bottom-line guidance for the entirety of 2026. Management now believes the company's adjusted EPS will come in at $7.47 to $7.52, well up from its preceding range of $7.17 to $7.32. It didn't tweak its net sales projection much; it has been narrowed to roughly $7.19 billion, down from a range of $7.16 billion to $7.19 billion.

DuPont posted strong showings in the healthcare, aerospace, and industrial water client segments. These made up for sluggishness in the lines most exposed to the downcycle.

DuPont's management has proven very nimble in divesting businesses subject to the cycle and in concentrating on segments with resilient demand. This has rightfully made investors significantly more bullish on its prospects, although I'd caution that the overall market remains weak. On Tuesday, the stock approached its one-year high, but I suspect it could top it soon.

#3134. Windows 11 can run better on PCs with 8GB now, Microsoft says – here’s why

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Tech > Services & Software > Operating Systems > Windows > Windows 11

The latest updates to Windows 11 offer improvements in speed, performance, and memory management.

The more memory your Windows PC has, the faster it should perform, especially when juggling multiple apps, files, and other data. My laptop has 32GB of RAM, while my desktop has a hefty 64GB, and both are absolutely zippy. Does that mean computers with only 8GB of memory, or even less, are also-rans? Nope, not according to Microsoft.

Also: How much RAM does your PC really need?

In a new blog post published Tuesday, Microsoft touted the gains in speed, performance, and memory management in Windows 11 achieved by the latest updates. These especially impact PCs with just 8GB of RAM, which can now run multiple apps and files faster and more efficiently than before.

To reach this goal, the company said it had three notions in mind.

The first was to make better use of the existing memory on each Windows 11 PC. The second was to protect the tasks that need to feel instant even as more memory gets used. And the third was to ensure that key apps and Windows require less memory. Yes, a computer with 16GB, 32GB, or 64GB of RAM can better handle more intensive activities. But Microsoft wanted to make sure that systems with 8GB could also keep pace.

Based on its own lab tests of PCs with 8GB of RAM, the company claims that Windows is now 20% more responsive and that multitasking is 40% faster than before the latest updates. Here’s how.

As more memory gets allocated, Windows must move inactive apps and files from RAM to the hard drive. When you need to access those apps and files again, you have to wait for Windows to move them from disk storage back to RAM. Normally, PCs with less memory have to do a lot of this disk swapping.

One way Windows eases the pain of this process is through compression. Here, the operating system shrinks less-active data into a smaller format so it can remain in memory. Though Windows has used compression for many years, Microsoft has fine-tuned the technology to determine how much to compress, when to move data from RAM to your drive, and when to fetch it back, especially on PCs with 8GB of memory.

Also: 5 apps I always install on new Windows PCs – most are free

As such, Windows can now keep up to 2GB more of your active data in memory rather than moving it to storage, Microsoft said. An inactive app, file, or browser that you leave open is less likely to have to reload from your hard drive when you return to it.

Opening the Start menu, launching File Explorer, right-clicking a folder or file, and searching for an item are all tasks that need to feel instant; otherwise, Windows itself feels sluggish. To achieve this on PCs with less RAM, Windows tries to prioritize certain actions you need to accomplish while backing off background tasks like search indexing, Microsoft said.

Here’s one example. After you update your computer and reboot, Windows has some housekeeping to do. But any setup work that occurs shouldn’t affect how long it takes to open an app or file.

Also: How to upgrade your ‘incompatible’ Windows 10 PC to Windows 11 – for free

The latest version of Windows 11 speeds up these types of tasks and interactions by up to 20% compared with the January 2026 version, according to Microsoft.

However, multitasking places the greatest strain on a PC with only 8GB of RAM. To analyze this, Microsoft said it conducted two tests on the same PC with 8GB of memory. One test measured the current version of Windows 11, while the other gauged the January 2026 flavor. Here, the testers opened 20 tabs in Microsoft Edge, kicked off a 4K video, and launched large files in Word, PowerPoint, and Excel, one after another. The current version of Windows is up to 40% faster than its predecessor, the company said.

To reduce the need for RAM among Windows and certain key apps, Microsoft said that it has improved how memory is managed and when it’s used.

As one example, memory is allocated to Word, PowerPoint, Excel, and Outlook more efficiently, resulting in a 360 MB savings in RAM. This maneuver also extends to third-party apps such as WhatsApp, which has reduced its memory requirements.

Also: 11 Windows apps I replace on every new PC (and the ones I keep)

On PCs with 8GB of memory, the Widgets app loads only when you open it and frees up about 200 MB of RAM when you close it. In Microsoft Edge, inactive tabs now go to sleep after 15 minutes, down from two hours previously, reducing overall memory allocation.

Across Windows, Microsoft 365, and Edge, those improvements reduced the growth in required memory by up to 900 MB, Microsoft said.

How does Microsoft test these scenarios to see whether the latest Windows updates and fixes are helping PCs with less memory?

“We test those fixes across a broader collection of over 20,000 PCs in our labs, including current 8 GB models, using automated workloads based on activities such as browsing, working in Microsoft 365 apps, joining video calls, and downloading files in the background and even using high-speed cameras to measure the time from an action, such as opening Start or launching an app, to the moment the result appears on screen,” the company explained.

Also: Why your RAM options cost 4X more now than last year

The ability to use Windows without much RAM on your PC is critical these days in light of the “RAMageddon” crisis. With AI companies scooping up massive amounts of memory and flash storage chips, remaining supply is limited, and the prices have surged. The more memory you add to a new or existing PC, the more you’re going to pay. That’s always been true, but now more than ever.

Of course, memory is only one part of the picture. Your PC’s processor and other hardware also play an important role. Here, Microsoft found that a 2026 Dell XPS 13 with 8GB of RAM showed 94% faster multi-core CPU performance than a 2021 8 GB model. But the same 2026 8GB PC performed on par with a 2021 16 GB system. This shows that memory does matter for multitasking, but that the processor also impacts performance.

Whether your Windows 11 PC has 8GB of RAM, less, or more, how can you take advantage of the latest enhancements? For that, you’ll want to make sure that you’ve updated to the newest version of Windows. That would be Windows 11 26H2, build 26300.9818 or later, according to Microsoft. But that includes the latest optional preview build released in late October. If you don’t want to install that one, you’ll be fine with build 26300.9711 for now.

Also: The best laptops: Expert tested and reviewed

To check, head to Settings, select System, and then click About. The section for Windows Info shows the current version and build. If you need to update, stay in Settings and go to Windows Update. Assuming your PC qualifies, you should find the latest Windows 11 26H2 build ready to install.

Beyond updating Windows, you also want to keep your apps current. If you use Microsoft 365, open any app in the suite, click the File menu, select Account, click Update Options, then select Update Now.

With most other apps, you have to update them manually or individually. To ease the process, consider a good software updater program. These will automatically scan most of your installed applications to identify which need updating, then download and install the updates for you.

#3133. DuckDuckGo fires back at Google: Schools don't need AI to search the web

Join the Tom's Guide Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

The search giant is giving schools more control over AI, but there's a big catch

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Google says it's giving schools more control over AI, but there's a meaningful catch that parents can't ignore. Schools that want to keep AI out of the classroom will also have to give up Google Search through student accounts.

In a Tuesday announcement, Google said it will disable access to several products, including Gemini and Google Search, for K–12 student accounts unless school administrators turn them on.

But there's one thing schools won't be able to do: Keep Google Search while blocking its built-in AI features.

"If a school is not comfortable with that and simply wants AI off, then they have the choice to not have Search on at all," Chris Phillips, Google's vice president of education, told Chalkbeat.

The changes, expected to roll out in early 2027, could leave school districts choosing between allowing AI-generated answers in Search or finding another way for students to research their homework.

Google has spent years integrating AI into its search engine, most visibly through AI Overviews, which provide generated summaries above traditional search results.

Now, those features are becoming a sticking point for schools that want students to research independently without relying on AI-generated answers.

Sign up to the Tom's AI Guide weekly newsletter summing up all the biggest AI news you need to know. Plus, analysis from our AI editors and tips on how to use the latest AI tools!

"Search and AI are deeply integrated," Chris Phillips, Google's vice president for education, told The New York Times.

According to Phillips, students in schools that prohibit AI entirely will not have access to Google Search through their school accounts. Instead, they'll see a message indicating that the service is unavailable.

The restriction applies to school-managed accounts under the new controls,

Google has also made it clear that administrators can enable individual products, including Gemini and Google Search, rather than accepting every AI service as a package. But the problem is much greater because schools still cannot offer an entirely AI-free version of Google Search.

Not everyone agrees with Google's explanation for keeping AI in Search.

In a statement to Tom's Guide, DuckDuckGo pushed back on the idea that schools must accept AI-generated answers to keep using a search engine.

"There is no technical reason why Google can't turn AI off," the company said, arguing that AI Overviews is a search feature similar to maps, flights or weather that Google can choose when to display.

DuckDuckGo points to its own search engine as an alternative. Unlike Google, it allows users to adjust how often AI-generated answers appear or turn them off completely.

The company also offers noai.duckduckgo.com, a version of its search engine that automatically disables AI features and filters AI-generated images from results without requiring users to change any settings.

Schools looking to avoid Google's restrictions can also make DuckDuckGo their default search engine, according to the company.

Alongside the changes to school controls, Google is expanding Guided Learning, an AI-powered feature designed to help students understand subjects rather than simply giving them answers.

Guided Learning is already available in Gemini, and Google announced that the feature is now available globally in Google Classroom.

The company is also bringing similar capabilities to Google Search, beginning with a pilot in selected U.S. schools later this month.

For example, a student who searches for help with a physics problem might receive questions and explanations that guide them through the solution rather than an immediate final answer.

Someone researching a book report could receive prompts encouraging them to think through the material. Google calls this approach "productive struggle," the idea that students learn more when they work through challenges rather than immediately receiving solutions.

In Google Classroom, teachers can assign Guided Learning activities based on class materials and receive feedback about where students might need additional support.

Google says its research shows that Guided Learning can improve learning outcomes. However, the effectiveness of these tools in everyday classrooms will depend on how students use them and how well the AI avoids simply completing assignments for them.

There are already questions about those safeguards. In testing reported by The New York Times, the consumer version of Guided Learning produced a detailed essay when given an Advanced Placement history prompt. Google says the school version includes additional restrictions.

The changes come amid growing concerns about how AI tools affect children and teenagers.

AI can be useful for explaining difficult concepts, brainstorming ideas and helping students study. But educators also worry that students may become too dependent on chatbots, use them to complete assignments or trust answers that aren't always accurate.

In September, New York City and Los Angeles, two of the country's largest school districts, introduced restrictions on student access to generative AI tools on school devices.

Some school officials have specifically wanted the ability to disable Google's AI Overviews while preserving conventional web searches.

Google's new policy does not provide that option. The company is reversing an earlier decision to enable certain AI features by default in Google Classroom for schools that had already permitted Gemini access.

That reversal gives administrators more control over which Google AI products students can use. But it stops short of letting schools completely separate Google Search from its built-in AI features.

For families, the most important thing to understand is that these changes are not taking effect immediately. Google says that it will share more details with schools in the coming months, with the new controls expected to arrive in early 2027.

Whether a student loses access to Google Search through their school account will depend on the choices made by their district or school administrators.

Schools can enable specific Google products individually, but those that want to keep AI entirely out of Google Search may need to consider alternative search engines or research tools.

Parents concerned about AI in their children's classrooms can ask their schools which Google services are currently enabled, whether students can access Gemini, and how the district plans to handle the upcoming changes.

It's also worth asking whether students will be using Guided Learning and what oversight teachers will have over those interactions.

#3132. Siri Is Getting Ears on the Apple Watch: Here’s What That Means for Your Privacy

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Two controversial new features arriving in beta this week will let the Apple Watch rewind and recap conversations.

Your Apple Watch is about to start listening a little more closely — if you want it to.

A beta rolling out this week gives Siri ears on the Series 12 and Ultra 4, with new features that can recall what was said in the last 15 seconds and summarize conversations throughout your day.

Audio Intelligence, as the new set of perks is called, consists of four audio-related features: music recognition, sound recognition, Live Rewind and Siri Recap. The first two, which have been available since launch, include a faster Shazam that can recognize songs without even opening the app.

I’ve been testing it in beta and noticed it responds much faster, especially since I don’t even have to open the app to identify a song. Songs now appear automatically in my watch’s Smart Stack. This even includes the lullabies I sing to my toddler at bedtime, though understandably, it hasn’t been able to identify those.

Sound Recognition can alert people who are deaf or hard of hearing to sounds such as a crying baby, barking dog or doorbell.

How to access: You have to enable the feature on your iPhone, under Settings > Accessibility > Sound and Name Recognition and toggle it on. You can even select which sounds you want it to identify.

In my testing, it successfully identified my fake cough, the doorbell and the car horn. But then it spent the rest of the day gaslighting me, telling me there was a dog barking or a car honking when there clearly wasn’t, which made me question both my own reality and its listening range. But this may require further testing, since I’m still on the beta versions of all these.

The newest features, which are out now in beta, are the most controversial, and I’ll be testing them more this week.

At any point during the day, Live Rewind lets you double-click the Digital Crown to pull up a text snippet of what was said in the previous 15 seconds, kind of like the audio version of Live Photos (which lets you see a few seconds of video before and after you pressed the shutter).

How to access the transcribed text? If you do nothing after seeing it on screen, it disappears. You can also ask Siri about it, or save it to the Siri app to view later on your phone. Old audio is continuously replaced by new audio and then deleted, so if you don’t trigger the feature to recall the last 15 seconds, it’s gone.

This is for unplanned moments where you need to revisit information without having to ask the speaker to repeat themselves. Apple’s keynote example showed someone reviewing the specials menu at a restaurant after the server had already walked away from the table. Whether you want to look away from the person speaking down at your watch instead of just asking them to repeat what they said is more of an etiquette question, but it could be helpful during large presentations.

Siri Recap can listen in the background while enabled and generate high-level summaries and key points of conversations throughout your day. This can be anything from a parent-teacher conference or work meeting to spur-of-the-moment conversations or brainstorming sessions with coworkers that you wouldn’t go out of your way to record with Voice Memos.

How to access it: Similar to Live Rewind, the notes from these recaps can either be saved in the Siri app and exported to the Notes or Journal app or deleted. They automatically delete after seven days if you don’t delete them manually.

Both features are opt-in, meaning you have to go out of your way to enable them during setup for them to work.

Once they’re on, Live Rewind requires you to manually double-tap the digital crown to activate it. Siri Recap is completely customizable.

You can leave it on at all times and let Siri decide what counts as important conversations to recap, set it to a schedule based on important meetings for the day, or enable it manually via the new Siri Recap icon in the Control Center on your Apple Watch like you would Focus mode or Do Not Disturb.

If you forget to turn it off, you’ll get a reminder in the morning telling you it’s on. You’ll also get reminders to check your recaps throughout the day and notifications when schedules start or stop, so you’re aware when it’s active.

And it won’t work for conference calls or FaceTime if you have your AirPods or headphones in. It only works with audio it can pick up through the speaker.

Even if you do decide to leave all four on all day, Apple says they won’t ding your battery. The Apple Watch Series 12 gets around a day and a half of use (in my real-world testing), while the Ultra 4 gets anywhere from two and a half to three days of use.

This is possible because of Apple’s new S11 chip and its dual architecture in which one part handles background tasks without draining the more power-hungry main processor.

The watch doesn’t save audio. It processes raw audio inside a hardware-isolated compartment on the S11 chip, called the Secure Exclave, that Apple says it can’t even access.

Siri Recap doesn’t send a full transcript to the cloud, either. It condenses the text first, deletes the full transcript and sends only that condensed version to Private Cloud Compute. It omits financial data, authentication details, personal identifiers, language related to self-harm and government secrets.

Still, there’s something about explicitly giving the watch on my wrist the ability to listen to conversations that doesn’t sit right with me and many others. Especially when those conversations involve other people who may have no idea it’s happening.

There’s a chime that alerts people when Live Rewind is active, but to my knowledge, there’s no other warning when Siri is recapping a conversation. Smart glasses like the Meta Ray-Bans have an indicator light when they record video, though even that isn’t foolproof, since there are ways to get around it.

Maybe I’ll change my mind once I can actually test it. Apple has certainly given me reasons to trust how it protects data. I’m just not convinced yet that this is the intelligence I want my watch to have.

The features are rolling out in beta with the update to iOS 27.3, initially in English, with more languages planned later.

I’ll be testing it over the coming weeks, so check back for more.

#3131. Ferrari: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

The business continues to see steady global demand across its model lineup, stretching client waitlists by several years. That multiyear backlog protects revenue stability across international markets and shields the company from short-term economic shifts.

That long-term strategy hinges on maintaining brand scarcity while expanding technical capabilities.

#3130. I tried Sonos’ latest app features – these 2 small upgrades fix big annoyances

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Home & Office > Home Entertainment > Speakers

New Sonos software and firmware updates are rolling out. Here's what you can expect.

Sonos’ promise to release its AI-upgraded voice assistant is nearing its “Fall 2026” deadline, and before fans can use the highly anticipated update, the company continues to make small improvements to its app and player features.

Also: Sonos Ace Ultra vs. Sonos Ace: Who should upgrade

This time, Sonos added System Health, a new in-app page to access a more comprehensive view of your system(s), though it’s only available on iOS for now. However, a new blog post states that iOS and Android users can expect volume presets, more interaction with the app miniplayer, and spoken voice prompts for the Ace Ultra headphones.

I poked around in the app and tried some of the latest features. Here’s what to expect.

If your system consists of multiple speakers, a mix of modern and legacy speakers, or multiple systems, you’re bound to experience a hiccup and be unsure which speaker is causing the issue.

It usually takes me some time to Google my issue and scroll through the Sonos Community forum, Reddit, or the official Sonos help documents to find suggestions and troubleshoot it. Sonos’ new System Health feature provides an overview of your systems, showing your network status and name, as well as the number of speakers and rooms in each system.

Also: I used Sonos’s new home theater feature – it’s a competition killer

According to Sonos’ blog post, System Health can detect a Double Network Address issue, which usually occurs when you introduce a new router to your home or plug a mesh system into your ISP-provided router. The result is two home networks, and your Sonos speakers will split their connection between them, flagging “System not found” errors, missing speakers, or connection dropouts.

System Health is in the Settings tab, under the Help Center menu.

Once you tap into your system, you can see all of its speakers, with unconnected speakers bearing a red “Not connected” badge, and wireless speakers showing a black “Powered off” badge when they’re off.

Then, you can see a speaker’s connection information, including status, Wi-Fi signal strength, signal/noise ratio, frequency, IP address, and more. You can also find its volume output, Trueplay setup status, line-in connection, voice control status, serial number, hardware model and version, and which Sonos platform it operates on.

System Health can also show you your account details, Sonos ID, Controller ID, and your current app build version. System Health comes after Sonos updated the System Updates tab, which now displays an overview of your app, system, and headphone update status. This page also links to the Sonos release notes.

Sonos’ blog post states that this feature is new and still open to change, especially if the Sonos community has a particularly overwhelming response to it. The blog post also disclaims that this feature is a first look, not a deep dive.

However, after a brief first use, I see it as a great way to quickly access all the details you need about your systems and speakers, especially if you’re doing some rewiring, moving a speaker to a new room, or general troubleshooting.

System Health is rolling out to iOS users first. I updated my Sonos app in the App Store and was able to access it, but Sonos’ blog post states that this update is a phased rollout.

Updates to the Sonos app and its player are a long time coming. After receiving several new ways to control your speakers’ volume (I think we’re up to three), Sonos is realizing that granular volume control is a high priority for many Sonos users, including me.

The in-app volume dial is cool, and the interactive volume scrubber is a nice touch, but there’s nothing more jarring than wanting to play some tunes on my kitchen counter speaker, only for it to blast at 80% from when my husband was watching football the night before.

Sonos’ latest big update to volume this time around is Presets, a way to start playback in a specific room at a set volume. I haven’t found this feature in my app’s update yet, but I think it’s because my hardware needs an update. Presets will be available on iOS and Android.

Fortunately, I did use another update to the Sonos miniplayer: swipe to skip. What a teeny, tiny but useful feature. It works just like the miniplayer in Apple Music and Spotify; when the app is open, you can swipe left to skip to the next song.

One major caveat: swiping right to replay a track doesn’t work — at least not yet. Swiping both ways seems like a given to me, so it feels odd that you can’t. Still, being able to swipe the miniplayer to the next track is better than always having the main player open to toggle tracks. This feature is also coming to iOS and Android.

Sonos’ miniplayer is getting so much better; you can swipe to the next track and easily control volume from it, even when you have the Ace or Ace Ultra connected (I tried). The in-app volume dial also works with Sonos headphones.

Also: Sonos Play vs. Sonos Move 2: Why I’d go with the $200 cheaper Bluetooth speaker

Speaking of the Ace Ultra, a firmware update is available. A new Vocal Guidance feature can announce the battery percentage when you power on the headphones, when the audio mode changes from ANC to Transparency, and which device your headphones are connected to via Bluetooth.

It feels like this update is the final stretch before Sonos 27voice arrives, as the release notes suggest. It appears Sonos is laying the groundwork for speakers to support the AI-powered voice assistant in Early Access. Once it’s here, I’ll test it as soon as I can.

#3124. Traders pile into 'bubble puts' on CoreWeave as neocloud stocks plunge

A key corner of the artificial intelligence trade is taking a beating on Wednesday and traders are betting it could get even worse.

Shares of neocloud companies – businesses that offer access to the GPUs powering artificial intelligence as a service – plunged Wednesday after debt yields for Nvidia-backed CoreWeave continued to reach new heights. (An article in The Financial Times that the company is targeting insurance companies for further debt financing apparently didn't help things either.)

Meanwhile, in a post on X, Social Capital founder Chamath Palihapitiya raised questions about a private credit bubble alongside a screengrab showing CoreWeave's bonds hitting a 15% yield. Elon Musk replied "yikes." CoreWeave stock plunged 15%. Nebius Group and Iren, popular trades connected to the data centers build-out, also fell as much as 10%.

Options volume in CoreWeave surged to more than seven times the 30-day average, Nebius almost four times, and Iren twice the normal volume. More calls traded than puts in Nebius and Iren, but in both, more calls were likely initiated by sellers than buyers, according to Cboe LiveVol data.

The most bearish action was in CoreWeave, where traders bought just shy of 200,000 puts, compared to 106,000 calls.

One standout trade happened around 10 a.m. ET when someone opened a new position of 26,000 37.5-strike puts expiring Dec. 18 that traded near the ask price, suggesting the puts were purchased, according to SpotGamma data.

"These are bubble puts," said Scott Bauer, CEO of Chicago-based Prosper Trading Academy. "These are not rip-ups that you buy thinking they'll go to zero. This is the hub, the center of the AI trade, and likely some kind of margin play for someone to hedge their exposure in highly correlated stocks or maybe even the Nasdaq."

The contracts sold for 74 cents apiece for a total of $1.9 million and need CoreWeave to erase another 42% over the next two months to pay off. Prior to this trade, there were just over 1,000 open contracts, meaning the position is a new one.

CoreWeave IPO'd in March 2025 at $40 per share.

"Maybe this is someone that was in early or in on the IPO saying this is my breakeven level," Bauer added. "Maybe they couldn't get this kind of protection in other stocks, the index, or other products."

#3123. Apple: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Rising memory chip costs are squeezing margins even as a massive device base opens new revenue paths.

On its fourth-quarter fiscal year 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 2, 2026, Apple (AAPL -1.90%) announced record September quarter revenue of $114.5 billion. Surging device adoption across global markets reinforces its long-term revenue ecosystem. Three key takeaways from management's comments highlight what lies ahead for shareholders.

Rising memory chip prices are compressing hardware profitability, forcing price increases on Mac computers and iPad tablets. While price hikes help offset cost inflation, higher expenses directly shrink gross margin, which measures profit left after production costs.

An expanding hardware footprint fuels predictable recurring services income, including cloud storage and subscription apps. A broader installed device base creates lasting monetization opportunities regardless of short-term hardware upgrade timing.

Tight manufacturing capacity for advanced system-on-chip (SoC) integrated microchips restricted product shipments across major hardware lines. Supply chain friction caps immediate revenue growth even as customer demand for new hardware remains high.

Revenue is tracking toward a record December quarter despite supply chain bottlenecks and elevated component costs. Hardware innovation and software integration support long-term competitive strength while leadership manages memory pricing discipline.

Ongoing research and development (R&D) investments focus on on-device artificial intelligence (AI) capabilities and customized silicon design. These architectural investments deepen consumer ecosystem lock-in and sustain product pricing power.

The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Apple. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3122. Trump Asked Grok AI About Invading Venezuela. Democrats Want His Chat History

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

An AI chatbot was consulted about military action, raising questions about security risks, sycophancy and hallucination.

House Democrats are trying to force the White House to turn over President Donald Trump’s conversations with AI chatbots, including when he consulted Elon Musk’s Grok about deposing Venezuela’s former president Nicolás Maduro at the start of the year.

In a joint letter to Trump last week, Rep. Jamie Raskin, a Maryland Democrat, and Rep. Robert Garcia, a California Democrat, argued that the president’s AI prompts — and the responses he received — should be considered public records under the 1978 Presidential Records Act. Lawmakers also raised ethical and security concerns about the president’s reliance on “unsecured, privately operated AI systems” to carry out foreign policy decisions, alleging that Trump consulted Grok, but not Congress, on military deployment to Venezuela.

According to public reporting cited in the letter, in December 2025, just weeks before ordering the military operation and Maduro’s arrest, Trump spent multiple hours prompting Grok about how Venezuelans would respond to the leader’s capture. Trump reportedly “came away thinking Grok was ingenious.”

Garcia and Raskin argue that Trump’s use of AI “exposes the country to malign influence from unknown and unaccountable third parties.” A January executive summary of a General Services Administration report, reviewed by The Wall Street Journal, concluded that xAI’s Grok “does not meet the safety and alignment expectations required” for general federal use and may pose a security risk in classified settings.

Using Grok as an informal policy adviser also raises questions over Musk’s political influence over a system he owns and has repeatedly altered to reduce content filters. “If the American President is relying on the policy advice of a Musk-designed, Hitler-friendly chatbot with a predilection for child pornography in order to carry out his official duties, Americans have a right to know,” reads the joint letter (PDF), referring to Grok’s infamous antisemitic episode in 2025 when the chatbot referred to itself as “MechaHitler.”

The letter also refers to the White House’s ongoing use of AI chatbots in military adventures abroad, including in the war with Iran: “If the United States military killed dozens of innocent schoolchildren based on the faulty advice of an AI chatbot, we have a right to know that too.” In June, months after the US military strike on an Iranian elementary school killed at least 123 children, the Pentagon’s artificial intelligence chief said the Grok chatbot’s continued operation was “a matter of paramount national security.”

Reached for comment, the White House referred CNET to the Pentagon, which didn’t immediately respond. Rep. Garcia’s office didn’t respond to a request for comment.

The case for using an AI chatbot for tactical decision-making is usually framed in terms of efficiency. But large language models, or LLMs, are merely probabilistic text generators that can often sound authoritative while producing biased or fabricated information. In a military context, those failure points can end in missile strikes, arrests and civilian deaths.

AI models are also known to amplify existing biases, feeding users with responses that agree with their strongly held beliefs. This sycophancy often leads to answers that mirror those found in an online echo chamber. One journalist found that previous Grok models had “tripled” sycophancy rates compared with popular ChatGPT and Claude models, representing a shift in “behavior that MIT researchers identify as actively harmful.”

In other words, if the president believes he has a brilliant invasion plan, it’s Grok’s modus operandi to serve as a “yes man” and validate that course of action.

Another problem with using an AI chatbot for military decisions is the material on which a model is built. An LLM is a compilation of code that produces contextual responses based on its massive training data — a wealth of information scraped from the internet, including (but not limited to) blog posts, scientific papers, legal documents and code. Grok models, in particular, are also trained on the public interactions from X, a platform infested with bot farms and right-wing engagement bait.

When Trump was prompting Grok in December 2025, officials alleged that the chatbot told the president “that Maduro was a repressive and deeply unpopular dictator and that many Venezuelans would likely celebrate his downfall.” In the wake of the Venezuelan invasion, X users continued to prompt Grok about the operation, asking if the South American country “deserved” to be bombed.

The other problem is what’s commonly referred to as “hallucination.” LLMs are known to invent outputs if they don’t have the information necessary to respond to a particular prompt. From fabricated cases in legal briefs, phantom citations in government reports and other serious documents bungled by AI, there’s no way to completely prevent false intelligence from trickling through.

Earlier this year, according to reporting cited in the letter, US military officials received an intelligence report alleging that a Chinese vessel in the Middle East was carrying components for a nuclear weapons program. Only as the military prepared to intercept the ship — an action that many say could have escalated into a confrontation with China — did officials discover that the report’s author had relied on an AI chatbot and misidentified the cargo. The error didn’t cause a war, but it revealed how dangerously a fabricated AI output can slip into a military decision chain.

Moreover, if the president or his advisers use a consumer AI product rather than a hardened government system, those chats could be processed by a private company’s infrastructure. Garcia and Raskin have asked the White House to explain whether it restricts classified information from AI chatbots and whether vendors, subcontractors or other external parties can access prompts, outputs, logs or metadata.

Trump’s use of AI in the Venezuela operation would be concerning even if it were isolated. But Garcia and Raskin argue that it fits a broader pattern of AI entering high-stakes government decisions without adequate safeguards.

The Pentagon said in a sworn statement (PDF) that xAI’s latest models enabled US forces to deploy over 2,000 munitions to target 2,000 different locations within 96 hours during Operation Epic Fury, the US campaign against Iran. Grok isn’t the whole story. Palantir’s Maven Smart System, which integrates intelligence from satellites, drones and other sources to identify military targets, reportedly played a central role in the US campaign against Iran. The Defense Department has also awarded contracts worth up to $200 million each to Anthropic, Google, OpenAI and xAI to accelerate military adoption of advanced AI.

Globally, AI systems are being used to make decisions about who lives and who dies. Researchers have regularly pointed to Israel’s indiscriminate bombing of Gaza as a clear example of AI-first warfighting. According to analysis cited by Georgetown Security Studies Review, Israel’s Lavender system marked roughly 37,000 Palestinians as suspected Hamas or Islamic Jihad operatives early in the war, with an estimated error rate of 10%.

The Lavender system “dramatically expanded” Israel’s kill list by relying on signals such as communication patterns and group-chat membership, producing misidentifications that included Palestinian police, aid workers, relatives of militants and people with similar names or nicknames. A related Israeli system, “Where’s Daddy?” tracked marked Palestinians and alerted the military when they entered their homes, striking multiple private family residences.

While the political shock of an AI chatbot influencing military strategy dominates the headlines, the real battleground is legal. Demanding that President Trump’s AI chatbot prompts and outputs be marked as official government property under the Presidential Records Act would turn AI conversations into public records, opening the door for Congress to subpoena the executive branch. Raskin and Garcia are requesting a full description of the president’s personal use of the technology and a closed-door briefing by Nov. 11 to review the administration’s AI protocols and cybersecurity safeguards.

Regardless of how the legal side shakes out, the question is no longer whether AI will be used in war, but whether the public will be allowed to see where AI begins and human responsibility ends.

#3121. HomeView with an arm? Apple reportedly plans $1,000 swiveling smart panel

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

The HomeView Ultra is expected to feature a robotic arm and 9-inch display. Shades of Amazon's discontinued Echo Show 10?

The Apple HomeView is the company’s first dedicated smart home display, and new reports of how Apple plans to improve the product are already circulating. Apple is reportedly planning to announce a HomeView with a robotic arm as early as next year. The dynamic display is expected to cost up to $1,000.

Also: Apple’s HomeView may double as security camera

The HomeView isn’t even on the market yet — it will be available for purchase next week, starting at $299. This 7-inch smart panel features a speaker that doubles as a stand, and Apple will also sell a wall-mounted option.

As a smart home device, the HomeView is designed to serve as a platform for interacting with your Apple ecosystem, including Apple Home and Siri. It’s a smart dashboard that lets you view all your smart home devices and security cameras, as well as your calendar, messages, and other integrated apps.

The Information reported that Apple is rumored to be working on a higher-end smart display for 2027, with shipments starting in 2028. The new device, codenamed J595, may be branded HomeView Ultra and feature a robotic arm and a 9-inch display.

The new HomeView’s screen would be mounted on an articulated mechanical arm to swivel the display, tracking users during FaceTime calls to keep them on-screen. The device could feature proactive smart home automations, such as turning on devices or triggering a room scene when someone walks into the room.

Also: Google’s latest Home updates just fixed my biggest annoyances

The same report indicates that the hardware has been complete for a couple of years, but launch delays are attributed to software development issues. Integrating advanced AI features and proactive automation has, according to The Information, been a challenge for Apple.

A swiveling smart home display isn’t entirely new. Amazon used to sell an Echo Show 10 with a display that tracked users to keep them in the camera’s view during video calls, though this model has been discontinued.

However, one that works within the Apple ecosystem could change how users interact with their home interfaces, given the popularity of FaceTime calls and the prevalence of iPhone users.

Also: 4 big Amazon smart home announcements

A HomeView Ultra could even anticipate users’ needs by tracking people’s movements and automatically executing routines and scenes depending on where they are in the home.

While there’s reason to doubt whether most smart home users would welcome such technology in their homes or consider it too invasive, premium robotic smart displays indicate that companies are standardizing interactive central hubs for the home.

Right on time, Expedia, Hotels.com and Vrbo just rolled out more than 30 new travel features. Expedia Group, which owns all three brands, said the goal is to make trip planning and execution easier with practical AI.

“Travel is a series of decisions, and travelers want the joy of discovery and planning to last through every stage of the journey,” Shilpa Ranganathan, chief product officer at Expedia Group, said. “That’s why we’re building AI-powered experiences across our brands that help travelers make informed decisions, give them flexibility and value, and personalize every step of their trip. And when travelers need a person, our human agents step in with the full context to make things right.”

Expedia says it’s introducing a more personal homepage, flexible travel packages, more straightforward bookings for families, interactive maps of your travel destination for easier planning and expanded airport perks like Clear Plus discounts and free lounge access for flight delays.

The platform is improving its AI Compare experience to help you compare multiple hotels in one place. The comparisons are personalized to your interests and preferences for location, amenities, price and policy details.

The Hotels.com homepage will also adapt to the type of trip you booked, such as a vacation or work trip, and include suggested links tailored to your booking, like options to extend your stay and access to receipts for easier work-trip expenses.

The Airbnb rival is launching several new features and services, including a tool to make group bookings easier to manage, an option to reserve now, pay later, and enhanced customer support.

With Vrbo’s natural language search, you can describe in plain language what kind of rental property you’re looking for. Rather than spending hours researching options one by one, you can simply say you’re looking for a beachfront home in South Florida with a kitchen and outdoor gathering space that’s dog-friendly. The enhanced search feature allows you to narrow down your options without relying on traditional filters.

Vrbo’s Arrival Experience is getting an upgrade to make your rental details more accessible. Key property information, such as check-in and checkout times, door codes, directions and house rules, will now be aggregated in one place, so you can stay on top of your booking without searching through multiple emails or messages.

#3117. SUSE lays off 1 in 10 workers – citing AI and automation

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Squarespace also announced layoffs this week, but explicit ties to AI remain inconsistent.

According to exclusive reporting by The Register, enterprise Linux provider SUSE will lay off one in 10 of its nearly 2,000-person workforce due to “automation and AI,” CEO Dirk-Peter van Leeuwen told staff via email.

Also: How many jobs is your state losing to AI?

Van Leeuwen noted that AI is rapidly changing how software companies function.

“AI capabilities are advancing exponentially, forcing us to radically reimagine our work,” he said, adding that “the organizational design and cost base that SUSE is built on today will not carry us forward successfully.”

He did not specify which roles would be terminated, but The Register reports that staff will be notified by Nov. 4.

AI is impacting many parts of the tech industry, including open source. Just last month, Linus Torvalds announced he had used Claude Code to write a Linux patch, which Linux enthusiasts read as an evolution of his stance on AI.

By some reports, businesses are finally seeing some ROI from AI after a few years of uncertainty. But that remains localized, ironically, to tech itself. As one economist recently noted, AI has primarily increased revenue per employee at major tech companies, while leaving most other companies unchanged.

The trade-off for that growth is, at least in some cases, layoffs. But layoffs credited to AI in some way don’t mean that human roles are always being replaced. Last month, Microsoft announced it would buy out 60,000 of its workers — some of which are in its Copilot division — to prioritize AI efforts, though it’s also likely the company is reacting to spending a significant amount of money on AI infrastructure.

Jobloss.ai’s layoff tracker shows primarily tech companies — including Uber, Salesforce, and GitLab — in its recent entries, but job loss attribution to AI is still mixed, and the number of roles cut isn’t always jarringly huge.

It’s also hard to tell whether a layoff strategically frames pre-AI plans as being due to AI capabilities or is actually a result of them. That question circulated around Block’s major layoffs earlier this year. Squarespace also announced layoffs of 19% of its staff yesterday, though company communications don’t explicitly name AI. Still, vibecoding has impacted website builders overall, though Squarespace itself has invested in AI offerings.

Also: Why replacing staff with AI backfires

Van Leeuwen’s email to staff acknowledged where SUSE has already begun to change, though it’s unclear whether he meant by adopting AI tools internally.

“Across SUSE, modern tooling is already changing our daily workflows, enabling teams to move faster with less operational overhead and flatter reporting lines. The requirement for some existing roles has greatly diminished. Other roles will evolve over the next few years,” he said.

While several tech companies have announced highly visible layoffs this year that they’ve also framed as related to AI, economists agree that the massive cross-industry layoff wave many fear has yet to arrive. Gartner even predicts that companies will actually rehire 30% of the employees they tried to replace with AI — and at a higher cost — by 2029 if they don’t “overhaul workforce planning.”

Still, AI investments, vibecoding, and shifting priorities are changing the software scene overall.
