#3115. DigitalOcean: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Balance sheet discipline, not just the pace of new data centers, is what the call suggests could protect DigitalOcean's margins if demand cools.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 3, 2026, DigitalOcean (DOCN +0.79%) raised its revenue outlook for both full-year 2026 and 2027. It's accelerating top-line growth. Expanding software returns on cloud infrastructure drive higher long-term operating margins. Three takeaways from the call stand out for long-term investors.

Customers building software powered by artificial intelligence (AI) rely on complete cloud services rather than stand-alone graphics processing units (GPUs), which are microchips designed for complex computations. Integrating essential cloud tools with hardware drives higher multiproduct usage across the customer base.

Maximizing revenue yield per megawatt of data center capacity allows cloud providers to expand profitability without relying solely on physical infrastructure additions. Software capabilities and higher-margin services increase annual recurring revenue (ARR), a metric measuring the annualized value of recurring customer contracts.

Maintaining low net leverage, which measures total debt minus cash relative to earnings, provides flexibility during periods of heavy capital expenditure. Conservative balance sheet management enables strategic equipment financing without overextending corporate capital resources.

Revenue growth is accelerating across product lines, supported by rapid execution on data center deployments. A diversified customer base and modular facility expansions provide operational advantages as computing demand grows. Management is monitoring the cadence of capacity expansion at Tier 1 data centers, which are facilities built to meet top industry standards for power redundancy and uptime.

Sustained platform adoption positions the business to capture higher software margins as workloads mature. Positive cash flow generation and low leverage maintain strategic flexibility to fund next-generation computing hardware while building long-term shareholder value.

#3114. Apple Is Putting an Age Limit on Falling in Love With AI

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Dating and AI companion apps have new age gate restrictions, while apps that share your location will have to disclose it.

Practicing flirting using Replika's AI system.

Falling in love with artificial intelligence raises a lot of questions about loneliness, human connection and what the heck we’re doing as a species. Apple has a more immediate concern: Are you at least 18?

The company is tightening its App Store rules, requiring dating apps and AI companions that offer romantic or sexual interactions to carry an 18-and-over age rating. The change applies whether you’re looking for love with another human being or flirting with a chatbot that never leaves you on read.

In a notice to developers on Monday, Apple said it will introduce two new content descriptors on Nov. 16: “Dating and Relationships” and “Shared Location.” Developers will have to disclose whether their apps offer dating features, including romantic or sexual interactions with virtual companions, or whether they allow users to share their real-world locations with others.

Location-sharing features may also affect age ratings, depending on the country. In Brazil, for example, apps that allow users to share their locations must carry a minimum age rating of 16, while dating apps require an 18-and-over rating. Developers have until Jan. 12, 2027, to complete the new disclosures for all their apps.

Most major dating apps, including Tinder and Hinge, already require users to be at least old enough to vote (in the US, that is). The more interesting part is how Apple’s rules could affect AI companions, which have become increasingly popular.

Last year, Character.AI, a platform where users can create and interact with fictional AI personalities, removed open-ended chats for users under 18. The company continued offering other features to younger users, but the decision came amid growing concerns about the emotional and psychological risks of forming relationships with AI.

A 2026 Common Sense Media report, which surveyed more than 1,000 children ages 9 to 17, found that many were already treating AI as a confidant. An earlier 2025 study found that nearly three-quarters of teens had used AI companions, with a third choosing chatbots over humans for serious conversations. The nonprofit has warned that such relationships can pose risks to minors, particularly around emotional attachment and dependency.

Apple’s announcement also follows the company’s expanded parental controls in iOS 27, which gave parents more control over their children’s apps, browsing and screen time. The update introduced features that let parents approve websites before their children visit them, set time limits for app categories and restrict access to potentially harmful content.

However, an “18-plus” rating doesn’t automatically mean everyone has to verify their age. The labels work with parental restrictions, while Apple may require users to confirm they’re adults before downloading those adult apps in certain regions.

So the new rules won’t necessarily stop teenagers from finding a digital soulmate. They should, however, make it harder for apps offering romantic AI relationships to slip past age-based content restrictions — regardless of whether a chatbot says you’re mature for your age.

#3113. CoreWeave credit default swaps hit distress levels as investors sour on AI debt

CoreWeave's credit stress continued to spiral on Thursday amid a broader sell-off in AI-related names.

CoreWeave — a so-called neocloud company — has taken on more than $51 billion of debt and obligations to finance its AI infrastructure build-out. It's now facing a rebellion from stock and bond investors as concerns mount about AI overspending and debt levels.

Yields on CoreWeave debt have continued to climb, with the yield to maturity on its 9.75% bond due in 2031 reaching 15.78%. Bond yields move inversely to prices. Meanwhile, the spread on its five-year credit default swaps, which are insurance against default, at one point soared above 1,140 basis points. It last stood at nearly 1,038 bps — a 16% week-over-week jump and 164% higher than a year ago.

Credit concerns are spreading into AI equities too. CoreWeave's stock dropped during trading Thursday before recovering and closing 5.94% higher. The moved followed Wednesday's 15.5% nosedive.

Other neocloud stocks were under similar pressure. Nebius also sank before climbing higher to close 1.29% and and Iren ultimately ended the day down less than 1%.

Anxiety about AI overspending is also spreading. For instance, shares of Micron Technology and Advanced Micro Devices sold off following a big sell-off in South Korean chip stocks. Micron closed up 1.87%, while AMD fell 1.5%.

While AI debt worries have been rising steadily, investors have suddenly grown skittish about the underlying finances of companies borrowing heavily to buy chips, build data centers, and rent out computing power.

Coreweave's credit default swaps are one place to see that stress.

Credit default swaps are contracts where buyers pay for protection in case the borrower can't repay its debt. The spread widening in CoreWeave's CDS means investors view the debt as riskier.

The market is "basically saying 'no mas' to AI debt without operating cash flow support," the Evercore ISI trading desk said.

A spread level of 1,000 basis points or above is generally a sign of credit distress, said Gavan Nolan, executive director of fixed income pricing at S&P Global Market Intelligence.

No one is questioning whether demand for CoreWeave's services is real, since the company has contracts with all the big hyperscalers, Nolan said. The question is whether the cash flows will ultimately arrive.

"It's obviously heavily negative free cash flow at the moment, and it needs that cash flow to arrive really to justify all the capex that it's spending," Nolan said.

CEO Mike Intrator dismissed the market moves on Wednesday, according to a memo obtained by CNBC.

"When you are building a new type of technology company there will be periods of time when the market misunderstands and acts irrationally," he wrote. "We are confident in our backlog which more than supports all of our debt obligations."

The backlog is over $100 billion, with around two-thirds of it with investment-grade counterparts including Microsoft, Meta, IBM and Caterpillar, Deutsche Bank said in a note Thursday. The firm reiterated its buy-rating on the stock Wednesday, calling the dislocation a buying opportunity.

"The problem is this kind of concern can prove to be a self-fulfilling prophecy, and risks triggering broader issues in other AI-related names," said Jim Reid, global head of macro and thematic research.

Domino effects may already be happening. Oracle's five-year CDS spread also spiked this week. It was last at 282 basis points, a roughly 10% increase in one week.

"A downgrade from its BBB rating would risk pressuring IG-only investors into forced sales," Reid said.

#3112. Your Apple Watch has 2 new controversial listening features – but you have to opt in

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Siri Recap and Live Rewind are finally here, two months after Apple unveiled the always-listening Apple Watch features. Here's how they work.

Siri Recap and Live Rewind, Apple’s two controversial, always-listening Audio Intelligence features, are now available on the Apple Watch Series 12 and Apple Watch Ultra 4 with the iOS 27.3 developer beta. Both features are opt-in.

Apple unveiled Siri Recap and Live Rewind in September alongside its Series 12 and Ultra 4 smartwatches, but it waited to release the features until after the smartwatches launched. Siri Recap provides a high-level summary of meetings, while Live Rewind continuously buffers audio so the watch wearer can pull up a transcript of the last 15 seconds.

Also: Apple’s Audio Intelligence is priming us for its real AI wearable future

To try both features today, update your iPhone to the iOS 27.3 developer beta, and then update your Apple Watch.

Within the Siri app, users will find a new section called “Recaps,” where both the Siri Recaps and Live Rewinds live.

Siri Recap isn’t listening for nitty-gritty details within a meeting or recording a transcript’s worth of text. It’s more geared toward a conversation’s key takeaways, like important dates, times, prices, and action items. A demo of the feature showed an off-the-cuff conversation between a parent and a child discussing college application strategies. Siri Recap took notes on the high school activities the student could pursue to include in their application, the topics they could write about in their essay, and the colleges they could consider applying to.

After a conversation, a user can save the meeting notes in the Siri app or export them to Notes or Journal. If they don’t save the conversation, it’s automatically deleted after seven days.

Apple designed the feature to exclude certain information from the Siri Recap. It includes a person’s name only if they introduce themselves or if their name is regularly brought up in conversation. Otherwise, it avoids identifying speakers. It also scrubs the audio of language that promotes self-harm, financial and authentication data, and personal identifiers.

You can enable or disable Recaps at any time through Control Center. You can create something akin to a Focus mode to automatically enable the feature once your Apple Watch detects you’ve reached your office or college lecture. Each morning, Siri Recap sends a notification to alert you that the feature is on. At the end of each day, you get a reminder to check your daily recaps in the Siri app.

The feature constantly assesses whether a conversation is relevant, optimizing its detection based on the conversation’s context, proximity, and length. It pauses summarization when a user is on a phone or FaceTime call, plays music, or uses earbuds. It won’t work when Bluetooth devices like AirPods are in use.

Double-press the Digital Crown to see a transcript of the past 15 seconds of conversation. The Apple Watch chimes and displays an animation to indicate it’s in use, then shows the transcript for 30 seconds.

Also: Is cellular on Apple Watch worth it? 4 ways people use it – and why I opt out

Live Rewind is built for those head-in-the-clouds moments when you miss a tidbit of information and can’t go back and ask what a speaker said. I think about how useful this could be in a college lecture or boardroom setting, where dozens of people’s conversations and questions prevent someone from asking a clarifying question about what was discussed.

The user can send the snippet to the Siri app to save it or ask about it (if they want to look up the definition of a mentioned term or get more information on the history of a mentioned topic, for example). If the user doesn’t act on it, the snippet is deleted. Any snippet you save is encrypted through iCloud. The audio buffer updates continuously, and unused audio is automatically deleted. It’s processed on the device and not sent to Apple.

Think of the Secure Exclave as a private island on the S11 chip where the Apple Watch Series 12 and Ultra 4 process audio. A secure audio buffer processes the raw audio, and a secure pairing mechanism privately enables the connection between a paired Apple Watch and iPhone.

Also: The Apple Watch Ultra 4 just hit its lowest price ever ahead of Black Friday

For Siri Recap, the S11 chip listens to detect whether the watch wearer is part of a current conversation. Once detected, audio enters the Secure Exclave, where it’s encrypted and sent to the Secure Exclave on the iPhone. The iPhone turns that audio into condensed text, essentially editing the conversation to remove filler words or irrelevant information, and then it’s sent to private cloud compute. Private cloud compute creates a recap, delivered to your iPhone’s Siri app.

This process ensures that no one, including Apple, can access the audio data.

Don’t want your Apple Watch to listen to you? Siri Recap and Live Rewind are off by default, according to Apple.

If your Apple Watch prompts you to enable Audio Intelligence during setup, you can choose which features to turn on.

On its support page, Apple said you can “turn on Siri Recap in the Watch app on iPhone.” Once it’s enabled, you can “turn it on or off at any time from Control Center.” To enable Live Rewind, Apple said to “open the Watch app, tap Siri, then tap Live Rewind and turn on ‘Double Click Digital Crown.’”

I haven’t tested these controls myself. I’d expect you can follow the same steps to switch Live Rewind off, but I haven’t confirmed that yet.

#3111. President Donald Trump Claims He'll "Veto Everything" if Democrats Retake Congress, and It Has Big Implications for the Stock Market

Democrats are widely expected to retake one or both houses of Congress, creating potential friction points for Wall Street.

With mere hours left on Election Night before polling closes across America, traders on prediction market platform Polymarket are forecasting an 89% chance (as of 7 p.m. ET) that Democrats sweep and retake both the House of Representatives and the Senate. For context, Republicans enter the midterms with 218 of 435 House seats and 53 of 100 Senate seats.

The potential implications of midterm elections on the Dow Jones Industrial Average (^DJI -0.38%), S&P 500 (^GSPC +0.36%), and Nasdaq Composite (^IXIC +0.83%) are massive. If a "blue wave" does unfold, things could get dicey, at least over the short run, for a historically expensive stock market.

President Trump may soon be working with a divided government. Image source: Official White House Photo by Molly Riley.

President Donald Trump hasn't been keen on the prospect of working with the opposition. In a recent Fox News interview, he proclaimed that "I guess I'll become Mr. Veto" if a blue wave takes shape, and has previously gone on record as stating that he'd "veto everything" sent to him by a Democrat-led Congress.

On the one hand, the political gridlock that ensues from a divided Congress can, in some ways, be beneficial for the stock market. A split Congress effectively takes all major tax and spending legislation off the table, likely locking in historically low personal and corporate income tax rates through at least the end of Trump's presidency in January 2029. Wall Street loves when things are predictable.

"I guess I'll become Mr. Veto." President Trump lays out his strategy for dealing with a potential Democratic-controlled Congress in an exclusive interview with Miranda Devine. Trump says he would veto legislation before eventually negotiating compromises, while expressing hope Republicans maintain control after Tuesday's midterms.

At the same time, a non-unified government can create friction points for the stock market. For example, a Democrat-controlled Congress could make debt-ceiling negotiations far more challenging. The debt ceiling is forecast to be reached in the first half of 2027. If Democrats take one or both houses of Congress, the odds of a lengthy government shutdown rise.

We'd likely also see friction between President Trump and Democrats over artificial intelligence (AI) regulation. Whereas Trump favors as little regulation as possible for Wall Street's and the U.S. economy's No. 1 catalyst, most Democratic lawmakers prefer to limit data center expansion and impose tougher restrictions on AI solutions, including large language models.

Even if President Trump successfully vetoes AI legislation from a Democrat-controlled Congress, simply putting AI regulations in the spotlight could be bad news for stocks.

From a statistical standpoint, no political scenario is less desirable for investors than a Republican president overseeing a divided Congress. According to Retirement Researcher, the S&P 500's average annual return over 34 years is just 7.33% under this scenario, compared to 14.01% to 16.63% under the other political scenarios.

While this might sound less than ideal, a little historical perspective changes everything. For instance, the S&P 500 has been higher at the six- and 12-month mark following every midterm election over the last 75 years, with November up 100% of the time!

S&P 500 is higher 6 and 12 months after every midterm election over the last 75 years 📈🤑🥳 https://t.co/N1Eu0fZDie

Furthermore, a 7.33% average annual return, while notably lower than other political situations, is still markedly higher than the long-term annualized returns for real estate, commodities, and Treasury bonds. Even at their "worst," stocks have continued to shine brightest for those willing to be patient.

Even if President Trump's threats to "veto everything" perturb equities over the short term, history says that the Dow, S&P 500, and Nasdaq Composite are well-positioned for success, regardless of which political party is in power.

The iPhone Duo likely won't make it into Santa's sleigh this year. Here's what Apple had to say about it.

Apple is unsure when it will have more of its foldable iPhone Duo in stock for customers to purchase ahead of Black Friday and the holiday shopping season.

#3108. Why Fiserv Stock Triumphed on Tuesday

The company showed signs of a potential turnaround in its third-quarter earnings.

Fiserv (FISV +12.46%), one of the sturdier and more experienced fintechs on the market, had a Tuesday to remember as far as its equity was concerned. After it served up an encouraging quarterly earnings report in the morning, investors eagerly bought the company's shares to send them almost 13% higher across that trading session.

Fiserv's third-quarter revenue not under generally accepted accounting principles (non-GAAP, or adjusted) slid by 1% year over year to $4.85 billion. Despite the slide, that figure was essentially in line with the consensus analyst estimate.

As for profitability, adjusted net income fell more steeply, decreasing by 15% to $936 million, or $1.75 per share. Those Fiserv-tracking pundits were expecting a slightly sharper fall, since they were collectively estimating $1.72.

Fiserv said in its earnings release that the erosion in revenue it suffered in its key banking customer segment was improving notably. Clients didn't migrate away from Fiserv back-end solutions as much as they had in other recent quarters, while the company reported "major client wins" in that business.

Investors were also heartened by management's doubling of its Project Elevate cost-saving and automation target to $1 billion from $500 million. Another confidence booster was the company's news that it would resume its share repurchase program, with a goal of buying back $500 million worth of stock in the current (fourth) quarter alone.

Fiserv maintained its full-year 2026 organic revenue guidance of a slump of 1% to break-even when matched against the previous year's figure. It's also sticking to its adjusted EPS forecast of $7.20 to $7.40, which is well down from the $8.64 it posted in 2025.

While no investor likes to see declines in either trailing results or guidance, Fiserv's moves position it for renewed growth in the near future. So now feels like it might be an opportune time to buy into the company's (hopefully continuing) recovery story.

#3107. AI Data Centers Have a PR Problem. OpenAI Is Proposing a Rebrand

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

As part of the company's post-midterms marketing campaign, data centers could be called “AI compute campuses.”

OpenAI is shifting its marketing strategy after the US midterm elections conclude this week, proposing to rebrand its data centers as “AI compute campuses.” The creator of ChatGPT is preparing a public relations campaign that could include regulatory lobbying and efforts to rehabilitate AI’s image, according to Axios.

The push coincides with a potential blue wave in Congress. OpenAI Global Affairs Officer Chris Lehane is reportedly trying to steer the AI policy debate and to send a message that “AI should be built by and for people,” as Axios reports.

The effort is a response to an increasingly enraged electorate, as backlash to AI data center construction becomes a unifying issue across party lines. A July Gallup poll found that as many as 70% of US adults are opposed to local data center construction, including Democrats, Republicans and independents.

Supporters of AI data centers often argue that the construction and operation of these massive AI server farms bring job growth to local communities. But studies show that employment is limited, and in some cases temporary. Residents continue to rally against the environmental impacts of water contamination, the burning of fossil fuels and noise pollution.

Lehane told Axios that OpenAI views the conversation as healthy and that “communities should hold companies responsible and accountable so they really and truly get a transformative benefit from these projects.” The company is trying to reframe AI adoption as a means to access economic opportunities.

OpenAI’s new PR campaign aligns with Silicon Valley’s effort to repackage the controversial technology so it can move full speed ahead with building more data centers, which the White House has touted as vital engines for the US economy. President Donald Trump’s recent move to rename AI as “super intelligence” was another bid to dress up the capabilities of frontier labs, which have been under increased financial scrutiny by investors.

A representative from OpenAI didn’t immediately respond to a request for comment.

(Disclosure: Ziff Davis, CNET’s parent company, in 2025 filed a lawsuit against OpenAI, alleging it infringed Ziff Davis copyrights in training and operating its AI systems.)

OpenAI’s post-midterms marketing package includes implementing speed limits and mandatory safeguards for AI model development, largely echoing Anthropic’s “pacing” talking points. The company is also trying to shape the international standards that dictate the technology’s development.

Up to now, a lack of regulatory oversight has left top AI labs largely unchecked, leading to incidents where frontier models slipped past flimsy safety guardrails and caused real-world disruptions across the web.

OpenAI’s lobbying strategy for an alleged “human-centered” path based on “trust” fits squarely in the company’s data center rebrand — all calculated to help deflect public criticism. If the company publicly advocates slowing its own AI development, it can be more effective in what critics call “regulatory capture,” a tactic that gives large AI companies significant advantages.

In other words, if OpenAI helps write the rules meant to govern the industry, it can shape regulations to protect its market dominance. If frontier labs are considered the experts in constraining the industry, they can create a legal moat around themselves, making it more difficult for smaller competitors to enter the AI industry, while protecting their bottom lines from any real damage.

The Trump administration has been a major boon to the “Magnificent Seven,” the group of major tech companies that heavily influence the S&P 500 and global financial markets. After cozying up to the White House during the past two years, the most influential figures in Silicon Valley have been rewarded with a lax regulatory environment and expensive federal contracts.

Many of the biggest tech and AI companies donated millions of dollars to the president’s inaugural fund and, more recently, to his $330 million White House ballroom project. (Apple and Nvidia were among the top contributors, at $25 million each.)

As it stands, Trump is working directly with AI companies to define security threats and respond on their own terms. If the political tide turns in DC, however, it would make sense for Big Tech to make contingency plans for operating in a potentially less favorable environment. Democratic politicians have generally eyed the AI gold rush with greater outward skepticism and scrutiny, with some members of Congress even backing a resolution opposing AI company bailouts in the event of a financial bubble popping.

Lehane told Axios he believes AI will be the primary issue voters care about in the next presidential election cycle, and that the midterms are a “bit of a movie trailer for what you’re going to see in 2028.” If nothing else, OpenAI is angling to make new political alliances before that time comes.

→ Baca artikel asli

#3106. Should You Buy Alphabet Stock After Anthropic Raised Its Google Commitment to $121 Billion?

The Claude maker just raised its minimum spending commitment to Google. Can it pay?

Anthropic, the artificial intelligence (AI) company behind Claude, updated its initial public offering (IPO) filing on Monday, Nov. 2, adding its third-quarter results. On Sept. 30, it had $95.6 billion in cash and cash equivalents and $36.3 billion in short-term investments -- around $132 billion combined, near its total at the end of June.

Against this, Anthropic has signed purchase commitments and leases worth about $895.4 billion, almost all for compute and infrastructure. The first filing, on Oct. 26, left that figure blank.

And one of the largest parts goes to Alphabet (GOOGL +0.37%)(GOOG +0.08%). Anthropic now says it committed to spend at least about $121.3 billion with Google between April 2026 and July 2033, no matter how much it uses. Last week's filing had that number at about $114.5 billion.

With Alphabet shares near $283 as I write, is this durable revenue for Google Cloud, or a bet on a customer that lost money from operations last quarter? I think it's mainly the former over the next couple of years.

Google Cloud's revenue backlog (contracted revenue not yet recognized) hit $560.8 billion on Sept. 30. Three months earlier, it stood at $513.9 billion. And a year before, Alphabet's total backlog, mainly Google Cloud, was just $157.7 billion, under an older count that left out contracts for a year or less. But growth has slowed lately, with around $47 billion added in the third quarter versus almost $52 billion in the second.

Anthropic's $121.3 billion minimum equals around 22% of that cloud backlog. Alphabet doesn't say how much of the number comes from Anthropic, and some of the minimum has probably been spent since April. But it's a big share tied to one customer.

Spread across a bit more than seven years, the minimum averages around $16.5 billion a year. Google Cloud's third-quarter revenue climbed 103% year over year to $30.7 billion, a rate of around $123 billion a year. This puts Anthropic's average minimum at about 13% of the cloud unit's current sales -- important, but not the whole business.

Anthropic also estimates it could spend another $125 billion to $150 billion on Google's custom AI chips, though some of that might go through third-party providers rather than Google.

For the next year, Anthropic looks able to pay. Around $69.8 billion of its commitments are due in the 12 months after Sept. 30. Its cash and investments cover it almost twice, not counting $14.8 billion of credit it hasn't tapped or any proceeds from its IPO.

The business is helping. Third-quarter revenue climbed around 47% from the second quarter to about $17.0 billion, more than 12 times its revenue for the year-ago quarter. Multiply that by four, and it's close to what comes due in the next 12 months.

Operating cash flow for the first nine months of 2026 was a positive $6.6 billion, versus negative $2.0 billion for the same period of 2025.

But growth cooled from the second quarter's 144% sequential jump. Anthropic had a third-quarter operating loss of $567 million, which included around $2.0 billion of non-cash charitable contributions. A year before, it lost $3.1 billion.

Still, compute and infrastructure costs rose back to 58% of revenue in the third quarter, against 48% in the second, but they were 148% of revenue a year ago.

Later on, the bills get far bigger. Around $447.5 billion of the commitments are expected before the end of 2030 -- about $105 billion a year, well over Anthropic's current revenue pace.

"We must grow our customer base and expand revenue rapidly in order to generate the cash flows necessary to satisfy these commitments," Anthropic wrote in the filing.

If Anthropic spends less than its minimum, it must pay Google the difference. Of course, that payment only helps Google if Anthropic has the cash. And Google has agreed to provide up to $20 billion of financing, in convertible notes or stock, subject to conditions, so part of what Anthropic pays Google might come from Google itself.

Alphabet doesn't need Anthropic to work out perfectly for its stock to make sense. Shares trade at around 19 times expected 2027 earnings, about 31% under their 52-week high. And Google Cloud earned $11.1 billion of operating income in the third quarter, around three times the $3.6 billion it made a year before.

Does the amended filing change my take on Alphabet? Not much. But I'd treat the next few years of the Google commitment as solid revenue and the later years as a bet on Anthropic's growth.

Overall, I think Alphabet stock is still worth a look at this price.

Daniel Sparks and his clients do not have positions in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Alphabet. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3105. Brian Sullivan: Why the 'real' price of oil is likely $30-$40 higher than where it is now

Oil, in reality, is $125 per barrel or higher.

That's the real cost of a barrel of oil because buying the physical commodity is a very different story from a number on traders' screens.

Oil futures are just that: paper contracts indicating a price today, tomorrow, next month or further out.  If you want to buy the physical commodity, it may be a totally different story — and price.

As I've reported, prices for oil delivery have skyrocketed. Last week, a VLCC (aka a "supertanker" in non-shipper parlance) was chartered from the U.S. Gulf Coast to China for $76 million. Another very large crude carrier went for $77 million from the U.S. to South Korea. That's 10 times more than the price of the same trip less than one year ago.

Assuming these ships hold about 2 million barrels of oil, that charter price means each barrel on the ship costs about $38 over the price of the oil itself.

I'm certainly not saying to avoid looking at oil futures prices, but you need to know that the price on the CNBC screen or CNBC.com isn't always the full price being paid. These days, any company that needs oil shipped by sea, rail or truck will pay a hefty premium over the price of the oil itself. So, while the volume of oil moving through Hormuz is closing in on pre-war levels, costs are not. And there's no sign that they're going anywhere but up.

You know what else is up? The stocks of the shipowners. Look at their moves this month and this year.

On a more serious note, I want to express my condolences for the awful attacks on the airport in Riyadh, Saudi Arabia. The cowardly strike on civilians—some of whom were religious pilgrims—took the lives of at least 14 people and injured more than 300.

The airport was attacked at least three times as travelers and energy industry professionals from around the world were flying into Riyadh for the World Petroleum Council conference. I was scheduled to fly in on Saturday, but the trip was canceled because of the strikes. The WPC went on as planned — here's one panel that took place Tuesday — and I look forward to seeing all my global energy friends again soon.

Thanks for reading—and watching. Be safe in these crazy and often scary times.

The supercap oil and gas stock to watch right now is Shell (SHEL). Shell is not a shell of its former self. It's back to being what energy investors wanted: a major player in global energy, focused on what it does best. Shareholders are being rewarded.

Shell hit a new high this week. While analysts remain bullish, the stock is getting close to its target price.

Shell may be underappreciated for its huge liquefied natural gas business. RBC analyst Biraj Borkhataria writes: "Without a sustained resumption in Qatari volumes and with European storage levels below seasonal lows heading into winter, European gas prices continued on an upward trajectory in 3Q26. With HH broadly unchanged throughout the summer, export margins have increased to the widest levels seen since 2022, on our estimates nearly double the level in 2Q26 (~$13/mmbtu vs. $7/mmbtu)."

Borkhataria expects Shell and other companies with huge U.S. LNG positions — TotalEnergies (TTE) and BP (BP) — to benefit from wider margins on exports to Europe.

Speaking of RBC and energy, the firm lists ConocoPhillips as one of its top 30 global stock ideas. The company is covered by analyst Scott Hanold, who believes it has a "returns-focused value proposition, a strong balance sheet, and peer-leading distributions." He loves its "global and diverse" asset base and management's clear "investment propositions." Hanold has a $152 target on COP. That's more than 20% upside.

In oil and gas, refiners and big energy stocks are generating cash for investors, and Morgan Stanley is optimistic more is coming.

The firm says the diesel shortage is now spilling over into gasoline, potentially marking another leg of the "golden age" for refiners. Morgan Stanley has been revising its earnings estimates higher for companies including ExxonMobil (XOM), Valero (VLO), Phillips 66 (PSX) and PBF (PBF).

Fun Fact →  While nearly every other company calls Texas home, refiner PBF is headquartered in a suburban office park just off a highway in New Jersey.

Two big calls to quickly highlight in power production.

First, Truist says you have to buy First Solar (FSLR). The analyst upgrades it to Buy with a $270 target. Tariffs should help the Phoenix-based company, giving it pricing power for at least four more years. The Section 232 tariffs—which are too complicated to detail here—should help First Solar raise solar module prices and boost earnings. We agree, having written about the company and stock back in July.

Another call on the Power Insider radar: TD Cowen is starting coverage of TeraWulf (WULF) with a Buy rating and a $21 target. That indicates about 50% upside for the stock, but it's actually one of the Street's lower price targets. TeraWulf and other AI power companies have seen their stocks hit hard following months of big gains, but Cowen analyst Michael Elias says the valuation is now attractive and a likely lease in the second half of 2027 at a Kentucky location could be a catalyst.

In the wake of the terror attacks on Saudi Arabia, we spoke with RBC Capital Markets' Helima Croft about the attacks, energy and more.

The entire energy world is watching Europe's natural gas storage levels. The question is whether the continent will have enough gas to make it safely through the winter. Power Insider wrote about the topic in the last issue.

The fact that JPMorgan highlights expected weather patterns should tell you how much temperature matters when it comes to gas storage. The early forecast calls for October and November to be average or slightly warmer than the 10-year average. The red line represents the 10-year average.

Europe has been largely "saved" by mild temperatures over the past few years. Natural gas storage levels are lower this year than in years past, so the thermometer will matter a lot when it comes to how well countries can, ahem, weather the winter. Random, but interesting.

#3104. Google Is Forcing Schools to Choose Between Its Search Engine and AI

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Experts weigh in on the promises and pitfalls of Google’s new policy, which leaves schools unable to use Search unless they opt into its AI tools.

Google will automatically turn off its AI products for K-12 student accounts, requiring school administrators to manually opt in before students can use the technology, the company announced in a blog post on Tuesday. But there’s a big catch: If schools choose not to enable AI, students won’t be able to access Google Search.

In August, the company’s AI products were automatically enabled in Google Classroom, Google’s free learning management system for students of all ages. Tuesday’s announcement reverses that decision.

In October, tech and child-safety organizations wrote an open letter to 150 US school districts and their leadership, requesting that districts disable Google Gemini AI on school devices, concluding that “children are at risk of being harmed.”

“After listening to schools, teachers, leading researchers and policymakers, we are now updating our approach to controls for Gemini, Gemini Notebook, Gemini in Classroom and Google Search,” wrote Chris Phillips, Google’s vice president and general manager of education.

For student accounts with Google Search enabled, Gemini will feature a new Guided Learning study tool. The interactive feature helps students solve homework problems step by step rather than providing direct answers. Later this month, at select US schools, Guided Learning will launch as a pilot program to collect feedback before rolling out to more schools.

According to a 2024 Google blog post, over 150 million teachers and students worldwide use Google Classroom and Assignments, the company’s assignment distribution and grading app.

A 2025 New York Times survey sent to the 20 largest US school districts found that Google’s Chromebooks, which start at $179, are the most common device in classrooms, with about 80% of teachers reporting that their school district provided Chromebooks for students.

The default search engine on Chromebooks is Google Search.

New York City Public Schools, the largest US public school district, is a Google client. When asked for comment, a NYC Public Schools spokesperson pointed to NYC Mayor Zohran Mamdani’s X post.

“Google is trying to use its monopoly power to force AI into our public schools,” Mamdani wrote in the Tuesday post. “No corporation should have the power to dictate how we teach our children. Our public schools exist to serve students, not to grow the profit margins of Big Tech.”

The second largest US district, the Los Angeles Unified School District, is also a Google client.

A Los Angeles Unified spokesperson told CNET in an email that the organization is reviewing Google’s announcement and its potential implications for schools, educators and students. The district believes emerging technologies, including AI, have the potential to enhance teaching and learning when introduced thoughtfully, responsibly and with clear safeguards, the spokesperson said.

Ying Xu, an assistant professor at the Harvard Graduate School of Education and founder and director of the Harvard Child-Centered AI Lab, told CNET in an email that Google’s Guided Learning tool could buffer some of the negative consequences of using general-purpose AI tools.

“But to me, the deeper question is: Do students still need to learn how to search for information using traditional search engines?” Xu said.

In her work, Xu found that seeking information using traditional search engines, as opposed to AI models, involves different processes, ranging from how students formulate queries to how they evaluate information to how they integrate it from multiple sources.

“I’m a bit concerned that, even with Guided Learning, students may be losing opportunities to practice these important information-seeking skills,” said Xu. “One concern I have with Google’s announcement is whether schools will still have the option to support traditional information seeking without AI features.”

Until recently, the conversation focused on whether AI should be allowed in schools at all. Now, Google’s announcement is forcing schools to decide which specific AI features to use and under what conditions, without the support needed to make these decisions.

“I do think there’s a risk of widening inequalities here, as schools with more resources and technical expertise may be better positioned to navigate these decisions, while others may have fewer options,” Xu said.

However, she doesn’t think that moving away from Google entirely is necessarily the solution, especially for districts that already rely on its systems.

Instead, Xu thinks the type of support available to schools as they navigate AI-related decisions will be crucial.

“With or without Google’s decision about search functionality, the responsibility falls on schools to protect students’ right to learn from human teachers and peers in human-led classrooms,” said Jennifer L. Steele, a professor at American University’s Baker School of Education. “Tools like Gemini Guided Learning, which uses AI to walk students through difficult questions, are not the answer.”

When schools and teachers rely on tech companies to teach students how to use their AI tools and understand their strengths and weaknesses, that’s when problems occur, she said.

“If schools take that responsibility seriously, then how Google configures its apps won’t really matter,” Steele said.

When asked for a response to the backlash surrounding the decision to turn off its search engine for schools that reject its AI, a Google spokesperson pointed to the company’s X post stating that schools can choose to turn on Gemini, Gemini Notebook or Search individually.

However, that post doesn’t address the fact that AI Mode and AI Overviews will still be enabled alongside Google Search. So if schools don’t want AI enabled for students at all, they will still lose the ability to use the search engine.

A Google spokesperson didn’t immediately respond to a request for further comment.

Tracy Pizzo Frey, a former Google managing director, wrote on LinkedIn that Google could allow schools to turn off AI Overviews, but instead is choosing to take search away from students.

Search engine DuckDuckGo, for instance, has noai.duckduckgo.com, its AI-free search engine that automatically disables all AI features and filters out AI-generated images.

“AI overviews is a search module like maps, flights or weather,” a DuckDuckGo spokesperson said in an email. “Google can determine when it shows up and when it doesn’t.”

Experts caution that educators can’t count on tech platforms to police themselves. Because AI tools are now permanent fixtures, schools must shape their own path forward.

The primary hurdle is an uneven playing field, where wealthier districts have the resources to adapt, leaving underfunded systems struggling to keep up.

#3103. Novartis: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Operating margins are on track to hit 40% by 2029 as cost discipline accelerates alongside new drug launches.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Oct. 27, 2026, Novartis (NVS -1.40%) raised its full-year 2026 core operating income guidance after delivering 4% net sales growth. This performance matters for long-term investors because the pharmaceutical giant is demonstrating that expanding sales from recently launched drugs can successfully replace revenue from older medicines losing patent protection. Three key insights from management explain how the company is maintaining its growth trajectory.

Novartis raised its full-year 2026 core operating income guidance, which tracks non-GAAP (adjusted) operating profit before one-time items, to in-line to low-single-digit growth, up from a previously projected decline. This operational shift shows that rising sales of priority medicines are offsetting patent expirations for key legacy treatments.

Management upgraded peak sales projections for key pipeline drugs, including Rhapsido for skin conditions such as chronic spontaneous urticaria (CSU), where hives appear without clear triggers. Promising trial results for remibrutinib in relapsing multiple sclerosis (RMS), a central nervous system disorder, further reinforce the company's ability to replace aging therapies.

Selling, general, and administrative (SG&A) expenses, which cover marketing and operational overhead, fell 7% during the third quarter. Lower third-party spending and efficiency gains from technology are driving progress toward a target of 40% core operating margin by 2029.

Sales momentum across newly launched drugs is outpacing legacy revenue losses, and management is openly highlighting this replacement capability. The company benefits from commercial scale across international markets and a deep pipeline, while management tracks patent litigation outcomes and pricing discipline.

Capital allocation remains focused on steady dividend growth and targeted share repurchases, supported by free cash flow generation. The long-term thesis hinges on whether late-stage clinical readouts maintain this momentum over the coming years.

TL;DR: Average mortgage interest rates jumped today.

If you're here looking for lower mortgage interest rates, today is definitely not your day.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage jumped to 7.32% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is 22 basis points higher than yesterday and 24 basis points higher than a week ago. (See our chart below for more specifics.) A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.

This comes after the Federal Reserve announced central bankers’ decision to hold the federal funds rate steady yesterday. While the Fed doesn’t directly set mortgage rates, the federal funds rate is one factor that moves them. Last month, when markets began pricing in a possible federal funds rate increase, mortgage rates spiked.

The next Fed meeting isn’t until December, so any rate movement in the next week will likely stem from other factors, like the upcoming jobs report. For more on that, read on.

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

Traders expected that the Fed would vote to hold the federal funds rate steady yesterday, so that wasn’t much of a factor for mortgage lenders as they set rates.

In his post-meeting press conference, Fed Chair Kevin Warsh said that September’s rate increase is “starting to work some” toward moving inflation down to the Fed's 2% goal, but that central bankers don't have any firm conclusions yet. He also acknowledged that the Fed’s monetary policy actions hadn’t achieved price stability yet, but that they’re “working on it.”

This hints that if inflation data continues to trend upward, central bankers might raise the federal funds rate again at their December 8-9 meeting.

The September Personal Consumption Expenditures price index (PCE), the Fed’s preferred inflation gauge, dropped this morning — a day too late for central bankers to consider it in their decision-making process.

The data shows inflation was 3.6% year-over-year in September, up from 3.4% in August.

September’s federal funds rate hike came in the middle of the month, so October’s inflation data will really capture its effect on inflation. The October PCE report will come out on November 25.

Next week, the Nerds will be watching the October jobs report, which comes out Friday, Nov.6. If it shows that employment remained steady (or even improved), that will build an even stronger case for the Fed to raise the federal funds rate again in December, because it would suggest that the labor market could withstand higher rates.

The Fed has a dual mandate to control inflation while supporting maximum employment. If they can work to lower inflation without hurting employment too badly, central bankers may feel more confident to act.

» Learn more: How the Fed affects mortgage rates

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.82% or higher — but there just aren't too many people for whom that's true.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

🤓 Nerdy Reminder: Rates can change daily, and even hourly. If you’re happy with the deal you have, it’s okay to commit.

#3101. BigBear.ai vs. Nebius: Which AI Stock Is the Better Buy for 2027?

One of these AI stocks is shrinking and losing money, while the other is growing at triple digits and just turned profitable.

As the artificial intelligence (AI) race intensifies, investors are looking beyond the biggest names for hidden opportunities. Deciding between BigBear.ai (BBAI -1.94%) and Nebius Group (NBIS +7.25%) requires weighing government contracts against cloud infrastructure.

BigBear.ai focuses on decision-intelligence software for high-stakes defense environments. Nebius builds the actual full-stack infrastructure needed to train and deploy these complex models. While both companies operate in the fast-moving AI sector, their business models, financial health, and customer bases offer investors starkly different paths.

BigBear.ai provides specialized decision-intelligence and autonomy software tailored for government and commercial sectors. It provides biometric and data analysis solutions, with a major concentration of contracts adding a layer of risk.

On a trailing-12-month (TTM) basis through the June quarter, revenue reached nearly $142 million, which represents a decline of approximately 1.5% compared to the prior year.

The company reported a net loss of close to $180 million. This performance indicates that expenses significantly exceeded total sales.

As of its June 2026 balance sheet, the current ratio is approximately 4.9x, measuring the ability to cover short-term debts with current assets. The debt-to-equity ratio was 0, as the company carries no debt.

TTM free cash flow, which is cash from operations minus capital expenditures, was negative $73 million, indicating the business consumes more cash than it generates.

Nebius Group positions itself as an AI cloud company, building the full-stack infrastructure required for complex machine learning tasks. Its platform supports AI builders across healthcare and financial services by offering tools for model training and deployment. The company operates a global footprint with data centers and roughly 400 AI engineers to maintain its competitive position.

As of the June quarter, TTM revenue reached nearly $1.4 billion, representing a significant 444% year-over-year increase. Net income improved to $62 million, up from under $10 million for the full year 2025. This performance shows a swing to profitability following significant net losses in previous years, driven by high demand for cloud services.

Based on its June 2026 balance sheet, the current ratio is approximately 4.0x, while the debt-to-equity ratio is roughly 1.0x. The company's total debt was approximately $8.6 billion, with an additional $1.5 billion in lease liabilities. This was partially offset by $8 billion of cash and equivalents.

TTM free cash flow was negative $5.9 billion, reflecting the capital-intensive nature of the company's AI infrastructure. Note that stock-based compensation accounted for roughly 21.6% of operating cash flow, inflating reported cash generation because it is a non-cash expense.

BigBear.ai faces significant risks from revenue concentration, as a large portion of its business depends on a limited number of government contracts. These agreements are subject to budgetary fluctuations and may be terminated at any time for convenience.

Nebius is funding massive capital spending needs. Heavy capital investment for data centers has resulted in negative free cash flow, and a slowdown in AI demand remains the primary risk.

BigBear.ai appears cheaper based on sales over the past twelve months, while Nebius is the only one with a positive forward P/E using future earnings estimates.

Valuation metrics include those sourced from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from those of other data providers.

I would buy Nebius over BigBear right now. BigBear has a multi-year history of flat revenue. It reported $146 million in revenue in 2021, and $128 million in 2025. It has reported a net loss across that period. The company has to compete for government contracts, while Nebius has seen its revenue explode due to a shortage of data center capacity and high demand.

Nebius is seeing immediate demand for its infrastructure as soon as it is brought online. Additionally, Nebius benefits from pricing power, with recent bids for compute capacity fetching higher prices than before.

Analysts expect BigBear to continue reporting losses over the next few years, while expecting Nebius to continue growing earnings over the long term as it brings more capacity online.

The risk for Nebius is that the stock trades at high multiples of sales and earnings. A lot of growth is priced in, so anything that stalls the AI build-out could hurt the stock. The stock has already fallen 38% in the last month over concerns about AI spending.

Still, Nebius has a scarce asset that is in high demand, and that scarcity is being validated by explosive revenue growth and improving profitability. That's the stock I would rather hold for 2027 and beyond.

#3100. Nike vs. Procter & Gamble: Which Consumer Stock Is a Better Buy for 2027?

Nike trades at a premium valuation despite slowing growth, while P&G's defensive portfolio offers a contrasting risk-return profile.

Investors often weigh the potential of iconic growth brands against the stability of essential household names. Deciding between Nike (NKE -1.64%) and Procter & Gamble (PG -0.92%) means choosing between athletic innovation and daily necessity.

Nike serves the athletic world with footwear and apparel, leveraging a massive digital presence to reach global fans. Procter & Gamble offers a defensive shield with brands like Tide and Gillette. These companies represent different strategies for long-term growth and capital preservation in a shifting economic landscape.

Nike designs and sells athletic footwear, apparel, and equipment across the globe through its own digital channels and a network of wholesale partners. The company operates primarily in the retail stocks space, focusing on high-performance gear and lifestyle products. In its latest annual report, filed for the period ending May 31, 2026, the company noted that its three largest U.S. customers accounted for approximately 29% of sales. Customer concentration like this adds a layer of risk to the business.

In FY 2026, revenue reached nearly $46.4 billion. This performance follows a period of higher sales in earlier years, as the company reported approximately $51.4 billion in revenue during FY 2024. Net income for the most recent year was $3.1 billion.

Procter & Gamble manufactures branded consumer packaged goods across diverse categories, including Fabric & Home Care, Baby Care, and Beauty. The company sells to mass merchandisers, grocery stores, and drug stores, reaching billions of consumers worldwide. Walmart (WMT -2.22%) and its affiliates represent approximately 16% of total sales, which indicates a notable reliance on a single major retail partner. This focus on essential goods helps the company maintain a steady presence throughout the globe.

In FY 2026, revenue reached nearly $87 billion, which represents a growth rate of approximately 3.3% over the prior year. Net income for the period was nearly $16 billion. This performance highlights the ability of Procter & Gamble to maintain stable earnings and profit levels even as revenue fluctuates across its various global markets.

Nike faces intense competition in the athletic footwear and apparel market from rivals like Adidas (ADDDF -1.81%), Lululemon Athletica (LULU -0.82%), Under Armour (UAA +1.53%), V.F. (VFC +3.35%), and ASICS (ASCCF -7.79%). The company also competes with Li Ning (LNNGF +0.00%) and other international brands. Dependence on a concentrated base of contract manufacturers, primarily in Vietnam, Indonesia, and China, creates supply chain and disruption risks. Global economic volatility and currency fluctuations also significantly impact consumer spending and operational margins.

Procter & Gamble operates in a highly competitive consumer products industry with global and local rivals and private-label brands. The company is vulnerable to global supply chain disruptions, including commodity and raw material cost fluctuations and the impact of climate change. Macroeconomic and geopolitical instability, including trade controls and sanctions in countries where it operates, pose ongoing threats.

Procter & Gamble offers a more conservative valuation relative to its earnings estimates, while NIKE carries a higher price tag despite slower recent revenue growth.

Nike intrigues me as it moves away from being more of a growth stock toward a dividend stock. While this isn't often talked about, it has increased its dividend payouts for 24 consecutive years. That doesn't make it a Dividend King yet, as it would need to increase those payouts for 50 consecutive years, but it's on the path to becoming one. In addition to that consistency, the yield for that dividend is hefty, sitting at 4.1%.

The catch with Nike, however, is that sales are slumping, and some of the market may still be waiting for it to return to its high-growth ways. That mismatch between what Nike was and what it's becoming is where I see the investable opportunity. In the meantime, the stock price could continue to decline until sales stabilize, cutting into those dividend returns. That's why, between the two, I would rather own Procter & Gamble. While Nike is on the track to becoming a Dividend King, Procter already is one, having increased its payouts for 70 consecutive years.

With its essential household goods, it has also fared better than Nike thus far in 2026; Procter & Gamble shares are up 14.1%, while Nike's stock price is down 38.6%. And while Procter's dividend yield of 2.6% may be lower than Nike's yield, you aren't dealing with a stock price trying to get out of a rut.

Jack Delaney has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Nike and Walmart. The Motley Fool recommends Lululemon Athletica Inc. and Under Armour. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3098. Mortgage Rates Today, Friday, October 30: Noticeably Lower

TL;DR: Mortgage rates slid enough today to get home buyers' attention.

Mortgage rates have mostly recovered today after yesterday's jump scare.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage dropped to 7.18% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is 14 basis points lower than yesterday and two basis points lower than a week ago. (See our chart below for more specifics.) A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.

Mortgage rates were elevated this month. The average 30-year mortgage rate for all of October was 7.3%, compared to September's monthly average rate of 6.98%.

While the economy never sleeps, markets are closed on the weekends. The rates you see Friday are unlikely to change much (if at all) until Monday. Happy Halloween!

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

Traders expected that the Fed would vote to hold the federal funds rate steady on Wednesday, so that wasn’t much of a factor for mortgage lenders as they set rates.

In his post-meeting press conference, Fed Chair Kevin Warsh said that September’s rate increase is “starting to work some” toward moving inflation down to the Fed's 2% goal, but that central bankers don't have any firm conclusions yet. He also acknowledged that the Fed’s monetary policy actions hadn’t achieved price stability yet, but that they’re “working on it.”

This hints that if inflation data continues to trend upward, central bankers might raise the federal funds rate again at their December 8-9 meeting.

The September Personal Consumption Expenditures price index (PCE), the Fed’s preferred inflation gauge, dropped yesterday— a day too late for central bankers to consider it in their decision-making process.

The data shows inflation was 3.6% year-over-year in September, up from 3.4% in August.

September’s federal funds rate hike came in the middle of the month, so October’s inflation data will really capture its effect on inflation. The October PCE report will come out on November 25.

Next week, the Nerds will be watching the October jobs report, which comes out Friday, Nov. 6. If it shows that employment remained steady (or even improved), that will build an even stronger case for the Fed to raise the federal funds rate again in December, because it would suggest that the labor market could withstand higher rates.

The Fed has a dual mandate to control inflation while supporting maximum employment. If they can work to lower inflation without hurting employment too badly, central bankers may feel more confident to act.

» Learn more: How the Fed affects mortgage rates

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.68% or higher — but there just aren't too many people for whom that's true.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

🤓 Nerdy Reminder: Rates can change daily, and even hourly. If you’re happy with the deal you have, it’s okay to commit.

» Stay informed: Check out NerdWallet's mortgage news hub for all our latest coverage.

The rate you see advertised is a sample rate — usually for a borrower with perfect credit, making a big down payment, and paying for mortgage points. That won't match every buyer's circumstances.

In addition to market factors outside of your control, your customized quote depends on your:

Even two people with similar credit scores might get different rates, depending on their overall financial profiles.

Maybe — but even personalized rate quotes can change until you lock. That’s because lenders adjust pricing multiple times a day in response to market changes.

#3097. The Austin Powers steamroller: How to trade CoreWeave's slow moving crisis

In Austin Powers: International Man of Mystery, a Virtuocon guard famously stands in front of a commandeered steamroller, screaming "No!!" three times before being run over. The joke, of course, is that the hazard moved so slowly he had ample time to step out of the way.

For months, equity traders holding CoreWeave (CRWV) have played that guard. Despite credit markets flashing warnings, equity holders essentially just stood there. Now, after a shocking 27.5% drop from last Friday's close to this Thursday morning's 52-week lows, followed by a whiplash 17% rebound, equity investors, if not squashed like Dr. Evil's henchman, are dazed and confused. Shares are down another 5% today.

With earnings just a few weeks away, massive leverage, a heavily split sell-side, and 15.4% short interest, the stock remains primed for explosive volatility.

The hazard approaching CRWV equity wasn't hidden. The AI cloud infrastructure platform essentially didn't exist five years ago and has accumulated $51.6 billion in debt (~$46 billion net of cash) against a backdrop of zero historical profitability. That was well known, but the CEO's comments that the company aspired to be investment grade so it could borrow "unlimited sums", even as investors already anticipated nearly another $60 billion in debt in the next 18months, spooked folks. (Trick or Treat!) Especially since the debt issued only in June had already fallen 20% since.

From an options trader's point of view, it was hard to reconcile deteriorating credit and rising leverage with falling options premia; as recently as last week, they were in the 6th percentile, 1.5 standard deviations below the one-year mean. At 72%, options weren't cheap in absolute terms, but given everything going on, they should have been higher. The stock also looked somewhat complacent, with the equity chopping around historical support levels dating back to May 2025. The credit side was on tilt:

Because CRWV is levered by more than 2:1, a 10% move in enterprise value translates to a nearly 22% swing in equity value.

Despite the dire credit picture, equity sentiment remains fiercely divided, driving this week's 17% bounce from the lows. For example, HSBC maintains a Sell rating with a $39 price target, essentially calling for the stock to be cut in half. Conversely, banks like BNP and Cantor Fitzgerald (my former employer) suggest these fears are overblown and the stock should more than double from here.

It's not just bankers defending the company either. Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang downplayed systemic concerns, and ARK Investment stepped in to buy 192,000 shares. BofA argues that CRWV's ability to bring active power online at scale, combined with higher pricing from Nvidia's upcoming Vera Rubin deployments, will drive faster-than-expected revenue growth. They described execution risk as a bigger concern than financing; I'm not sure those who bought the 2032 8 1/2s at par would agree.

With CRWV's credit heavily distressed but its equity heavily defended by high-profile dip-buyers, going outright long or short into earnings carries excessive directional risk. A November/January Call Diagonal, currently trading for just under $6 per spread. Almost 9% of the current stock price. Normally, that's a lot, but given this stock has fallen 27.5% and then gained 17% in the past 5 sessions, it isn't.

#3096. There's a 99% Chance That Democrats Retake the House, and History Says This Shift Will Have a Huge Impact on Stocks

Nearly a century of S&P 500 return data shows that a worst-case scenario is likely to unfold -- but this isn’t the complete story.

Throughout the day, Americans from across our great nation have headed to the polls or mailed in their ballots to make their voices heard. According to popular prediction market Kalshi, as of 8:50 p.m. ET, the odds have skyrocketed to better than 99% that Democrats will retake control of the House of Representatives. Regardless of what happens in the Senate, President Trump would be working with a divided Congress come January 2027.

Based on what history tells us, a split Congress can significantly alter annualized return expectations for the Dow Jones Industrial Average (^DJI -0.38%), S&P 500 (^GSPC +0.36%), and Nasdaq Composite (^IXIC +0.83%).

President Trump will likely be working with a divided Congress in the new year. Image source: Official White House Photo by Joyce N. Boghosian.

If prediction markets prove accurate and Democrats do retake one or both houses of Congress, President Donald Trump's second nonconsecutive term will mirror his first. He'll have had two years of full Republican congressional control in the first half of each term, and a divided Congress in the second half of both.

To some extent, congressional division is welcome by Wall Street. Political gridlock makes it virtually impossible to pass major tax and spending legislation. In other words, personal and corporate income tax law should remain unchanged for the foreseeable future.

DEMOCRATIC SWEEP? UBS SEES 50% ODDS. UBS assigns a 50% probability to Democrats winning both chambers, while prediction markets put Democratic control of the House at 94% and Senate at 77%. UBS warns a sweep could complicate Trump's fiscal agenda and 2027 debt-ceiling negotiations, while tariffs and AI investment remain largely insulated.

But a congressional shake-up does have its sticking points. For instance, debt-ceiling negotiations would be far more challenging. Additionally, Trump's desire to minimize the regulation of artificial intelligence would clash with Democratic lawmakers who favor much tougher oversight of the stock market's top catalyst.

However, the biggest worry for investors comes from nearly a century of historical return data. A few years ago, online retirement planning site Retirement Researcher examined the average annual return of the S&P 500 from 1926 through 2023 under four political scenarios, yielding one outlier:

Statistically, no political scenario has produced less desirable returns for stock investors than what's expected to unfold on Election Night. But this also isn't the complete story.

While nearly a century of data points to lower average annual returns for the Dow, S&P 500, and Nasdaq Composite during President Trump's final two years in the White House, investors are in better shape than this data suggests.

For starters, a 7.33% average annual return still (pardon the apropos pun) trumps the long-term average annual return of other major asset classes, such as commodities, real estate, and Treasury bonds.

These next six months are historically the best of the year, but they are even better in a midterm year. In fact, 19 for 19 times we've seen November - April higher when starting in a midterm year. Will this time be different? https://t.co/kebp03JxpR

Furthermore, Carson Group's Chief Market Strategist, Ryan Detrick, notes that the six months following midterm elections have historically been exceptionally generous to investors since 1950, with November higher 19 of 19 times. In fact, the S&P 500 hasn't been down in the six- or 12-month period following a midterm election over the last 75 years.

In other words, even though history shows that near-term returns may be tempered a bit as President Trump and a divided Congress learn to work together, the long-term outlook for the Dow Jones Industrial Average, S&P 500, and Nasdaq Composite remains as bright as ever.
