#3095. How to Make a $50 Holiday Gift Feel Like $100

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Trade time and a little extra thought to enhance gifts this holiday season.

Each month, NerdWallet suggests one wallet win to help you feel more confident about your money. This month’s topic is making holiday gifts feel more valuable, and we’re going to show you ways to elevate a gift without overspending.

Making an inexpensive gift feel fancy is a winter challenge I relish.

As a relatively frugal person who often gets sticker shock when shopping for gifts, I’m always looking for creative ways to save money without diminishing the present. Over the years, I’ve developed a few strategies to make a $50 gift feel like it’s worth at least double that.

In the spirit of the season, I’m sharing these strategies with you. I hope you — and your recipients — find them as celebratory as I do.

What does your gift recipient love to do? It helps if they have a favorite hobby, sports team or other interest.

Let’s say your recipient likes to fish. Giving a gift card to a niche fishing store makes it feel far more valuable than the standard Amazon card because it shows that you considered who they are. And there are specialty stores for every hobby these days.

Maybe gift cards aren’t your go-to. You can easily enhance other gifts, too.

Paying $5 extra to engrave or embroider their name on the gift — whether it’s stationery, jewelry or a backpack — makes the item feel far more valuable than its price tag because it’s customized to the individual.

If you choose a gift that couldn’t have been given to anyone else, it increases its value.

Time required: A few extra minutes to think about your recipients’ preferences and apply them when shopping.

A vintage or thrifted scarf can be a wrapping paper alternative that also functions as a gift. Similarly, you could wrap a gift in tea towels, artwork or online prints that can be reused by the recipient.

Another idea: Wrap a gift in printed sheet music that contains a special meaning for the recipient.

Customizing the presentation of the gift elevates it without adding much to the expense.

Time required: Since you have to wrap the gift anyway, this method doesn’t need to take extra time. You’ll just need to find the cloth or paper you plan to use.

One of my favorite gifts in recent memory involved turning a family photograph into a Jane Austen-era image set on a bucolic English estate with the empire-waisted gowns to match. My mom is a big fan of Jane Austen, so I knew she would appreciate seeing the family in a Regency-style photo.

I hired an Etsy artist to transform my original image, but you may be able to do it yourself with a photo app. Similarly, you could create a custom song, illustration or other form of art made specifically for your recipient.

Adding your own creative spin on a gift shows you spent extra time thinking about the recipient to make something extra special for them.

Time required: Applying your own creativity can be time-intensive, but the process is enjoyable so it goes quickly.

I like to call this technique the hybrid gift approach: You buy something, then get crafty to transform the gift into something one-of-a-kind.

One of my favorite ways to do this is with a photo. I’ll buy a nice but inexpensive frame and then put a photo of the recipient, or the recipient alongside loved ones, inside it.

If the person you’re buying for posts on social media, you might not even have to ask them for a photo in advance. Instead, you can find a favorite one that they already shared and surprise them.

The options are endless, especially with online tutorials to help hone your crafting skills. Adding your own customization to a purchased gift makes it feel special, but doesn’t have to cost much.

Time required: Baking, crafting or creating can take several hours, but again, it’s an enjoyable process.

Experiential gifts tend to go over well, and you may even get to have some fun, too.

One year, I gave my mom a membership to a nearby art museum and tacked on an offer to attend an exhibit with her. We had something on the calendar to look forward to together, and she got the option to visit more times throughout the year.

My parents have given me memberships, too. More recently, they gave me one to a local nature center. It’s great because I can visit on my own or attend kid-friendly events at a discounted rate. It makes me feel part of the community, and I love supporting an organization that helps kids learn more about nature.

Another option is to donate to charity in a loved one’s name and then make plans to volunteer at the organization together.

Experiential gifts help create more family memories together, which is one of my favorite ways to celebrate the holidays.

Time required: Setting aside time to visit a museum or other destination can take an entire day, but it’s time well spent.

The bottom line is that giving a highly personalized gift adds value without eating too far into your own budget. Spend $50. Make someone feel like a hundred bucks.

If you’re following along with us on this monthly financial challenge, start by testing out the tips we shared today over the next few weeks. We’ll be back again next month to learn about setting money goals for the new year. And if you missed last month’s edition, take a look back at how to shop the holiday sales.

#3094. TransUnion: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Cloud platform migrations are cutting legacy costs while accelerating new product launches across the business.

On its third-quarter earnings call, held on Oct. 27, 2026, TransUnion (TRU -0.86%) highlighted broad-based revenue growth driven by international markets and core product diversification. Broader vertical expansion and technology modernization help shelter the credit bureau from cyclical swings in U.S. mortgage lending and rising interest rates. Three things management said stand out.

Insurance has evolved into a key growth pillar, with non-credit solutions such as driver history and fraud prevention providing steady revenue regardless of underlying economic cycles.

Concerns over the Federal Housing Finance Agency (FHFA), the regulator overseeing housing lenders, switching from tri-merge credit reports that combine three bureaus to bi-merge reports that combine two are manageable because most mortgage revenue rests outside mandated requirements.

Moving core data operations to OneTru, the company's single global cloud platform, reduces legacy computing costs while enabling teams to build and deploy new analytical products faster.

International expansion and software innovation continue to power financial performance, giving management room to return cash to shareholders through buybacks and dividends. Low leverage ratios, high free cash flow conversion, and automated platform tools give the business durable pricing power across economic cycles.

Management is looking past near-term rate headwinds by cementing market share in emerging verticals and expanding digital identity offerings across global regions.

#3093. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on the growth potential of these 3 stocks

Fears around the artificial intelligence trade and higher pressure on oil prices are weighing on global markets, but a few stocks offer investors solid growth prospects even in choppy times.

Investors with a long-term horizon can look beyond the ongoing volatility and track the recommendations of top Wall Street analysts to pick attractive stocks with strong long-term growth potential.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Health insurer UnitedHealth delivered better-than-expected results for the third quarter of 2026. The company also raised its full-year earnings outlook, reflecting continued progress on its turnaround plan.

Following the Q3 print, Morgan Stanley analyst Erin Wright reiterated a buy rating on UNH stock with a price target of $529. "UNH's beat & raise reinforces turnaround momentum, with improving MA [Medicare Advantage]/Optum Health supporting a higher '26 EPS baseline & upside to '27 consensus," said Wright.

The five-star analyst added that better-than-expected performance in the UnitedHealthcare and Optum Health businesses supported another upgrade to the company's 2026 adjusted earnings per share outlook to at least $20.50. Wright noted that UNH raised the earnings guidance despite incremental investments planned for the fourth quarter of 2026.

Furthermore, Wright highlighted management's view that the consensus 2027 EPS of $22.63 was too conservative. Reaching even the low end of the company's 13% to 16% long-term EPS growth target would imply an EPS of $23.17.

Wright raised her 2026 EPS estimate to $20.65 from $19.82 and 2027 EPS to $23.24 from $22.54, reflecting the latest upside and an improving 2026 medical loss ratio to 87.4% from 88.1%. Overall, she believes that UNH's operational performance continues to improve, increasing her confidence in its UHC strategy and Optum Health turnaround execution.

Wright ranks No. 264 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 15%. See UnitedHealth Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Social media giant Meta Platforms has been making headlines since the launch of its Muse AI agent, which is seeing solid traction. Investors look forward to updates on the company's AI strategy when it announces its third-quarter results on Oct. 21.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Guggenheim analyst Michael Morris maintained a buy rating on Meta stock and increased his price target to $850 from $700, reflecting higher estimates for 2027. He expects Muse usage momentum and engagement levels to shape investor sentiment into the Q3 earnings call.

Specifically, Morris highlighted that, according to Apptopia, Muse reached 1.3 million daily active users in five weeks versus 300,000 in the first week. Given that Muse's current adoption represents less than 0.05% of Meta's 3.6 billion daily active user base, the analyst expects management to prioritize boosting usage over near-term monetization.

"Over time, we believe Muse monetizes primarily through businesses paying to reach consumers through their agents, consistent with the economics of Meta's core advertising business, rather than consumer wallets," said the five-star analyst. He added that the checkout integrations with Shopify and PayPal reflect this framework.

While there are concerns that a rival could overtake Muse or the recently launched Muse Wonder and push Meta into a prolonged cycle of overinvestment, Morris remains bullish due to the company's strong record in scaling WhatsApp and competing strongly with TikTok via Reels as well as free distribution to an extensive user base.

Morris ranks No. 629 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Meta Platforms KPIs on TipRanks.

Next is semiconductor company Marvell Technology. Earlier this month, Marvell held its Investor Day, where it raised its total addressable market estimate to $400 billion by 2030, reflecting solid AI-led demand tailwinds.

After hosting a non-deal roadshow with Marvell's senior management following the Investor Day, Stifel analyst Tore Svanberg reiterated a buy rating on the stock with a price target of $370.

The five-star analyst said that a major focus of the roadshow was the XPU and XPU Attach mix, as Marvell's Custom business expands toward its FY31 revenue target of $30 billion. In this regard, management reiterated that it expects a balanced mix of XPU and XPU Attach revenue in FY31, though it noted that either segment could grow rapidly enough to shift the mix toward 60/40 or 40/60.

While XPU drove most of the nearly $1.5 billion Custom revenue in FY26, Svanberg expects Marvell's XPU Attach business to grow substantially through the end of the decade, leading to a more balanced mix in this business. At the Investor Day event, the company raised its FY29 Custom target from more than $10 billion to over $12 billion, mainly driven by strength in the XPU Attach business.

Svanberg said that another key topic in the roadshow was whether Marvell can secure enough capacity to meet its raised targets. Management said that it met with suppliers ahead of Investor Day to share its five-year goals and received commitments to support these targets. The analyst believes that Marvell's supply chain commitments give it a competitive edge over rivals as customers seek reliable partners for large-scale infrastructure buildouts.

Svanberg ranks No. 91 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 31.8%. See Marvell Technology Statistics on TipRanks.

#3092. I Don’t Buy With a Little Help From My Friends

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

To save money on holiday gifts this year, Americans are using these 5 strategies that require coordination with friends and family.

Gift giving can bust a budget, so some Americans plan to have conversations with friends and family this year about setting limits around holiday gifting.

This isn’t as Scroogey as you might think: 55% of holiday spenders plan to use at least one money-saving strategy that likely involves coordinating with loved ones, according to a new NerdWallet survey conducted online by The Harris Poll.

Money can be a taboo subject, especially among family and friends. But a focused discussion on gift boundaries could be the difference between staying within your budget and piling up debt this holiday season.

What other Americans are doing: 28% of holiday spenders plan to set a per-gift dollar limit with family or friends.

Why this works: It’s simple and easy to remember. Unlike some other ideas on this list, you can probably coordinate this one over text.

For best results: If you’re the one bringing up the topic, you should come with a dollar amount that would be reasonable for everyone in your gift exchange group, so no one feels excluded or overextended.

Your circle of friends and family has a lot of kids.

What other Americans are doing: 23% of holiday spenders plan to buy gifts for children in a family, but not the adults.

Why this works: Cutting out adult gifts altogether can rescue a gift budget that has undergone major “kidflation” as new children join the family.

For best results: If you still want to include adults, give a gift that’s fun for the whole family, like a board game or an experience. And make sure your kid-free loved ones are on board.

You traditionally exchange gifts one-on-one with a friend, but the last few gifts you’ve given — and received — were duds.

What other Americans are doing: 18% of holiday spenders plan to skip exchanging gifts with at least one friend or family member.

Why this works: If you’re feeling gift fatigue, there’s a chance they are, too.

For best results: Frame this as an improvement to the gift-giving tradition rather than a scaling back. Propose an alternative way to celebrate your friendship, like swapping out the $40 gift that’ll probably get shelved for a $7 coffee together.

What other Americans are doing: 16% of holiday spenders plan to do a secret Santa gift exchange, or another game that requires purchasing only one gift.

Why this works: 15% of holiday spenders list buying gifts for more people than they originally planned for as among the reasons they’re most likely to overspend this holiday season. This strategy keeps your list in check, even if the number of people playing grows.

For best results: There are bound to be people in your group for whom the rules to the game are important. If you’re the one organizing, be ready to answer questions — can you trade or steal gifts, for example, and, if so, how many times? Also, this works best for family or friend groups who aren’t likely to go rogue and buy extra gifts anyway.

You have no idea what your college friend’s kids (or spouse) are into.

What other Americans are doing: 12% of holiday spenders plan to give one shared gift per family instead of individual gifts.

Why this works: In addition to saving money, this avoids the awkwardness that comes with uninformed gift giving.

For best results: Presentation is key. Don’t sheepishly give an UNO game you clearly bought on the drive over. You may be cutting back, but enthusiasm is free.

Talking about the holidays and talking about money can be stressful conversation topics. About four in five Americans avoid discussing some money topics with family and friends, according to a 2026 survey by the CFP Board. So discussing both at once may seem daunting. But without a doubt, you’re not the only one thinking about them.

Nearly 9 in 10 (86%) holiday spenders plan to use at least one money-saving strategy this holiday season. Start with an icebreaker: “Hey, have you noticed how expensive everything is lately? Would you be open to talking about how we can do gifts this year while keeping spending under control?” This conversation might be the thing that’s secretly on everyone’s wish list.

#3091. Smithfield: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Smithfield's pivot to outsourced hog production and brand ownership cuts capital risk while packaged meats delivers steady cash flow.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Oct. 27, 2026, Smithfield (SFD -0.81%) reported an adjusted operating profit of $145 million, down from $310 million a year earlier as hog oversupply crushed upstream margins. High feed and transport costs hurt live animal production, but packaged meats generated stable cash flow and expanded margins. Three things management said stand out.

Smithfield signed a supply agreement to cut its internal hog production -- its upstream live-animal farming operations -- by 900,000 head per year. Shifting live-animal production to external partners reduces capital intensity while preserving processing volume.

The consumer products division -- which transforms fresh pork into bacon, sausage, and lunch meat -- expanded operating margin by 80 basis points to 11.6%. Smithfield outperformed rivals by taking volume share in its five largest retail categories.

Smithfield finalized its acquisition of Nathan's Famous, ending a licensing agreement that was set to expire in 2032. Owning the brand outright eliminates license royalty fees and opens up direct sales in food service channels.

Pork commodity oversupply continues to depress processing spreads, but steady consumer demand for packaged meats gives Smithfield cash flow to fund long-term growth. Management is watching export volume recovery and pricing discipline across retail channels.

With net debt at 0.5 times adjusted earnings, the balance sheet provides flexibility to maintain dividend payments through cyclical downturns.

#3090. CMS Energy: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Capital-light financial compensation mechanisms let the utility grow clean energy without straining the balance sheet.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Oct. 27, 2026, CMS Energy (CMS -1.07%) reaffirmed its full-year adjusted earnings per share guidance of $3.83 to $3.90. Operational reinvestments de-risk future earnings for long-term investors. Three things management said stand out.

CMS Energy is selling non-utility renewable assets at NorthStar Clean Energy, its non-utility business segment, to exit non-regulated renewable development. Reallocating capital away from parent debt funding improves overall earnings quality and increases focus on core utility investments.

The updated integrated resource plan, a long-term utility strategy for energy supply, calls for $27 billion in clean energy investment. Power purchase agreements, contracts to buy electricity from third-party developers, allow the utility to earn fee income through a financial compensation mechanism.

Industrial demand in Michigan is fueling consistent 2% to 3% annual electric sales growth. Contracting load with semiconductor and manufacturing customers spreads fixed system costs over a broader customer base without relying on speculative data center deals.

Regulatory clarity in Michigan and expanding industrial demand keep earnings momentum on track. A pure-play regulated focus and capital-light compensation mechanisms position the utility to deliver steady growth while management watches rate case proceedings.

Consistency remains the core engine of shareholder value as the utility reinvests operational gains to safeguard system reliability.

#3088. This Janus Henderson fund manager owns CoreWeave debt — here's why he's sticking with it

CoreWeave can overcome the market's skepticism about its debt.

That isn't the consensus view — some Coreweave debt remains deeply distressed with yields above 15%. But Coreweave should power through this tough stretch, according to John Lloyd, global head of multi-sector and corporate credit at Janus Henderson.

"It's a little bit of overreacting," Lloyd tells CNBC, referring to the sell-off in Coreweave bonds, which have tanked along with its equity.

Lloyd owns Coreweave 9.75% bonds due in 2031 in the firm's Multi-Sector Income Fund. The yield to maturity on the debt is now 15.97%, a more than 70% jump since its issuance in April. Bond prices and yields move in opposite directions.

"Bond prices are lower, spreads are wider, yields are wider, and I don't see a huge fundamental change in the ecosystem right now that would warrant that, other than their higher cost of financing, which I think they can get creative with," Lloyd added.

CoreWeave has taken on a massive amount of debt to fund its data center buildout. The company has roughly $51.6 billion in total debt, including about $35 billion in notes, bonds and loans.

The market is getting nervous about how CoreWeave will be able to keep financing its data centers, and at what cost. Its stock has tumbled 20% over the past week, while the spread on its five-year credit default swaps is nearly 1,081 basis points — lower than earlier in the session but up 13% week over week. Spreads of 1,000 basis points and above are generally a sign of credit distress.

CoreWeave shares moved roughly 2% higher on Tuesday, after tumbling over the past several days and hitting a 52-week low earlier in the session. Its bond yields and spreads remain highly elevated.

Lloyd believes the selloff is a bump in the road for the neocloud company.

The market has been spooked by the jump in Treasury rates, which pushes up funding costs for companies, he explained. The focus largely turned to companies like CoreWeave that have refinance risk when rates move higher, he said.

CoreWeave leverage is high with a debt-to-equity ratio of 702%, up from 290% a year ago. Moreover, CoreWeave will spend an estimated $36.4 billion on capital expenditures this year against Ebitda of $7.5 billion, according to consensus estimates.

Revenue and Ebitda will ramp up next year, hitting $26.5 billion and $16.7 billion respectively, analysts estimate. But capex will hit $47.9 billion, indicating the company may need to finance its data center buildout with more equity issuance and/or debt.

Lloyd isn't necessarily worried about CoreWeave's ability to finance its capital expenditures, despite the fact that its capex outweighs its free-cash flow.

"Next year … they're going to be generating operating free-cash flow that'll support some of that capex need," he said.

The company can also turn to other methods of funding, like more GPU financing, which will allow it to borrow against its graphics processing units or rental contracts, he noted. In addition, CoreWeave could ask its biggest customers — including Microsoft, Meta Platforms and OpenAI — to increase their prepayments, he added.

"They can probably get creative enough where they don't even need to come to the high-yield market," Lloyd said.

The company also doesn't have any near-term maturities, which means it isn't facing a refinancing risk anytime soon, he added.

Meanwhile the supply/demand dynamic should remain tight across the AI compute landscape. Overall adoption rates within the ecosystem are running above consensus demand curves, he said. Add to that a bottleneck in supply, with chips and memory power sold out, and it's a nice set up for CoreWeave.

"All data point to the business model functioning very, very well," Lloyd said. "CoreWeave is one of the best-in-class at getting data centers up and actually running them," he added, noting that CoreWeave's financials have been improving throughout the year.

Another turning point could come if Anthropic goes forth with an IPO, expected in November, followed by OpenAI. Successful IPOs for the frontier labs could soothe market nerves and help support the neocloud operators.

"You just need more of those data points where people become more comfortable with the AI demand side," Lloyd said.

CoreWeave still needs to convince markets it can handle higher market rates and get through the financing bottlenecks.

Lloyd sees the company making it, albeit with some bumps along the way. The bonds should recover, he says, "just not in that straight line."

#3087. Honeywell Aerospace vs. GE Aerospace: Which Pure-Play Aerospace Stock Is the Better Buy Now That Both Stand Alone?

One looks like it has hit the ground running, while the other is still trying to get its footing.

GE Aerospace (GE +1.55%) is being led by the same CEO who orchestrated the breakup of General Electric into three separate businesses. Honeywell Aerospace (HONA -0.28%) is not being led by the CEO who oversaw the breakup of that iconic industrial conglomerate. That doesn't mean Honeywell Aerospace is a bad company, but it's worth noting that GE Aerospace is achieving more impressive results right now. If you are looking at these two aerospace companies, here's why you might want to focus your attention on GE Aerospace.

Wall Street goes through phases. Sometimes creating conglomerates is all the rage; other times, the focus is on breaking companies into smaller pieces. Recently, the industrial sector has been focused on simplification, leading to the breakup of both General Electric and Honeywell, two iconic names in the sector. That's how GE Aerospace and Honeywell Aerospace were born.

Both, however, are still fairly new businesses on a stand-alone basis. That creates a big problem when it comes to analyzing either business. For example, as of this writing, GE Aerospace's price-to-sales ratio is 6.3x, and its price-to-earnings ratio is 36x. Is that high or low? If you compare those valuation metrics to the five-year averages of 2.2x and 28.5x, respectively, they look high. But those averages include years when GE Aerospace was still the old General Electric.

For comparison, Honeywell Aerospace's P/S ratio is 2.7x, and its P/E is 44x. There are no longer-term averages because the stock was created by Honeywell's corporate breakup. That said, based on the P/S ratio, it appears to be a more attractive value than GE Aerospace, but the opposite is true when you look at the P/E. There's no easy answer to which is the better value.

That said, there is a clearer difference when you look at each aerospace company's third-quarter financial performance. GE Aerospace reported that revenues advanced 16% year over year in the quarter. Sales in the company's commercial business rose 21%, while sales in its defense business jumped 7%. Roughly 75% of GE Aerospace's commercial business is related to services, with services accounting for 50% of its defense business (services are 70% of overall sales), creating a reliable foundation of recurring income. Honeywell Aerospace saw sales increase 2%. Its commercial original equipment sales jumped 7%, commercial aftermarket sales rose 2%, and its defense business's sales improved by 1%.

To be fair, GE Aerospace has been a stand-alone business for longer. So Honeywell Aerospace is still working through the complexity of becoming a stand-alone business. In some ways, it is still finding its footing, while GE Aerospace is clearly running at full stride. But for an investor, that's not a subtle point.

Given the relative success GE Aerospace is seeing right now compared to Honeywell Aerospace, you can understand why the CEO of GE chose to stay on and run GE Aerospace. As an investor, GE Aerospace appears to be the better-positioned business. It is also much larger, with a market cap of around $320 billion compared to Honeywell Aerospace's roughly $50 billion.

It probably wouldn't be a mistake to buy Honeywell Aerospace. However, you would be buying it while it was still in the middle of a big transition. GE Aerospace is clearly performing better as a business, at least partly because it has had more time operating as a stand-alone company. Both have solid long-term growth prospects as people travel more globally, but for moderate and more conservative investors, larger and better-performing GE Aerospace will likely be the better choice right now.

Reuben Gregg Brewer has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends GE Aerospace and Honeywell Aerospace. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3085. Schwab's Dividend ETF Keeps Hitting Records. I'd Still Buy It Now.

Its yield has slipped under 3%. How did buyers do the last time a strong year left it there?

The Schwab U.S. Dividend Equity ETF (SCHD -0.97%) ended Friday, Oct. 30, at $35.78. That was the fund's 28th record close in 2026, and it traded near $36 as of this writing.

On price alone, the fund is up about 31% this year, while the S&P 500 has climbed about 11%. With dividends, the fund has returned about 34%, leaving it on track for its best calendar year since it started in 2011. Its strongest full year yet was 2013, at around 33%.

But for a fund of dividend-paying value stocks that most people buy for income, a higher price means a lower yield for anybody buying now. After all, the payout doesn't grow just because the share price does. And since mid-October alone, the price has climbed around 5%.

I still think the fund is worth buying. I'd just expect less than this year has brought.

The fund doesn't set its own dividend. It passes on what its 102 holdings pay quarterly, minus an expense ratio of 0.06%. Its last four payments, including the one made on Sept. 28, come to around $1.05 a share, according to Schwab.

At today's price, that's a yield of around 2.9%. It was around 3.1% in mid-October and about 3.8% at the end of 2025.

In dollars, $1,000 invested now buys around $29 a year of income at the current payout rate. The same $1,000 at the end of last year bought about $38.

But the fund has had a yield this low before.

The closest recent match is the end of 2021. The fund gained around 30% that year including dividends, finished the year just under its record close, and yielded about 2.8% at year-end -- a little less than today.

The next nine months were tough. By Sept. 30, 2022, the fund's price had fallen around 18% from where 2021 ended. And after a brief climb in early January 2022, it didn't close above that year-end mark again until July 2024. Counting dividends, the fund still lost around 3% in 2022.

The payout, though, kept growing. The fund's 2022 payments rose about 14% over 2021's. Still, a buyer who reinvested every dividend didn't get back to even for good until January 2024.

In all, that buyer is up around 58% with dividends reinvested, or about 10% a year. And with the yearly payout up about 41% since 2021, the income on that original purchase now works out to a yield of about 3.9%.

Of course, the 2021 buyer got help from fast payout growth. The fund's yearly payout climbed around 11% in 2021, then another 14% in 2022.

Now, the payout's growth is cooling. Yearly growth slowed from around 12% in 2024 to about 5% in 2025. And the last four payments are only about 2% higher than the four before them.

Put another way, a buyer today is counting on the payout picking up again, and it might not do so quickly.

The holdings' valuation doesn't look rich, at least. Schwab showed the fund's price-to-earnings ratio at around 18 at the end of September. For the Schwab U.S. Large-Cap ETF, which tracks the broader large-cap market, the number was around 25.

That same day, the dividend fund's trailing distribution yield was about 3.2%, versus around 1% for the large-cap fund. The dividend fund has gained about 10% since then while the S&P 500 has been nearly flat, so both gaps have probably shrunk.

Yes, a fund up around 31% over 10 months might lose a big chunk of that, like it did in 2022. But the 2021 buyer kept collecting a rising payout through that drop, and a buyer now should still collect one, even if it grows slower.

Is the fund still worth buying at a record? I think so. A yield just under 3% is less income than buyers got in January, but it's near what 2021's buyers paid, and the holdings seem to trade at a lower price-to-earnings ratio than the broad market. I'd buy shares at this price, maybe adding a bit at a time. I just wouldn't count on another year like 2026.

Daniel Sparks and his clients do not have positions in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

TL;DR: Mortgage rates kicked off the week with a drop, but they’re still above 7%.

Mortgage rates are cooling off as we roll into November. However, they’re still above the 7% threshold.

#3083. AI Is Making The App Optional

Haz Hubble is the Co-Founder and CEO at Pally (YC S25).

When I started building Pally, an AI assistant that keeps people on top of their messages, I assumed it needed its own app. We built one, with onboarding, settings and a home feed. Fewer than 20% of new users were still active in their second month.

We removed the app and moved the product into iMessage. Users now sign up by texting a number. With the same AI underneath, month-two retention rose to over 70%. That result made me question how much of consumer software exists only to support the app around it.

Every app asks people to do work before they get anything back. They have to find it, install it, create an account, learn where things are and remember that it exists. Some share of users gives up at each step.

The phone they’re installing it on is already crowded. The average user has more than 80 apps, and about 62% of them go unused in a given month. In the U.S., people spend around 80% of their app time in their three most-used apps. A new app is asking for a place that most people have stopped giving out.

The first is new hardware. OpenAI has said it will show its first consumer device, a screenless product designed with Jony Ive, in the second half of 2026. The last well-funded attempt, Humane’s AI Pin, failed. A common explanation is that it asked people to change their habits without giving them enough reason to.

The second is the super-app. WeChat hosts more than 4.3 million mini-programs, and roughly 764 million people used them each day in 2025. People in China order food and pay bills through services they never downloaded, because those services sit inside an app they already open all day. The model depends on one company controlling the platform, which is part of why nothing comparable exists in the U.S.

The third is putting AI inside the messaging apps people already use, such as iMessage, SMS, WhatsApp and email. Language models make this practical because a conversation can now do the job that buttons and menus used to do. The assistant can also message first, so users don’t have to remember to open anything. Of the three approaches, this is the only one that doesn’t ask people to form a new habit.

Satya Nadella made a version of this argument about business software in December 2024. He said, “The notion that business applications exist will probably collapse” as AI agents take on more work. He reasoned that most apps are databases with rules on top, and agents can apply those rules on a user’s behalf.

The same reasoning applies to consumer products. When an assistant can read your messages, check your calendar and act on a request written in plain English, many of an app’s screens become optional. Messaging is where this shows up first, because it’s where people already spend their attention.

Building inside another company’s messaging app means that company can shut you out. Meta did exactly that in January 2026, when WhatsApp stopped allowing general-purpose AI assistants such as ChatGPT and Perplexity on its business platform. After EU regulators intervened, Meta restored access in the European Economic Area in July. As of late July, the ban had not been lifted in the U.S.

Apple’s position is different. In June 2025, a federal judge refused to dismiss the Justice Department’s antitrust case against Apple, and the allegations the court let stand include restrictions on iMessage and on super-apps. Apple has reportedly made several settlement offers to the DOJ this year. While that case is open, blocking third-party assistants from iMessage would hand regulators a fresh example of the conduct they’re already suing over.

That doesn’t resolve the risk. Apple could still change its rules, and no messaging platform has made a lasting commitment to AI developers. Anyone building this way should plan for losing a channel.

1. Within five years, most consumer apps will work mainly as back-ends. Their data and logic will still matter, but people will reach them through an assistant instead of opening them.

2. A phone number will replace the App Store listing as the way many people first try a new product.

3. Screens will be generated when a task needs one, such as signing a contract or reading a chart, and will close when the task is done.

4. Consumer products will be judged by completed tasks. Time spent in an app will start to look like a cost to the user.

5. Regulators will treat AI access to messaging platforms the way they treated browser choice and app store rules, and at least one major platform will be required to open up to third-party assistants in the U.S.

If you’re building a consumer product today, make three changes.

• Track how long a new user takes to get value, counted in messages rather than screens. If the first exchange doesn’t help them, fix that before anything else.

• Build the version with no interface first. Add screens only where text doesn’t work, such as payments or reviewing long documents.

• Keep your data and logic on your own infrastructure, so you can move between iMessage, SMS, WhatsApp and email if a platform changes its rules.

For Pally, this means treating iMessage as our first channel, not our only one. We keep the product’s logic on our own infrastructure so it can run over SMS, WhatsApp or email if Apple’s rules change. We add a screen only when a user can’t finish a task in text, and every new feature has to work without asking anyone to open something new.

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#3082. Nokia vs. AT&T: Comparing Cyclical Sales Patterns vs. Steady Revenue

AT&T's revenue base dwarfs Nokia's by roughly five times, but Nokia's sharp Q4 spikes reveal a business model that operates on a different rhythm entirely.

Nokia Oyj (NOK +2.17%) primarily generates revenue by engineering and delivering advanced mobile radio access technologies, fixed fiber access solutions, and specialized cloud network infrastructure solutions to global communications service providers, major internet hyperscalers, and digital industries.

It recently completed the sale of its business in fixed wireless access customer premises equipment to Inseego Corp. for an ownership stake, and it signed a letter of intent with the Ministry of Defence of Ukraine to develop customized satellite communications systems.

AT&T (T -0.23%) primarily generates revenue by delivering domestic wireless voice communication services, mobile data solutions, high-speed fiber optic internet, and integrated cybersecurity technology offerings to an extensive base of residential consumers, small enterprises, and multinational corporate clients.

It recently announced an agreement to sell its Mexican telecommunications operations, while securing a multi-year infrastructure agreement with Corning Incorporated for ongoing supplies of connectivity solutions.

Revenue serves as a fundamental financial metric that helps individual investors gauge the basic market demand for a given company's core products and services before accounting for any operational expenses or taxes.

Nokia Oyj vs. AT&T Revenue over the last 8 quarters. Figures: Nokia Oyj shown in U.S. dollars, converted from euros.

Data source: Financial Modeling Prep. Nokia Oyj's figures are converted from euros to U.S. dollars. Data as of Nov. 1, 2026.

The revenue trajectories of AT&T and Nokia tell very different stories to investors. Both are experiencing year-over-year sales increases, but while the former shows steady top-line growth, the latter sees spikes in the fourth quarter that reflect how IT customers seek to spend their budgets before the end of each calendar year.

Nokia is trying to galvanize greater sales by shifting its focus toward high-growth artificial intelligence and cloud networking markets. In its third quarter earnings report, the company's revenue represented a 9% year-over-year jump on a constant currency basis, but its sales to AI and cloud customers grew 123%. Over time, its current revenue trajectory could look very different from the last eight quarters if its AI business continues to post triple-digit growth.

#3081. History Shows a Worst-Case Scenario Unfolding for Stocks on Election Night, as Well as a Silver Lining That Isn't Getting Enough Attention

A Republican president overseeing a split Congress has delivered outlier S&P 500 returns since 1926.

Today, Americans charted a path forward for our great country by heading to the polls or mailing in their ballots. In addition to shaking up Congress, voters may also be, inadvertently, altering the near-term outlook for the iconic Dow Jones Industrial Average (^DJI -0.38%), broad-based S&P 500 (^GSPC +0.36%), and tech-driven Nasdaq Composite (^IXIC +0.83%).

According to prediction markets, the odds of Democrats sweeping both houses of Congress have soared throughout the day. Even if Democrats only retake the House of Representatives, it would mean that President Donald Trump will be working with a divided Congress come January 2027.

President Trump will almost certainly be working with a divided Congress come January 2027. Image source: Official White House Photo by Daniel Torok.

Nearly 100 years of history says a worst-case scenario is unfolding for the stock market on Election Night. However, most investors are overlooking an election silver lining that isn't getting enough attention.

Although no predictive tool can ever guarantee a short-term directional move in the Dow, S&P 500, and Nasdaq Composite, close to a century of S&P 500 return data calculated by online retirement planning platform Retirement Researcher, spanning 1926 to 2023, points to a choppy road ahead.

Retirement Researcher examined the average annual return of the benchmark S&P 500 under four political scenarios. Only one stood out as a dubious outlier:

If prediction markets are correct, the historical worst-case scenario, a split Congress with a Republican president, will take shape over the next two years.

Two factors could drive this underperformance.

DEMOCRATIC SWEEP? UBS SEES 50% ODDS. UBS assigns a 50% probability to Democrats winning both chambers, while prediction markets put Democratic control of the House at 94% and Senate at 77%. UBS warns a sweep could complicate Trump's fiscal agenda and 2027 debt-ceiling negotiations, while tariffs and AI investment remain largely insulated.

Firstly, debt-ceiling negotiations are more challenging when Congress is divided. If Democrats retake one or both houses of Congress, the odds of a government shutdown in the first half of 2027 (i.e., when the debt ceiling is next projected to be reached) go up.

Secondly, and perhaps most importantly, Democrats favor more restrictive regulations for artificial intelligence (AI). While Trump wants as little regulation of AI as possible, Democratic lawmakers have largely favored limiting data center expansion and vastly increasing the testing and vetting of large language models. Anything that draws interest to AI regulation could be bad news for Wall Street's No. 1 catalyst.

While history doesn't always offer the rosiest of short-term outlooks for the Dow, S&P 500, and Nasdaq Composite, there are silver linings in this data that simply aren't getting enough attention.

For example, a 7.33% average annual return is still stellar compared to the average returns of Treasury bonds, commodities, real estate, and certificates of deposit (CDs). In other words, perspective changes everything.

What happens if Congress goes from red to blue? Three other times Congress flipped and the S&P 500 was up each time the following year. Small sample size yes, but doesn't appear to be the end of the world. https://t.co/pUyrQKKgvp

What's more, Carson Group's Chief Market Strategist, Ryan Detrick, recently pointed out that, since 1950, when a party loses both houses of Congress, the benchmark S&P 500 has rallied 100% of the time, by an average of 21.3% the following year. Furthermore, the S&P 500 has been higher in November in each of the last 19 midterms, dating back to 1950.

While political shake-ups can be scary and create short-term turbulence, corporate earnings and major innovative trends, such as AI, tend to be the primary forces driving equities. If investors widen their lens, they'll see that major elections are nothing to fear.

Sean Williams has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3079. AI Pods Are Redefining The Unit Of Software Delivery

Alexey Spas is the founder and CEO of Instinctools, a software engineering company focused on AI-powered digital solutions.

For most of the software industry’s history, when a software backlog started growing faster than the team could handle it, a CTO or product leader knew what came next. It was either opening new roles or sending some work to an outside partner.

Whichever the option, the rule of thumb used to be the same: More software to build meant more people needed to build it, until AI put that long-held assumption into question.

A senior engineer working with coding agents can now produce far more raw output than before. That is why software delivery is beginning to reorganize around a different unit: a small senior team surrounded by AI-enabled workflows.

The label is still loose, but the operating idea is easy to picture: An AI pod is a compact, senior-led engineering team accountable for a defined software outcome. Specialized AI agents support the delivery flow from requirements drafting to code generation and testing, while humans keep their hands on the wheel when it comes to business context, quality, security and the final result.​​

And just like that, AI has completely reshuffled the deck for software engineering. An age-old dilemma is now taking on new contours: If companies need a different kind of delivery unit, where should it live? Some organizations will have good reasons to build it in-house. Others may prefer to bring in an external pod. For many, the most practical answer may sit somewhere in between.​

A big part of the appeal of an in-house AI pod is its lean structure, and it’s not hard to see why: Hiring one or two senior engineers supported by AI agents is a very different proposition from building a cross-functional team of five-plus people.

The strongest case for keeping the pod internal, though, may have less to do with headcount than with proximity of in-house engineers to the product, business context and the decisions. Moreover, the lessons learned while building AI-enabled delivery workflows remain inside the organization.

That advantage takes time to build. Giving experienced developers access to coding agents does not immediately produce an effective AI delivery system. Teams still have to determine what can safely be delegated, where human review belongs, which quality gates can be automated and how AI-generated work fits into the rest of the software lifecycle.

There is also the expertise question. A small pod can still draw on a broad range of skills. A modernization effort can suddenly require security input, an AI-heavy feature may call for data engineering or a customer-facing workflow may need experienced UX support. Building all of those competencies internally makes sense when the demand is sustained. It is harder to justify when the need appears for only a few weeks.​

There is no secret AI stack tool available only to software vendors. Anyone can access the same models, coding assistants and agentic frameworks. The advantage of an external pod usually comes from reducing the distance between access to AI and a delivery system that works.

If spinning up a few agents could solve the software delivery problem overnight, things would be simple. In practice, faster generation creates its own downstream load: More code has to be reviewed, tested, integrated and checked for security. The challenge is turning that AI-driven speed into production-ready software.

An outsourced team that has already cut its teeth on different projects can bring patterns refined across more than one production environment: agent configurations, evaluation methods, review workflows, reusable components and quality controls. I know—there’s a lot of skepticism when software development vendors talk about their “unique expertise.” But in this case, uniqueness does not mean exclusive knowledge that nobody else can acquire. It just implies a shorter route through mistakes someone else has already made and experience that has been tested often enough to become adaptable.

There is also a flip side to that breadth of experience. An external pod does not begin with the same institutional knowledge as an internal team, nor is it naturally as close to every product decision. The model makes the most sense when the value of faster ramp-up, cross-project experience or temporary capacity outweighs the benefits of having a team deeply embedded in the product.​

When a company needs AI-enabled delivery capability now but is not yet certain how much of it should become a fixed part of the organization, a hybrid model can be considered. Demand may surge around a product launch, modernization program or backlog push, and then ease again. An external pod can take the edge off this sudden spike without turning peak capacity into permanent headcount.

The same arrangement can also serve a longer-term purpose. A company that ultimately wants the capability in-house can use an outsourced pod as a working prototype for the team it may later build itself.

A “borrow, prove, then build” approach gives leaders a chance to observe which roles they repeatedly need, which workflows survive production, where human judgment remains essential and which metrics genuinely improve. The parts that prove strategic can then move inside the organization with much less guesswork.​

AI pods are making the old build-versus-buy decision more nuanced. For leaders, the choice comes down to a few practical questions: How enduring is the demand? How important is institutional knowledge? How quickly should the capability become operational?

The answers may change as the organization learns. That is probably the main point. AI is reshaping software delivery quickly enough that the operating model that fits today does not have to be the one a company commits to for good.​​

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#3078. BlackRock's Rick Rieder sees 'historic' opportunity in high-quality bonds

Investors may be wary of the volatility hitting parts of the bond market right now, but they have an "historic" opportunity to grab attractive yields in high-quality credit, Rick Rieder, BlackRock's chief investment officer for global fixed income, told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

Hypothetically, it's now possible for investors to build a diversified portfolio that yields more than 7% with less than three years of duration and an average credit quality of A-, he said. Duration is a measurement of a bond's price sensitivity to fluctuations in rates, and issues with shorter maturities tend to have lower duration.

"Not only is it historic in that these yields are where they are, but the only time these yields are ever here is when there's some problem, like an economic problem or default problem," Rieder said in an interview about his fourth-quarter outlook, released Wednesday.

That isn't the case now, where the economy remains strong, he said. "So you don't really have to worry about defaults of any significance."

While Rieder didn't specifically identify the credits in the aforementioned portfolio, the iShares Aaa - A Rated Corporate Bond ETF (QLTA) has a 30-day SEC yield of 5.68% and an effective duration of 6.48 years — higher than Rieder's low duration focus.

The iShares Flexible Income Active ETF (BINC), which is managed by Rieder, has a 5.66% 30-day SEC yield and three-year effective duration, but its portfolio extends beyond investment-grade credit.

Rieder sees the best opportunity right now in investment-grade corporate bonds — which he pointed out he hasn't said "in a really long time." Investment grade bonds are rated AAA through BBB- by S&P and Fitch, while Moody's rates them Aaa through Baa3.

That includes hyperscaler bonds, which have flooded the market with issuance this year as they ramp up their AI infrastructure. Alphabet, Amazon, Meta Platforms and Oracle have issued a total of about $228.4 billion in bonds year to date, as of Oct. 20, according to LSEG.

"They're very low leverage. They have tremendous amounts of cash flow coming in," Rieder said of the hyperscalers in general. "You're now getting to buy these entities which are higher quality, paying you a very attractive yield to get it."

Rieder's iShares Flexible Income Active ETF has upped its allocation to investment-grade corporates to 14.3% of its portfolio. In July, that stood at 8.5%.

That said, investors need to be patient and sit tight since rates could still move higher from here, he advised.

"Your starting point is by far your best barometer of what your return is going to be," he noted. "So if you get seven and a quarter, maybe you get eight, maybe you get six, but ... if your holding period is a year or longer, you're going to get that yield."

Caution should also be exercised outside of investment-grade credit, which requires more selectivity and deep research, he warned. That includes AI names.

"There's parts of the data center supply that's not hyperscaler guaranteed or hyperscaler backed," he said. "That we've been more cautious around, and we'll continue to be."

The bond market, particularly high-yield credit, has been rocked as investor concern grows over the massive amount of debt some companies are taking out to fund their artificial intelligence build-out.

Junk-rated CoreWeave has become the poster child, seeing its stock drop, bond yields jump and credit-default spreads widen. Oracle, which is sitting in the lower end of investment grade and is seen as a bellwether on the broader AI market, has also taken some hits.

Rieder is also keeping an eye on the Federal Reserve, which is expected to raise its federal funds rate again in December to combat inflation. The market is currently pricing in a nearly 90% probability that the central bank will hike at that time, according to the CME Group FedWatch tool.

The money manager expects that to happen, although he does not agree with it. Higher rates are hurting parts of the economy, like housing, while not having much of an impact on other areas — such as tech companies' capital expenditures, which have become a big driver of growth, he said.

"They're not borrowing off the overnight funds rate," Rieder noted. "There's a lot of floating rate finance in housing, credit card, auto loan finance, and you're seeing those areas already under pressure."

Instead, the market is and will continue to price the AI supply coming into the market, he added.

Rieder sees a number of possible risks ahead, including rising levels of government debt across the globe. In the United States, the budget deficit has grown to $2 trillion for fiscal 2026.

"The more the central banks raise the rates, the cost of debt to these to already indebted governments is something we've got to be careful about over time," he said.

Bond supply coming from AI companies should also be watched.

"With the amount of supply that's coming — data center, hyperscaler — there's some crowding-out effect that's taking place," he said. "With government rates moving higher, that is something that we just got to keep an eye on."

In the meantime, Rieder would just continue to reap the rewards of high investment-grade yields "and smile a lot," he said.

#3077. Sequans (SQNS) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

Sequans is pivoting to 5G and defense after exiting its Bitcoin strategy.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Sequans Communications S.A. (SQNS +10.89%) reported a strategic pivot back to its core semiconductor operations following the full exit from its Bitcoin treasury strategy and the redemption of its convertible debt. Management reported that the company is now focused on scaling its 4G IoT product business and expanding into the defense and drone sectors via its RF transceiver platform. The company stated that growth is being driven by the conversion of its design-win pipeline into mass production revenue, particularly within the smart metering and telematics verticals. Efforts are also underway to transition the product roadmap from 4G to 5G technology, with development kit shipments and lab testing advancing for next-generation applications. Sequans is managing supply chain constraints through diversified sourcing while maintaining a simplified capital structure to support its path toward breaking even.

Before discussing our core business, I will start with a brief update on our balance sheet, as we have now fully concluded our Bitcoin treasury strategy. As announced on September 24, we sold the remaining 314 Bitcoin held on our balance sheet as of June 30, completing the Bitcoin treasury exit that began with the full redemption of our convertible debt in May. These final sales were executed at an average price of approximately $78,500 per Bitcoin, for total proceeds of $24.6 million. As a result, Sequans no longer holds any digital assets, and we have no outstanding debt other than a small amount tied to government-financed R&D projects.

We ended the third quarter with [inaudible] cash equivalents, and short-term deposits, compared to $21 million in cash and 314 Bitcoin at the end of the second quarter. With this exit complete, the Bitcoin treasury chapter is closed, and the full strategy and financial attention of the company is now on our semiconductor business. Our priority going forward is squarely on executing our semiconductor strategy, scaling our IoT product business, developing our new defense and drone business, and advancing our 5G RedCap roadmap to create long-term shareholder value. We enter the fourth quarter with a strong balance sheet and the financial flexibility to support our operations and invest in growth. Now, turning to our semiconductor business.

Our 4G IoT products showed strong momentum in the third quarter. Third-quarter revenue came in at approximately $9.1 million, in line with our guidance range. That is up 22% from $7.5 million in the second quarter and up 183% from $3.2 million in the third quarter of 2025. The vast majority of our Q3 revenue was generated by product sales, which increased by more than 4x year-over-year. Through the first nine months of 2026, revenue totaled $22.6 million. That already exceeds our full-year 2025 revenue of $18.3 million, excluding the $8 million of non-recurring Qualcomm-related revenue we recorded in the first half of 2025.

We are seeing strong order momentum across our customer base, driven by design wins that have reached mass production, several of which we expect to keep scaling through 2027. This is reflected in its strong and growing backlog extending into the second half of 2027, which give us solid visibility and confidence in our product growth trajectory. As in the previous two quarters, our Q3 book-to-bill ratio was well above one. Our product design win pipeline, measured by potential three-year product revenue, keeps expanding. We update this figure once a year, so for now, we are maintaining the $300 million reported at the end of 2025.

However, in the third quarter alone, we added nine design win projects and eight advanced engagements. As reported at the end of the second quarter, $165 million of this pipeline is in mass production and generating revenue. Two additional projects moved to mass production in Q3, and we expect several more to follow in Q4, bringing the mass production portion of the pipeline to more than $180 million by year-end. Smart metering, telematics, and asset tracking remain our strongest verticals, and the volume and visibility we are building in these markets reflect the strength of our product positioning and customer relationships.

Looking at our product families, our Monarch CAT-M products remain a core growth driver, accounting for more than 80% of Q3 product revenue, with asset tracking and smart metering applications leading the way. We expect this business to keep growing in 2027, as we continue to win new customers and projects every quarter. Revenue from our Calliope Cat 1bis product is driven by our design wins in telematics and security, where several projects have now reached mass production. We expect this momentum to carry into Q4 and beyond, with significant revenue growth materializing in 2027. Overall, our cellular IoT pipeline for both CAT-M and Cat 1bis keeps growing, with many new opportunities every quarter.

Beyond the design win pipeline, we are currently working on more than a dozen new projects that we hope to win in the near future. In addition, we have begun substantive discussions on several 5G eRedCap projects, with customers preparing for the network transition from 4G to 5G, a market we are targeting with a highly competitive chip now in development. As we previously said, our 5G eRedCap test chip is in-house. We expect to complete end-to-end lab testing late this year, with a plan to engage customers in the second half of 2027 and generate revenue in 2028. As the IoT market transitions from 4G to 5G, eRedCap remains a key pillar of our long-term strategy.

It positions Sequans to capture a significant product design win opportunity and expand our market share in cellular IoT while enhancing our IP leadership and supporting future licensing agreements. Beyond IoT, our Iris RF transceiver is attracting strong interest across defense, drone, and space applications. Since we began shipping our development kit platform in Q2, we have generated more than 20 leads in these markets, including many tier-one customers. The first drone customer we announced last quarter is now ready for production, and we expect shipments to begin in Q4. While most of the RF pipeline is still in the engagement phase, it already represents more than $30 million in three years revenue potential, with very high margins.

We are fully committed to scaling this defense and drone business by building out our go-to-market strategy and strategic partnerships, several of which are currently under discussion. Turning to services and licensing, this business remains an important source of high-margin revenue, although the timing of individual engagements can vary from quarter to quarter. Our goal is to leverage our RF and 5G IP portfolio to address new markets and regions beyond our own addressable markets. We have already concluded several licensing deals in the past, some of which are now generating royalty revenue that we expect to grow in 2027.

We also have several active licensing and strategic engagements under discussion with global customers and partners across a range of end markets and geographies. Individually, these opportunities range from several hundred thousand dollars to well over $10 million, and their total revenue potential exceeds $75 million. We are making progress on most of them and aim to close some in Q4. These opportunities provide potential upside to our revenue, would advance our path to break-even, and expand our strategic reach into new markets and applications. Now, a few words on supply chain and product costs. We continue to navigate a challenging supply and cost environment.

While memory remains the primary constraint, we are increasingly seeing pressure across the broader semiconductor supply chain. To mitigate these challenges, we have implemented multiple sourcing options for key components and manufacturing materials, including substrates, flash memory, and assembly and packaging, which puts us in a stronger position heading into 2027. However, we still need to address silicon supply, as TSMC will be operating under capacity allocation for all mature nodes in 2027. With significant growth expected next year, our main challenge is securing enough wafers to meet all customer demand.

We have already secured the majority of our wafer needs, and our goal is to work closely with TSMC to secure the remaining volume required to support our full 2027 growth potential. At the same time, we are mitigating cost pressures by passing through increases to customers where appropriate, limiting the impact on gross margin. This is particularly important for memory components, where pricing remains volatile and changes month by month. We managed all of these challenges well in 2026, and we have confidence in our ability to manage these challenges again in the coming year. Third quarter cash burn came in line with our plan.

We remain focused on disciplined cost management and on reducing cash burn over time as revenue grows, with the objective of reaching break-even as quickly as possible. Overall, our third quarter reflected real progress in strengthening our core IoT business, completing the simplification of our capital structure, and positioning the company for sustained growth. For the fourth quarter, we expect revenue in the range of $9.5 million-$11 million. This guidance assumes a limited contribution from licensing and services. Should we close a major licensing deal during the quarter, revenue could exceed this range. We are executing from a position of strength.

We have a simplified balance sheet with approximately $40 million in cash and short-term deposits, no debt other than government-financed R&D funding, and significant financial flexibility. We have an IoT business with strong underlying momentum, supported by a growing backlog and a maturing design win pipeline that is increasingly converting into production revenue. We have an exciting new market opportunity in defense, drone, and space, which adds scale and diversification to our business with significant potential. We have a differentiated strategic IP portfolio in 5G and RF, which we believe remains one of the most important long-term value drivers for Sequans.

We remain focused on scaling our product revenue across both our IoT and defense and drone markets, advancing our 5G RedCap roadmap, and executing against the key drivers we believe will unlock the full value of Sequans over time. This is coming more in 2027 than Q4. So in Q4, I remain a little bit cautious on my gross margin, similar to what we have seen in current quarter.

So that's why there is an urgency to start taking a 5G product as soon as possible to market. Obviously, the carrier are not ready yet. They are deploying their network or preparing to deploy their networks. Ericsson and all the infra vendor, they didn't yet finish their release to those carriers. This will happen really an activity in 2027, and from there on, we'll start seeing more and more design. All new design will be switching to 5G.

It doesn't mean we stop shipping 4G. We keep shipping this for maybe 10 years if you want some of them. But at least all new design will be, you need to win them with the RedCap. The opportunity for Sequans there is like, first of all, there is higher ASP on the 5G.

We'll keep investing only into the 5G. Our RF is also mature and ready for the drone and the defense. So there is no big surprise in terms of investment with the current plan we have.

On the gross margin, not to be confused, obviously, we have a chip and modules in the IoT, and obviously we have the RF chip that is adding up. Prediction markets and the IMG Arena integration are the two pieces that could matter most to a patient investor evaluating Sportradar's growth prospects.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 3, 2026, Sportradar (SRAD +10.33%) highlighted 24% year-over-year revenue growth driven by expansion into prediction markets and content integration. Long-term investors care because expanding into adjacent financial ecosystems scales revenue without adding heavy infrastructure costs. Three things management said stand out.

Sports prediction markets present a fast-growing avenue alongside traditional sports betting. By supplying official data feeds to exchanges, brokers, and market makers, the company taps new revenue streams while using its existing infrastructure.

Integrating major sports rights portfolios often carries execution risks, but one year after acquiring IMG Arena, management has combined operations and monetized premium golf and martial arts content across its customer base.

Management agreed to sell Atrium Sports, a coaching and scouting business, for $170 million to streamline operations around core sports data. The proceeds strengthen the balance sheet and fund share repurchases.

Demand for real-time sports data continues to climb across media platforms, streaming services, and emerging trading hubs. Scaled data rights and proprietary processing technology create operating leverage as trading volumes build.

Growth remains anchored in long-term rights lockups, international expansion, and disciplined capital allocation. Expanding high-margin technology services into emerging formats sets up sustained financial momentum.

This page includes information about these cards, currently unavailable on NerdWallet. The information has been collected by NerdWallet and has not been provided or reviewed by the card issuer.

Both the Citi® / AAdvantage® Platinum Select® World Elite Mastercard®  and Citi Strata Premier® Card can help you rack up AAdvantage miles. The former earns them directly; the latter transfers points to American Airlines at a 1:1 ratio. So which one is better for American Airlines flyers?

For earning rewards, the Citi Strata Premier® Card is the winner. Although it's not an American Airlines credit card, it can make it easier to accumulate AAdvantage miles on many purchases with its higher earning rates and the option to transfer ThankYou points to American.

Still, the Citi® / AAdvantage® Platinum Select® World Elite Mastercard® can help you achieve AAdvantage elite status and offers perks when flying American Airlines, including a free checked bag and preferred boarding. In 2027, the annual fee will increase, but the card will add new bonus categories and other benefits.

The Citi Strata Premier® Card is a better fit for travelers who want to earn American Airlines miles without committing all their rewards to a single airline program.

Although the Citi® / AAdvantage® Platinum Select® World Elite Mastercard® is an American Airlines credit card, the Citi Strata Premier® Card can be a faster way to accumulate AAdvantage miles on many purchases.

That's because Citi Strata Premier® Card holders can transfer ThankYou points to American Airlines AAdvantage — and several other loyalty programs — at a 1:1 ratio.

Currently, the Citi Strata Premier® Card offers more rewards than the Citi® / AAdvantage® Platinum Select® World Elite Mastercard® in every bonus category. On Jan. 24, 2027, when the benefits on the Citi® / AAdvantage® Platinum Select® World Elite Mastercard® change, that will change — but only in a couple of narrowly defined categories: select streaming services and eligible Rides and Rails purchases, which include taxis, rideshares, subways and trains. Unless those categories make up a significant portion of your spending, the Citi Strata Premier® Card will likely yield more points overall, even if your ultimate goal is American Airlines miles.

» Learn more: 11 Benefits of the Citi Strata Premier® Card

The Citi Strata Premier® Card also offers a $100 annual hotel benefit when you book a single hotel stay of $500 or more, excluding taxes and fees, through Citi Travel. Using the full benefit can more than offset the card’s annual fee, and hotel bookings through Citi Travel earn 10 ThankYou points per dollar.

Just keep in mind that booking through Citi Travel may mean giving up hotel loyalty program benefits you would receive by booking directly. Whether the $100 hotel perk offers enough value to justify booking through Citi Travel will depend on the property, the rates available and how much you value those hotel loyalty benefits.

» Learn more: Is the Citi Strata Premier® Card worth its annual fee?

One of the biggest advantages of the Citi Strata Premier® Card is the ability to earn ThankYou points, which can be transferred to several airline and hotel loyalty programs. If you want the option to transfer points to American Airlines but also see value in the card’s other airline and hotel partners, the Citi Strata Premier® Card is a better choice.

Free checked bag: Get a free first checked bag on domestic American Airlines itineraries for the primary cardholder and up to four companions traveling on the same reservation. If you check bags with American, this benefit alone could make the card worth getting.

Preferred boarding: The primary cardholder and up to four companions on the same reservation receive preferred boarding.

American Airlines Vacations discount: Save 10% on eligible American Airlines Vacations packages.

Inflight discount: 25% off inflight food and beverage purchases. This perk is being replaced on Jan. 24, 2027, with an inflight statement credit.

Starting Jan. 24, 2027, the card will offer:

Inflight statement credits: Receive up to $100 back in statement credits annually on inflight purchases. This replaces the old 25% off inflight food and drink purchases.

$150 flight discount: Spend at least $20,000 during your card membership year and renew the card to receive a $150 American Airlines flight discount. This replaces the $125 flight discount; the spending requirement is the same.

The inflight statement credits, in particular, will be easy to maximize and could potentially offset a meaningful portion of the card’s annual fee. Combined with the ability to earn Loyalty Points toward elite status and the card’s other perks, that can make the card more valuable for American’s flyers.

» Learn more: Is an American Airlines credit card worth it?

The Citi® / AAdvantage® Platinum Select® World Elite Mastercard® offers Turo credits. You receive up to $180 annually in statement credits on eligible Turo rentals, with up to $30 back per eligible rental.

On Jan. 24, 2027, the card will add several more perks:

Walmart+: Primary cardholders receive a complimentary annual Walmart+ membership, valued at $98.

Global Entry or TSA PreCheck: Receive up to a $120 application fee credit every four years.

FOX One: Get 50% off an annual FOX One subscription.

Travel protections: Get trip delay protection, trip cancellation and interruption protection, worldwide car rental insurance and lost baggage protection.

It’s unusual to see a Global Entry or TSA PreCheck credit on a card with an annual fee under $200. When that perk kicks in, the card could become a good option for travelers who prioritize AAdvantage miles but also want a credit card that fully reimburses the application fee for Global Entry or TSA PreCheck.

» Learn more: American Airlines credit card benefits guide

Cardholders receive 1 Loyalty Point for every eligible AAdvantage mile earned from purchases. That means everyday spending can move you closer to AAdvantage elite status, in addition to earning miles you can redeem for flights. American’s entry-level elite status requires 40,000 Loyalty Points, so putting significant spending on the card can help you reach that threshold.

If you want to earn rewards on everyday spending and have the flexibility to move those points to American Airlines — and you don’t need the airline-specific benefits — the Citi Strata Premier® Card is the best choice.

That said, the Citi® / AAdvantage® Platinum Select® World Elite Mastercard® makes more sense for frequent American Airlines flyers who can take advantage of benefits such as a free checked bag and preferred boarding, or for travelers who want to earn Loyalty Points toward American Airlines elite status through credit card spending.
