#3069. The ERP Migration Blind Spots That Technology Alone Can't Fix

Bruno Billy, President & CEO of APGAR North America, advises organizations on the operational reality of data and enterprise transformation.

Enterprise resource planning (ERP) transformations remain high on the agenda for many large organizations. Mergers and acquisitions, consolidation and modernization are driving significant investment. For SAP customers, the approaching end of mainstream ERP Central Component (ECC) support adds further urgency.

But replacing an ERP is much more than replacing technology. It affects nearly everything about how a company does business, from processing customer orders to managing its supply chain and reporting financial results. The scope makes it notoriously difficult and expensive to execute.

The expense creates enormous pressure to get moving. Teams responsible for different workstreams often start before the organization agrees on how the new ERP should represent the business. Often, those differences start with the most basic assumptions about how the business operates.

Here’s a simple question that comes up surprisingly often during ERP transformations: what is a customer?

The obvious answer is the person or organization purchasing your products or services. Simple enough, right? But consider a manufacturer that sells through distributors. For finance, the customer might be the distributor that places the order and pays the invoice. For service, it might be the business actually using the product. Neither is wrong. Both definitions serve legitimate business needs.

An ERP needs to accommodate these nuances through its business objects, relationships and hierarchies. If implementation teams decide how to represent customers independently, they risk embedding conflicting assumptions in data mappings, system configurations and business processes.

Those conflicts may not become apparent until integration testing or financial reconciliation. By then, resolving them can require substantial rework, increase project costs, delay go-live and potentially disrupt business operations.

You won’t resolve every business requirement before an ERP migration begins, but there are three decisions I’d recommend making before the teams get too far into configuration and data migration.

Start by agreeing on how the business will be represented in the new ERP. For each major data domain, define the business entities, their relationships and the hierarchies needed to support operations and reporting.

This is particularly important when consolidating systems following an acquisition. In one organization I worked with, acquisitions had left the business operating six different ERP environments, each with its own representations of customers, suppliers and products. Supplier records in separate systems, for instance, may refer to subsidiaries of the same parent company. The future-state model should preserve those relationships so procurement can understand total spending across the supplier organization and negotiate accordingly.

Before approving the model, test it against actual legacy records. Can the migration team determine what each record becomes, which hierarchy it belongs to and how it relates to other entities? If the answers differ depending on who you ask, more business-design work is needed.

Not every difference needs to disappear. Some reflect legitimate regional or business-unit requirements. Not every local variation is inefficiency. Some may actually reflect a competitive advantage.

A manufacturer may sell the same product in several countries while using different packaging or meeting different regulatory requirements in each market. I find it useful to distinguish between what should be written in ink and what can be written in pencil.

• Write in ink the definitions, identities and relationships that need to remain consistent across the enterprise.

• Write in pencil the attributes and classifications that must accommodate local requirements.

Make these decisions with future rollouts in mind. The first implementation often establishes conventions that subsequent regions are expected to follow. A model designed exclusively around the first region’s needs can become an expensive constraint on everyone else.

Decide which systems are responsible for maintaining master data and, equally important, who has the authority to make decisions about it.

A customer record might originate in the CRM, while finance maintains credit information and payment terms. Authority may therefore need to be defined at the attribute or business-process level rather than assigned fully to one system. For each major data domain, identify who can create records, approve changes, resolve conflicts and authorize exceptions.

Consider a customer changing its legal name. Who determines whether that requires updating an existing record or creating a new legal entity? If CRM and finance reach different conclusions, the ERP can end up with conflicting information even when both systems are functioning correctly.

Establish these governance processes before go-live and sustain them afterward. Customers merge, suppliers reorganize and businesses expand. Without ownership and change management, the fragmentation you worked to eliminate will quickly return.

It is understandable to worry that business-model discussions will slow an already demanding program, but these decisions must be made eventually. The longer they are deferred, the more configuration, mappings and processes are built on assumptions that may need to be revisited.

Making the decisions early reduces rework and gives implementation teams a common foundation. It also creates value beyond the migration itself. The same business definitions and hierarchies can support reporting, analytics, future acquisitions and even AI applications.

An ERP doesn’t merely store your business’s data. It encodes a model of your business. If you haven’t agreed on that model before implementation starts, the implementation will make those decisions for you. ​

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#3068. Why Shake Shack Stock Jumped 20% in October

A broader upswing in restaurant stocks lifted Shake Shack in October.

Shake Shack (SHAK -3.20%) stock was moving higher last month in a broader rotation into restaurant stocks as investors sold AI stocks and rotated into undervalued sectors like restaurants.

There was no major news item that drove the stock up last month, but Shake Shack did benefit from its announcement that it would expand into Brazil, and it received several positive analyst notes in the month. Additionally, Nelson Peltz's buyout proposal for Wendy's lifted other restaurant stocks, as it suggested the sector was undervalued and that additional private offers could emerge.

According to data from S&P Global Market Intelligence, Shake Shack finished the month up 20%. As you can see from the chart below, the stock gained gradually for most of the first three weeks of the month, then jumped at the end of the month on news about Wendy's.

Shake Shack started the month with momentum and continued to climb after announcing plans to enter Brazil through a licensing partnership with Bold Hospitality Company. The company noted its successful entry into Mexico seven years ago, and it's also about to enter Panama as it expands further in Latin America.

Later in the month, Morgan Stanley raised its price target on Shake Shack from $74 to $79 with an equal weight rating, finding that overall demand was resilient in the fast food industry.

Finally, UBS lifted its price target on Shake Shack from $68 to $73 the following week.

Towards the end of the month, third-quarter reports from peers like Chipotle and Starbucks began to come in, pointing to a strong quarter for the industry.

The news about Wendy's buyout offer also gave Shake Shack a boost, and Shake Shack, with a market cap of only $3 billion, could potentially be a buyout target as well.

Shake Shack will report third-quarter earnings after hours on Thursday, and analysts are expecting revenue to grow 16% to $426.3 million, but for adjusted earnings per share to fall from $0.36 to $0.34.

Shake Shack continues to expand across the country and is coming off a second quarter where it delivered 3.5% same-store sales growth.

Investors will want to keep an eye on same-store sales growth and margins when it reports earnings, as the company has struggled to convert revenue growth into profit growth. Still, if the performance of peers like Chipotle is reflective of the broader industry, Shake Shack could top expectations on Thursday.

Three takeaways from the call stand out for long-term investors.

Integrating fire and gas detection manufacturer Autronica broadens the company's platform into large-scale global projects. While the transaction creates short-term margin dilution, an expanded market unlocks long-term cross-selling opportunities.

Software-linked safety devices deepen customer retention and generate steady service revenues through subscription packages like MSA+, the company's subscription-based connected safety platform. Adoption of connected portables expands profitability while locking in multiyear hardware replacement cycles.

Strategic price increases and factory productivity initiatives protected operating margins against inflation. Pricing discipline across essential safety gear enables consistent cash generation during mixed industrial cycles.

Demand for advanced safety equipment remains steady across fire service and industrial markets, reinforced by product innovation and regulatory safety requirements. The integration of recent acquisitions and disciplined pricing protect profitability as adoption of connected safety expands.

Execution of long-term strategic initiatives positions the business to capture higher-margin market share across global safety verticals.

#3047. Biotech stocks are surging. How investors can play the new drug innovation boom

Moderna gave investors a scare as its stock plunged on Thursday. But it's still one of the hottest biotechs this year. And it's hardly alone – the sector is on a roll and looks poised for more gains.

The iShares Biotechnology ETF (IBB) is up 24% through October 23, building on a 28% gain in 2025. Two dozen of the ETF's 233 holdings have more than doubled year-to-date. Moderna leads the pack with a 587% surge — even after an 18% decline following a leak of clinical data for its cancer vaccine (which was actually positive).

Analysts say a new biotech cycle is underway, fueled by clinical trial results, M&A activity, and promising technologies such as genomics, mRNA — and AI.

"We're in the midst of a powerful innovation cycle," said John Hall, portfolio manager of the T. Rowe Price Biotechnology ETF. "What's super exciting is we're seeing newer modalities start to make it to market." Companies "understand the disease biology better," he adds, and that could ultimately lead to more novel drugs making it to market.

Investors are excited about technologies like mRNA and genomics, along with a steady stream of new drug approvals. The FDA approved about a dozen novel biologic drugs in 2025, out of 46 approvals in total. Roughly 66 novel drugs are in the pipeline for FDA approval, including potential blockbusters like Moderna and Merck's cancer drug for melanoma, and a next-generation obesity drug from Eli Lilly.

Analysts say AI could also lead to more approvals as companies deploy the software to speed up drug development.

"Biotech is a phenomenal application for artificial intelligence and machine learning," says Piper Sandler analyst Ted Tenthoff. "You can set up exciting predictive models that, frankly, can do things that we've never been able to do in the space in terms of novel drug discovery."

Granted, the industry is recovering from a fallow period. The sector's last strong performance was during the Covid era when ultra-low interest rates encouraged investment in early-stage drug developers, and mRNA vaccines fueled a revenue surge for companies like Moderna, Pfizer and Biontech.

The sector then went cold as many early-stage companies fizzled. Demand for Covid vaccines evaporated, and surging interest rates put a chill in IPOs and M&A.

The risks haven't evaporated – this is still a sector whose products take years to develop, resulting in far more failures than successes. But analysts say the outlook is healthier now. While that's reflected in strong performance and richer valuations, there's still good reason to invest.

Recent clinical trial results are stoking excitement that novel drugs in the works could tackle some of the toughest problems in medicine.

Moderna and Merck's melanoma drug, for instance, uses mRNA to trigger an immune response in patients, targeting tumors that have been surgically removed and genetically analyzed. It's not a preventive vaccine, but it uses a similar immunology mechanism and showed a 42% reduction in cancer recurrence or death (used in conjunction with Merck's cancer drug Keytruda).

While the Phase 3 results disappointed Wall Street, oncologists say the outcomes were still highly positive. And they see the technology as a potential game changer, ushering in an era of personalized treatments for cancers. Moderna and Merck have said they aim to submit the drug for FDA approval and get it launched in 2027.

Inflammation treatment is another promising area. AstraZeneca and Amgen recently released positive Phase 3 results for another use for their drug Tezspire, an injectable approved for nasal polyps and asthma. In clinical trials, it showed that it successfully relieved swallowing symptoms and healed microscopic and visual tissue damage in patients with severe throat inflammation.

Roche, meanwhile, notched a win after the FDA approved its drug Enspryng, a therapy to treat rare inflammatory and autoimmune diseases. It became the first and only at-home injection for adults with thyroid eye disease.

Obesity drugs are also advancing beyond the first generation of GLP-1s. Eli Lilly's retatrutide, a "triple agonist" targeting three different hormone receptors, cleared two more clinical trials over the summer. The drug produced 28%-30% weight loss at the highest doses in the study, Lilly said. The company plans to submit the drug for FDA approval in the first quarter of 2027.

Much of the innovation isn't just happening in drug-making – it's also in areas like gene sequencing, mRNA, and high-tech instruments.

Illumina has more than doubled this year. The DNA and gene sequencing company is seeing accelerating demand for its NovaSeq X high-end sequencing platform. Pharmaceutical companies including Bristol-Myers Squibb, Merck and Kura are working with Illumina to develop precision oncology solutions.

Another big winner is 10x Genomics, up more than 300%. The biotech firm offers its lab instruments to help scientists research cancer, immunology and neuroscience. The company's Atera platform is helping produce data sets to train AI models, a process that helps accelerate personalized therapies.

On the AI front, companies are using the technology for more efficient drug discovery, says Shreefāl Mehta, president of Guidant BioTherapeutics. Using AI, "you can now bring a drug to clinical trials with less capital. More investment is also going into companies that are demonstrating that," he said.

Global AI in drug discovery will grow from a $20 billion market in 2025 to more than $160 billion by 2035, according to forecasts by Towards Health, a research and consulting firm.

One other catalyst for the sector could be M&A. Dealmaking for biopharma hit $134 billion in the first half of 2026, up from $112 billion for all of 2025, notes Jeet Mukherjee, biotech analyst at BTIG. That's a positive signal, indicating more drugs in development are showing clinical promise.

It also helps that Big Pharma has a "patent cliff" ahead. Products representing $180 billion of revenue will lose exclusivity by 2030, according to research firm RTW Institute. Companies with looming expiries "are looking for novel products and innovative products to put into their pipeline," Mehta says.

GLP-1 blockbusters have also left some companies flush with cash for acquisitions. Eli Lilly alone has completed 10 acquisition deals in 2026 worth $25 billion.

ETFs can capture the investing theme, though they aren't all alike. The iShares Biotech ETF is one of the lower cost funds. It tracks a market-cap weighted index of biotech stocks, led by companies like Gilead Sciences, Amgen, Vertex Pharmaceuticals, and Moderna.

The State Street SPDR S&P Biotech ETF holds stocks in equal weight, offering more exposure to small- and mid-cap names. Its top holdings are Moderna, Natera, Twist Bioscience and Halozyme Therapeutics.

Piper Sandler calls Illumina and 10x Genomics the "primary infrastructure plays" in the life science arena, saying their technologies are "driving the next generation of precision medicine."

Piper analyst David Westenberg has a buy rating and $305 target on Illumina, implying 14% gains from recent prices around $267. He has a hold rating on 10X with an $80 target, above recent prices around $70.

Twist Bioscience is another name to consider. The company makes customized synthetic DNA and sells DNA sequencing tools. Twist has partnered with Eli Lilly's AI and machine-learning platform and linked up with Anthropic in August to start testing its AI model, Claude, for protein development.

Twist isn't expected to turn an EPS profit until 2029, according to consensus forecasts. But revenue is expected to go from $572 million in 2027 to $833 million in 2029. Analysts have an average target of $168 on the stock, implying 21% returns from recent prices around $138.

In the small-cap space, Piper Sandler's Tenthoff likes Monte Rosa Therapeutics, a pioneer in protein degradation that selectively destroys disease-causing proteins.

The firm has partnered with Novartis to develop a drug for inflammatory conditions and autoimmune diseases. The companies announced a Phase 2 clinical study in September for an autoimmune disease treatment, which has been "triggering milestones to Monte Rosa," Tenthoff said.

Tenthoff has a buy rating and $37 target on the stock, which recently traded around $10.70.

Also in the small-cap arena, BTIG's Mukherjee recommends Faeth Therapeutics.

The biotech is developing a novel two-drug regimen to treat breast cancer and other tumors. The regimen "has the potential to address safety and dosing limitations of another recently approved drug for breast cancer," he says. And it could become "a mainstay of the future breast cancer treatment paradigm and other cancers."

Faeth is a clinical-stage company that has yet to bring a drug to market. Yet the stock is up 93% year-to-date on positive clinical trial results, trading around $18. Mukherjee has a $55 target on it.

Kymera Therapeutics is worth considering too. The company is developing an oral version of the blockbuster drug Dupixent (made and co-developed by Regeneron Pharmaceuticals and Sanofi). Dupixent is an injectable, however, and oral agents "are frequently preferred by patients," Mukherjee says. "The drug has the potential to not only take share from patients taking Dupixent for numerous immunological diseases but also expanding the market to those not taking systemic biologic drugs," he tells CNBC.

Kymera has said it doesn't expect a patent fight over Dupixent because its drug is distinct, not a biosimilar or biologic copy.

T. Rowe's Hall also likes Kymera for its potential "to unlock disease targets that have been classically considered un-druggable." The stock is the second largest holding in T. Rowe's Biotech ETF, accounting for 4.2% of the portfolio.

#3039. To Defend Against AI Attackers, Start Thinking Like One

Yair Grindlinger is CEO and Co-Founder of Surf AI.

AI is not black magic. It still has to work through real systems, identities, applications and infrastructure, which means its limits are knowable. That matters, because fear is only useful when it’s aimed at the right things.

For enterprises, the right thing to fear is how AI changes the attacker. AI-driven attacks are faster, more persistent and more sophisticated, and they can exploit a single critical weakness or chain together a handful of small ones until they find a way in. ​​

There are several obvious ways AI changes attacks. First is speed. We’re seeing attacks happen in hours instead of days or weeks because AI systems can operate 24/7. The window for defenders is already shrinking. CrowdStrike’s 2026 Global Threat Report found that the average time it took criminal attackers to move from initial access deeper into a network​ fell to 29 minutes, and the fastest observed breakout took just 27 seconds.

But I think stamina may be even more important than speed. A typical human attacker might spend a few hours trying to get into an organization. If nothing works, they may move to the next victim. AI doesn’t necessarily have that limitation. If you tell an AI system to find a way in, it can keep working 24/7 until it finds something.

Then there is breadth of knowledge. Human attackers are typically good at something, whether it’s social engineering, zero-days or a particular system. AI can have the whole playbook. The same system can work across identity, software vulnerabilities, configurations and other attack surfaces. Because it has both stamina and breadth of knowledge, it can connect those weaknesses together.

That ability to connect weaknesses changes how we need to think about exposure. An attack might begin with an exposed secret, then move to an asset with a risky configuration, a software vulnerability or an identity problem. Each issue might look relatively insignificant on its own. You might classify each one as medium or low risk and decide none is critical enough to demand immediate attention. But together, they can create a critical attack path.

Microsoft made essentially the same point in its July 2026 “Secure Future Initiative“ progress report, noting that attackers can use “frontier models to discover vulnerabilities, chain attack paths, and scale exploitation faster than manual approaches allow.” It also observed that serious security failures can emerge when identity gaps, unmanaged assets and inconsistent configurations sit next to one another.

The Hugging Face intrusion offers a vivid example. In July 2026, the company disclosed that an autonomous AI agent, which had escaped an AI lab’s evaluation environment, carried out an end-to-end intrusion into part of its production infrastructure, with no human directing its steps. The attack moved from code-execution weaknesses to credential harvesting and lateral movement across internal clusters. Hugging Face later reconstructed roughly 17,600 attacker actions as the system searched for a viable path.​ ​Most of those actions failed. The agent simply kept going until something worked.

A human attacker may look for one big open window and move on if it isn’t there. AI will find that window faster, and if it isn’t there, it will patiently look for the little things and chain them together.

Attackers and defenders often see the environment differently. From a defender’s perspective, we frequently manage security in islands. There may be a cloud vulnerability team, an identity team, IT teams and someone else responsible for third-party applications. The attacker doesn’t care about those organizational boundaries. To the attacker, all of those things are part of the same attack surface.

Suppose a team is looking at a cloud vulnerability. On its own, it might not appear urgent. But what if the asset can be accessed by someone whose MFA is misconfigured and who also uses a highly privileged third-party application? If the teams responsible for those areas don’t know the issues are connected, they don’t understand the real priority.

That becomes especially important as AI gives defenders visibility into more exposures. In many cases, these weaknesses were probably always there. We simply know about more of them now. That’s a good thing because defenders can use powerful tools to find the same types of exposures attackers are looking for.

The problem is that organizations will get a lot of results. It will be impossible to manage everything at once. Prioritization therefore has to change. Instead of looking only at how severe an individual vulnerability appears to be, ask whether it is exposed, how it connects to other weaknesses and whether it forms part of an attack chain.

A critical exposure on an important asset still demands attention, whether or not it’s part of a chain. But something that appears lower-risk may also offer a quick win because fixing it breaks a larger chain.

AI has made finding vulnerabilities faster and easier than ever. Closing them still depends on knowing who owns the affected asset, understanding what a fix could affect and getting approval, and that’s where most organizations get stuck. In many of my conversations with CISOs, we never get to attack chains. They have critical exposures they can’t close.

One CISO at a large enterprise told me that 25% to 35% of employees leave or change roles in a given year. Many of them own systems, and the asset inventories meant to track that are almost never up to date. So security teams spend their days chasing people to find out who can approve a fix. That isn’t the job anyone signed up for, and it’s the bottleneck that keeps exposures open. Before you can think in chains, you have to be able to close the basics quickly.​

I see two fundamental requirements for responding to this shift. First, organizations need to know their assets: what they have, who owns each one and what a change could affect. Second, they need automation operating continuously on top of that knowledge.

If attackers are operating at machine speed, defenders cannot depend entirely on human-speed processes. Organizations need systems that continuously collect information, connect exposures and help security teams identify which problems matter most.​​ Then they need to get each fix to the right people, because a prioritized list nobody can act on doesn’t reduce risk.

This matters even for threats no one has seen yet. You can’t patch a zero-day before you know it exists, but you can shrink your blast radius every day by closing the exposures around it, so the next one matters less before it’s even disclosed. And when it does land, you can be ready to answer “are we exposed?” in hours, not weeks.​

For CISOs, my practical advice is to pause and rethink exposure management. The way we have managed exposure until today isn’t necessarily going to work for the AI era. Assign someone, or create a team, whose job is to think about exposure holistically. Then reconsider the people, process and technology required to support that approach.

AI creates real reasons for concern. But the most useful fear is a specific one: attackers are becoming faster, more persistent and more sophisticated. Defenders need to respond in kind: know their assets, shrink their blast radius every day and be ready for the next one.

In other words, we need to start thinking a little more like the attackers.​

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#3036. Apple Says It Will Keep Paying More for Memory Chips After December. That's Good News for Micron Stock.

If the iPhone maker keeps paying more for memory into next year, why did the memory chipmaker's stock just fall 6%?

Shares of memory chipmaker Micron Technology (MU +2.58%) dropped around 6% on Nov. 2. The slide came the same day shares of Japanese flash memory maker Kioxia Holdings (KXIAY +2.10%) sank around 16% in Tokyo.

Kioxia, which posted results on Oct. 30, expects its revenue to rise only about 1% sequentially in the December quarter. And this comes right after a 40% surge from the June quarter to the September quarter.

Then, after the market closed on Nov. 2, Apple (AAPL -1.90%) sent a very different message. On its earnings call, the iPhone maker said it expects the prices it pays for memory to keep rising into next year.

I think Apple's comment matters more for Micron, because most of what Micron sells isn't the type of chip Kioxia makes.

"[L]ooking beyond the December quarter, we see the pricing for memory purchases continuing to increase," Apple CEO John Ternus said on his first earnings call on the job. He called the memory pricing Apple sees now "unprecedented."

The cost is already showing up in Apple's results. Its gross margin was 47.9% in the September quarter (its fiscal fourth quarter), a figure that included around 1 percentage point of benefit from tariff refunds. For the December quarter, management guided for a gross margin of 45% to 46%. Putting the tariff benefit aside, chief financial officer Kevan Parekh said most of the expected sequential drop at the midpoint of the range comes from higher memory costs. Apple also expects memory to limit how many Macs and iPads it can supply in the quarter, because demand has been stronger than it thought.

Of course, Apple didn't name its memory suppliers on the call, so this isn't about any single contract. But when a tech company with about $479 billion in fiscal 2026 sales says memory prices will keep rising, it's a useful signal for the whole market.

Kioxia makes flash memory (NAND, the chips that store data in phones, PCs, and data center drives), and its prices have been climbing more slowly. Its average selling price surged roughly 70% from the March quarter to the June quarter, then rose by a percentage in the mid-20s in the September quarter. For the December quarter, Kioxia expects flat shipments and just a moderate price increase.

However, Kioxia's own explanation points partly to currency, timing, and mix. Its guidance absorbs a hit of around ¥55 billion from a stronger yen. Some volume was sold early, in the September quarter, and more will slip into the March quarter as sales shift toward data center customers, whose orders take longer to fill. Chips for phones and other smart devices will also be a bigger share of the December quarter, which weighs on the average price. And Kioxia still expects supply shortages to last through 2027.

Micron, though, mostly sells DRAM (the short-term working memory that devices use as they run). During the three months ending Sept. 3 (Micron's fiscal fourth quarter of 2026), DRAM revenue reached a record $39.8 billion, up 343% from the year-ago quarter and equal to 73% of the company's total.

Flash was 26%, and even then, data center drives brought in over two-thirds of it.

Granted, Micron's price increases are slowing, too. Its DRAM prices climbed by a percentage in the low 60s in the fiscal third quarter, then by a high-teens percentage in the fiscal fourth quarter. Still, management expects revenue to grow sequentially in every quarter of fiscal 2027.

The bigger risk to memory stocks is that these price increases keep shrinking until they end. And higher prices might eventually hit demand. Kioxia expects PC makers to buy less flash memory through 2027 since higher component costs weigh on PC sales.

But Apple's comment suggests at least one massive buyer expects to keep paying more after December.

Meanwhile, Micron stock trades near $860 as I write this, over 30% off its 52-week high of $1,255.

Management's guidance calls for about $38 of earnings per share in the fiscal first quarter on a non-GAAP (adjusted) basis, up from about $33 in fiscal Q4.

Four quarters at this rate would put the stock at under 6 times earnings. In other words, the stock price arguably assumes memory profits fall sharply, and soon.

Is Apple's warning good news for Micron stock, then? I think it is. At that valuation, I'd consider buying shares after Monday's drop.

Kioxia's outlook is still worth remembering, though, because the next sign of slowing prices might hit the stock just as hard.

#3031. Russia confirms new Ukraine peace talks as Melania Trump prepares to host ceasefire meeting

Russia on Tuesday confirmed that peace talks with Ukraine and the U.S. will go ahead next week in Abu Dhabi, United Arab Emirates, while also signaling that a meeting between Presidents Vladimir Putin, Donald Trump and Xi Jinping to end the conflict was on the table.

Putin aide Vladimir ​Medinsky will serve ⁠as Moscow's chief negotiator ​at the talks in Abu Dhabi on Nov. 10-11, Kremlin spokesman ​Dmitry Peskov told reporters on a conference call, according to Reuters.

Separately, first lady Melania Trump will bring Russian and Ukrainian representatives to the White House to discuss the local ceasefire in Kherson, the White House said Tuesday.

Monday saw the end of a six-day ceasefire in the Russian-occupied city of Oleshky and surrounding areas in the Kherson region to allow for evacuation of residents, in a pause of hostilities that the White House said was brokered by the first lady.

Ukraine on Monday accused Russia of using the ceasefire to reinforce its military positions in the city.

The first lady's summit on Thursday will be "an extraordinary moment in American history," the White House said, but did not name the representatives who will attend. Melania Trump will deliver remarks on the Oleshky ceasefire at 11 a.m. ET on Thursday, it added.

Peskov also said Tuesday the Kremlin hopes to finalize plans for a trilateral meeting between the leaders of Russia, the U.S. and China in the coming days. The comments follow earlier reports that these countries were planning to negotiate an end to the war in Ukraine, without Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy at the table.

Zelenskyy has said that any negotiations over Ukraine's future must include Kyiv.

"It can be acceptable only when we are at the table of negotiations," he said on Oct. 20, adding that he had discussed the proposal of a three-way meeting between the U.S., China and Russia to negotiate an end to the nearly five-year full-scale war. "Nothing about Ukraine without Ukraine," he said.

Tensions between the U.S. and Ukraine have mounted over recent weeks amid continued strikes between Moscow and Kyiv, including on civilian and energy targets.

A series of moves by the Trump administration in October raised questions about Washington's reliability as Kyiv's most important military partner.

Tuesday's announcement follows U.S. special envoy Steve Witkoff saying late Monday that Washington will mediate Ukraine-Russia peace talks in Abu Dhabi on Nov. 10-11, focusing on security arrangements, de-escalation and humanitarian issues.

Witkoff also said that U.S. negotiators will meet European and NATO officials in Brussels this week to discuss postwar security guarantees and Ukraine's reconstruction.

#3027. Realty Income: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Shifting to credit deals and private capital lets the company preserve investment spreads without diluting shareholders.

On its third quarter of 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 2, 2026, Realty Income (O -0.14%) raised its 2026 adjusted funds from operations (AFFO) guidance to $4.45 to $4.46 per share while reaffirming its $10 billion annual investment target. AFFO measures normalized cash flow available for shareholder payouts. Broadening investment types and capital sources gives the commercial real estate owner greater flexibility to capture higher returns as borrowing costs fluctuate. Three key insights from management explain how the company is driving this growth.

Management expanded international deal activity in which property pricing yields wider margins than local debt costs. Industrial acquisitions in Continental Europe now provide stronger initial rental cash flow than typical domestic assets.

Rather than relying only on traditional equity purchases of real estate, the company is deploying capital into secured loan structures. Taking debt positions backed by high-quality properties offsets elevated borrowing costs while preserving potential pathways to future asset ownership.

The business is shifting away from funding deals primarily through issuing public stock. Tapping institutional and private capital, entering into joint ventures, and selling existing properties create non-dilutive funds for higher-yielding reinvestments.

Diversified funding channels and multi-region sourcing keep deal spreads wide even when public markets prove volatile. Management relies on disciplined capital recycling and private partnerships to fuel growth without adding balance sheet risk.

Expanding into European logistics, selective data centers, and real estate credit protects cash flows while positioning the enterprise for steady long-term income.

#3024. Apollo to buy nearly 10% stake in Abu Dhabi-backed alternative asset manager Mubadala Capital

Investing giant Apollo Global Management is finalizing a deal to buy nearly a 10% stake in Mubadala Capital, the alternative asset manager subsidiary of Abu Dhabi's sovereign wealth fund, Mubadala Investment Co., according to people familiar with the matter who spoke on condition of anonymity.

The deal is expected to be announced later Monday morning, they said. Financial terms were not immediately known.

The transaction is significant on multiple fronts. It further advances Apollo's ambitions in Abu Dhabi often called the "capital of capital." The firm has spent the past 15 years building key strategic relationships there, with both the country's investment arm and its core leadership.

Apollo already has a multibillion-dollar partnership with Mubadala Investment Co., first established in 2022 and extended two years ago. At the time of that extension, Apollo said the agreement would strengthen the firm's Capital Solutions platform by enabling the firms to jointly source and provide customized debt and equity financing to companies worldwide.

In 2023, the institutions formed a $2.5 billion joint venture designed specifically to co-invest in global private credit opportunities over a five-year horizon.

For Mubadala Capital specifically — the region's only large-scale asset manager, advising and administering more than $755 billion in assets — the deal further advances its growth strategy under the direction of CEO Hani Barhoush, and its relevance on the global investing stage. It also marks an inflection point for the company's core alternatives business, which has grown to more than $60 billion.

John Zito, co-president of Apollo's Asset Management arm, and Barhoush have worked closely together over the past several years and, according to sources, their close-knit relationship and the success of prior partnerships led to talks of deepening ties. So did the belief that scale in the asset management business matters more than ever.

"Capital as a moat is a big thing," said one person familiar with the deal.

Importantly, the deal also continues a notable shift in the relationship between American investment firms and the Middle East. While the region, and Abu Dhabi specifically, has long been a sought-after source of capital, this deal further reverses that dynamic, with Apollo making a significant direct investment in the country and its investment vision.

Mubadala Capital's international offices include London, New York, Rio de Janeiro and San Francisco.

Apollo's investment in Mubadala Capital is similar to deals the firm has done with other asset management platforms, including Diameter Capital, Thoreau, Motive Partners and others.

Spokespersons for both firms declined to comment.

#3021. Job openings posted big September gain in positive sign for labor market ahead of payrolls report

Job openings surged in September, as did the number of workers who quit their positions, the Bureau of Labor Statistics reported Tuesday, while hiring also rose and layoffs dropped.

In its monthly look at labor market slack and worker mobility, the BLS reported job openings at 7.52 million, up 333,000 from August and well above the Dow Jones consensus outlook for 7.15 million.

As a share of the total workforce, the rate of job openings increased to 4.5%, up 0.2 percentage point from the prior month, according to the Job Openings and Labor Turnover Survey. There are now some 414,000 more job openings than workers classified as unemployed, or about 1.05 openings to every available worker.

Openings rose in manufacturing (54,000) as well as state and local government (108,000) and professional and business services (106,000).

At the same time, the total of employees leaving their jobs spiked to 3.36 million, an increase of 321,000 from August and the highest level since March 2025. Quits are considered a barometer for worker confidence in finding new jobs after leaving their prior positions.

"There are more job openings than unemployed people in the United States right now. That doesn't solve all problems, but it's a big change from a year ago," said Heather Long, chief economist at Navy Federal Credit Union. "It's an encouraging sign that despite all the headwinds right now with high diesel and gas prices and ongoing inflation problems, companies are slowly increasing their hiring."

Hires rose on the month, up 131,000 to 5.4 million, while layoffs and discharges fell by 127,000 to 1.64 million, the lowest since March 2025.

The report comes ahead of the October nonfarm payrolls report, which the BLS will release Friday. The Wall Street consensus is for an increase of 59,000 after September eked out a gain of just 29,000, while the unemployment rate is figured to hold at 4.2%.

In other economic news Tuesday, September factory orders for manufactured goods jumped 1.8%, well above the estimate for 1.3% after increasing just 0.3% in August.

#3018. Apple stock sinks as global memory crisis hurts profits, giving Ternus his first big test

John Ternus' first earnings report as Apple CEO was going fine until it was time to issue a forecast for the company's gross margin, one of the key metrics followed by investors.

Apple reported better-than-expected earnings and revenue on Monday, including in the company's core iPhone unit, and even projected sales in the current period that topped estimates. But just before Ternus opened the earnings call up to analysts' questions, CFO Kevan Parekh came forward with the first real piece of bad news.

Parekh said that for the December quarter, which goes through the holiday season, Apple's gross margin will be between 45% and 46%, trailing the 46.3% average analyst estimate, according to LSEG.

"This includes primarily the impact of higher memory costs, as well as some costs related to the ramp of Siri AI," Parekh said.

It's part of an ongoing trend for Apple, which is absorbing some of the soaring costs from the global memory shortage. A margin drop in the fiscal first quarter would be a third straight decline for Apple, following a gross margin of 50.1% in the June period and 47.9% in the September quarter, boosted by 1 percentage point of tariff refunds.

At the midpoint of the forecast range, it would be Apple's lowest gross margin since the fiscal fourth quarter of 2023.

"What we're seeing with memory pricing right now is unprecedented," Ternus said on the call, adding that the company is paying more than it did earlier this year. "Looking beyond the December quarter, we see the pricing for memory purchases continuing to increase."

Ternus, who took the reins from Tim Cook on Sept. 1, is grappling with a problem that his predecessor spent his final months addressing. Cook told The Wall Street Journal in June that the memory situation was a "hundred-year flood." Apple followed by raising prices across the board on Macs, iPads and accessories.

In September, Apple increased the prices of its iPhones by $100, which Ternus said the company did "reluctantly."

Computer and smartphone makers everywhere are suffering. On Friday, Intel had its worst day on the market since the 1970s due to a weak forecast tied to the softening PC market. It's all because of insatiable artificial intelligence demand.

AI server giants like Nvidia and hyperscalers are buying up all the RAM they can find, and they're willing to pay a premium for it, leaving little supply for companies that make smartphones and tablets.

Despite its size and global clout, Apple said it expects supply constraints on iPhones and Macs in the holiday quarter, primarily driven by memory.

On the call, Ternus had some top lieutenants field component-related questions. Sabih Khan, chief operating officer, became the first Apple COO to appear on an earnings call since Cook in 2010, when Steve Jobs was CEO.

"As we have said before, we're seeing less flexibility in the supply chain than normal," Khan told analysts on the call.

In response, Apple has drastically increased its purchase obligations to secure supply, Khan said. Those obligations rose by $33 billion from the June quarter to the September period, Khan said, though he declined to answer questions about whether Apple had signed "long-term agreements" with memory makers.

"We're always working hard to improve supply," Khan said.

Even with the recent margin slide, Apple's gross margins are still well above where they sat earlier in the Cook era, when the number would predictably come in between 38% and 39%. As Apple's profitable services business grew, its gross margin expanded, hitting 40% in 2021 and growing from there.

Analysts see a potential silver lining for Apple in the current market turmoil. Because Apple is more capable of eating a portion of the increased costs, some experts predict the company will take market share from makers of Windows PC and Android phones that don't have such a cushion.

Citing data from a third-party researcher, Ternus said gains are already happening.

"According to IDC, we gained share globally for the quarter and for the full fiscal year," Ternus said. "And we have never sold more Macs in a fiscal year than we did this time, which is a truly remarkable testament to these incredible products."

#3016. Empire State Realty: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Rent spreads have stayed positive for 21 straight quarters, while overhead cuts of 5% to 10% are slated to finish by year-end 2026.

On its third quarter of 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 3, 2026, Empire State Realty (ESRT +1.20%) reported steady commercial demand and outlined strategic steps to recycle capital into share buybacks. The real estate investment trust is trimming overhead costs and bringing in new leadership to revitalize its flagship tourism business while riding consistent rent increases in its Manhattan office portfolio. Three things management said stand out.

The company sold its 1359 Broadway office property for $217.5 million to deploy the proceeds toward repurchasing its stock, which management considers undervalued relative to its asset backing. This capital recycling strategy boosts cash flow per share while preserving balance sheet strength with no debt maturities until March 2029.

Demand for Manhattan commercial space pushed portfolio capacity to 95.6% during the quarter. By securing higher rates on new leases than expiring ones, the firm maintains pricing power and extends lease terms to lock in predictable revenue. Mark-to-market rent spreads measure changes in rental rates between old and new leases for the same space.

Corporate restructuring and leadership updates are driving lower expenses across the organization. Empire State Realty expects to reduce run rate general and administrative expenses, or G&A, by 5% to 10% by year-end 2026, with an additional 2% reduction targeted for 2027.

High office occupancy rates and low energy costs anchor portfolio stability as executive leadership refocuses digital customer acquisition at the Empire State Building observation deck.

Disciplined capital allocation and balance sheet flexibility remain central to the long-term plan.

#3015. Crude oil prices fall for second day as market hopes for diplomatic resolution to Iran war

Crude oil prices fell Tuesday for a second consecutive session, as the market hopes the U.S. and Iran will reach a diplomatic settlement to the conflict that has disrupted global energy supplies for eight months.

U.S. West Texas Intermediate futures lost 37 cents to close at $96.09 a barrel after dipping below $95 earlier in the session. Brent crude, the international benchmark, shed $1.17 to settle at $103.60.

The move lower came after rumors circulated the U.S. and Iran were making progress toward a deal.

Oil sold off more than 7% on Monday after Washington and Tehran signaled they were both open to discussions to reach a settlement. President Donald Trump told reporters Tehran wants "to make a deal and that could happen."

Qatar is continuing to mediate talks between the U.S. and Iran, the Gulf state's foreign ministry said Tuesday. A delegation from Qatar held talks in Tehran in an effort to reach an agreement that prevents further escalation in the region and ends the U.S.-Iran conflict, the foreign ministry said.

However, Iran's Foreign Ministry spokesman Emsaeil Baqaei said Tuesday that Tehran will not accept diplomacy based on coercion, according to the state-owned media outlet Islamic Republic News Agency. It was unclear where talks actual stand.

The market had been pricing in the risk of escalation after the U.S. midterm elections, as reports circulated that Washington was drafting plans for strikes on Iran. Crude prices gained more than 10% in October in response.

The situation in the Middle East remains volatile and there's still substantial risk that fighting could break out. Washington attacked Iran's Qeshm Island on Monday, U.S. officials told The Wall Street Journal, adding that the U.S. has been carrying out "limited strikes" against the country in recent weeks.

The military campaign against Iran has been kept deliberately low-profile to avoid signaling plans for a major escalation in fighting, the report said, quoting an unnamed official.

And commercial ships continue to come under attack in the Persian Gulf, Strait of Hormuz and Gulf of Oman. At least four ship attacks have been reported since the start of the month, according to the United Kingdom Maritime Trade Operations Centre.

Despite these attacks, the Trump administration has also been touting a possible diplomatic solution to the conflict, with Vice President JD Vance telling CNN, "The president and I both feel really good about the prospect of potentially coming to a negotiated settlement."

On Sunday, U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio said Trump would like to resolve the Iranian war "diplomatically," and prefers not to use military force.

High oil prices have contributed substantially to inflation and high gasoline prices in the U.S., which have become a cause of concern for Trump ahead of the midterm elections.

Last week, two Federal Reserve presidents said interest rates will need to move higher to rein in inflation. Higher rates generally crimp growth and raise costs for consumers, but help to bring down inflation.

— CNBC's Priyanka Salve contributed to this report.

#3014. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Global stock markets remain volatile amid persistent geopolitical tensions and concerns about the sustainability of AI trade. Given this backdrop, investors seeking steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking recommendations from top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick attractive stocks, as these experts assess a company's ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is a dividend king. The company has increased its dividend for 39 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share (annualized dividend of $7.12 per share), CVX offers a yield of 3.14%. Chevron will announce its third-quarter results on October 30.

In an earnings preview note on U.S. oil majors, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Chevron with a price target of $241. The analyst is focused on the company's international growth prospects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa, and the Eastern Mediterranean.

Specifically, Mehta highlighted management's commentary on solid execution in Venezuela, with gross production expected to more than double to nearly 600 kbd (kilo barrels per day) by 2031 via three joint ventures. He also noted a recent oil and gas condensate discovery in offshore Angola and is awaiting commercial updates on the West Qurna 2 and Nasiriyah projects in Iraq.

Furthermore, Mehta expects CVX to report improved productivity and capital efficiency across domestic shale assets. He expects the improvement mainly in the Permian Basin, where Chevron projects advanced chemical treatments to drive about a 10% recovery uplift in new wells and bring down capital spend per barrel by about 25% year-over-year. Overall, Mehta expects Chevron to report Q3 2026 EPS of $4.95, above the consensus of $4.89.

"While unfavorable timing effects and volatile commodity prices may present quarterly earnings headwinds, we highlight healthy U.S. refining margins and strong asset utilization as partial offsets," said Mehta.

Mehta ranks No. 381 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Chevron KPIs on TipRanks.

Kinetik Holdings (KNTK) is a fully integrated, pure-play, midstream company operating in the Delaware Basin. Earlier this month, the company declared a dividend of $0.81 per share, payable on October 30, 2026. With an annualized dividend of $3.24 per share, KNTK offers a yield of 5.82%. Kinetik will announce its Q3 earnings on November 4, with a conference call scheduled the next day.

Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, Goldman Sachs analyst John Mackay reiterated a buy rating on Kinetik stock and slightly raised his price target to $58 from $57. He expects the company to report Q3 2026 EBITDA of $279 million, higher than the Street's expectation of $270 million but lower than the second quarter's EBITDA of $281 million.

The 5-star analyst expects Kinetik's Q3 performance to gain from higher gas volumes after notable curtailments in the second quarter. Mackay also expects the quarter to benefit from higher production and steady commodity margins, even though some lower-margin contracts and weaker Waha spread marketing gains could weigh on results.

Overall, Mackay increased his Q3 2026 estimates to reflect higher volumes, driven by rapid Permian egress pipeline commissioning, stronger throughput at Kings Landing I, and higher M2M commodity prices, offset by increased costs.

"As natural gas takeaway constraints ease across the Permian, investor focus centers on KNTK's YE26 volume trajectory and durability of its multi-year growth," said Mackay.

Mackay ranks No. 569 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.9%. See Kinetik's Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving to Crescent Energy (CRGY), an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian, and Uinta Basins. With a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share (annualized dividend of $0.48 per share), CRGY's dividend yield stands at 3.56%. Earlier this month, Crescent announced an agreement to acquire Eagle Ford assets from Devon Energy (DVN).

Heading into Q3 earnings on Nov. 2, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reiterated a buy rating on Crescent stock and slightly lowered the price target to $17 from $18, citing dilution from new shares.

"CRGY's recent acquisition of DVN's Eagle Ford assets marks a strategic, growth-oriented move, potentially enhancing cash flow performance," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst noted that the acquisition of the Eagle Ford assets at a net purchase price of $3.85 billion will add about 68 MBoe/d of net production and over 600 net Tier 1 locations. Richardson added that a solid track record of synergies from past deals in the Eagle Ford region increases confidence about Crescent achieving efficiencies with this deal.

Additionally, Richardson noted that the financial implications of the acquisition seem impressive, with a projected increase in cash flow per share of about 9% for 2027 to 2029.

The analyst believes that Crescent is not just expanding its business through acquisitions but positioning itself as a more durable company, regardless of market conditions.

Richardson ranks No. 478 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Crescent Energy Statistics on TipRanks.

#3013. Alphabet's Free Cash Flow Is Declining Faster Than Analysts Expected. Is This AI Stock Still a Buy?

Alphabet's AI expenses are rising, but the investment in long-term AI services could pay off.

Alphabet (GOOGL +0.37%) (GOOG +0.08%) stock fell sharply after the company reported its third-quarter results (ended Sept. 30), as investors processed several pieces of bad news. The first was that the company's search revenue of $63.4 billion was below analysts' consensus estimate of $64.5 billion. Fears that the company won't be able to monetize its AI Overviews as well as chatbots eating into its traditional search function weighed on its share price.

Worse yet, Alphabet reported negative free cash flow of $9.1 billion, far more than the $2.7 billion drop analysts expected.

Some shareholders viewed that information and immediately sold Alphabet stock. Here's why I think they're wrong and why it's a good time to pick up some shares amid the sell-off.

Let's get the Search revenue disappointment out of the way first. Alphabet's Search sales rose by 12% from the year-ago quarter to $63 billion, but missed Wall Street's consensus estimates for the second consecutive quarter.

There are legitimate concerns that ChatGPT, Claude, and other chatbots could undermine the company's ability to monetize search results. But it's worth noting that all artificial intelligence (AI) companies are trying to figure out how to be profitable with their services right now, so this isn't a unique problem for Alphabet.

What's more, Alphabet said it now has 1 billion people using its Gemini app; that's up 54% from the same time last year. The company also said AI Mode searches are 3X longer than traditional searches. In short, Alphabet is adapting to AI, and I think it'll continue to figure out how to monetize artificial intelligence search in new ways as usage grows.

Alphabet's negative free cash flow has everything to do with the company's surging spending on artificial intelligence infrastructure and little to do with its long-term profitability. What's more, $5.2 billion of the decline was due to a one-time cash payment for a European Commission fine.

Yes, the spending is very high. Alphabet's management said it will spend up to $205 billion in capex this year, mostly for AI. But focusing on at that amount, while overlooking the company's surging AI cloud sales, misses Alphabet's long-term prospects.

Google Cloud revenue spiked 103% to $30.7 billion in the quarter, and the company said it has $561 billion in backlog, more than half of which it expects to convert to revenue over the next two years.

That growth and large backlog of potential revenue show just how big Alphabet's AI cloud opportunity is. Google is the third-largest cloud player behind Amazon and Microsoft, with about 14% of the market, and is spending heavily now to maintain its position.

That's because AI cloud services are expected to be worth $2 trillion by 2030. To stay competitive in this space, Alphabet has to invest heavily on the front end to tap this market for years to come.

There's no guarantee of success, of course. But with cloud sales more than doubling in the quarter, shareholders should understand Alphabet is making big strides.

Alphabet's stock has a trailing price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio of just 14.7 right now, which is far below the tech sector average P/E ratio of about 24. By this metric, Alphabet stock is a downright bargain.

Does Alphabet have the best AI model with Gemini? Probably not. But does the company have a large user base, an established Search function that's evolving with AI, and a booming cloud business that benefits from artificial intelligence compute demands? Indeed, it does.

I think it's wise for investors to keep an eye on Alphabet's spending, and they should certainly watch for Google Cloud sales to continue rising as the company invests hundreds of billions in AI infrastructure.

But while the company's disappointing negative free cash flow isn't great to see, it shouldn't worry long-term investors because Alphabet is positioning itself now to continue tapping into the massive AI cloud market for years to come.

Chris Neiger has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Alphabet, Amazon, and Microsoft. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3012. Bill Ackman warns of 'casino' stock market as speculative trading grows

Bill Ackman told CNBC on Tuesday he worries that the stock market is broken, saying the rise of speculative trading is leading to wild price swings thanks to the amount of leverage in the system.

"Every number of days, I feel like markets are broken," Ackman said on CNBC's "Squawk Box." "Stocks trade at prices that make no sense to me. Yes, it's good if you're a long-term investor and you can buy at a stupid price. I don't think it's good for the country generally."

"We're back to the casino-type markets when there was 95% leverage," he added. "I don't think that's inherently good for the economy, for society, and it's leading to these massive moves in stocks."

The founder of Pershing Square Capital Management said those same dislocations in the market have given the hedge fund the opportunity to snag big bargains.

Pershing bought Microsoft, for example, when it was pummeled during the "SaaSpocalypse" earlier this year, and Amazon when it fell following President Donald Trump's tariffs, Ackman said. It also bought Alphabet when the stock moved "massively" following ChatGPT's launch, he said.

But the billionaire investor worries that the rise of one-day leveraged vehicles, perpetual futures and sports betting is dangerous for investors who are treating the stock market as a get-rich-quick scheme — an attitude that could have disastrous consequences.

"It's good for us long term — 'us' being Pershing Square," he said. "I don't think it's good for the country."

Among the stocks Ackman is especially bullish on are Visa and Mastercard, which he called among the world's "greatest" businesses, as well as streaming platform Netflix, in which he disclosed a position in August.

#3011. L3Harris: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Manufacturing efficiency gains and a pause on dealmaking signal L3Harris is betting on internal execution to drive returns.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Oct. 29, 2026, L3Harris (LHX -0.46%) reported organic revenue growth of 9% and a backlog of $53 billion. Demand for missile propulsion and defense communications continues to expand across military programs. That growing order book offers multiyear revenue visibility for long-term shareholders. Three takeaways from management's comments stand out.

A $11 billion contractual booking for Patriot Advanced Capability-3 and Terminal High Altitude Area Defense missile systems expanded total backlog to $53 billion. That massive order volume secures years of manufacturing activity in core defense sectors.

Management is using artificial intelligence and automated testing to remove factory bottlenecks in missile production. Faster quality checks allow factory lines to double annual rocket motor output without sacrificing product quality.

After acquiring multiple businesses, the defense contractor is halting major dealmaking to direct capital toward internal operations and debt reduction. Management plans to fund a $3 billion missile capacity expansion using internally generated cash flow.

A $53 billion backlog and elevated global defense spending position the company to navigate shifting political budgets. Internal investments in manufacturing capacity create structural cost advantages over competitors. Management focuses on operational execution, debt reduction, and disciplined capital allocation.

Substantial free cash flow gives the company financial flexibility to expand facilities while returning capital to shareholders.

#3006. The 'poison pill' ballot measure that could kill the California billionaire tax

The Inside Wealth newsletter by Robert Frank is your weekly guide to high-net-worth investors and the industries that serve them.

The California billionaire tax could face a stiff challenge from a competing ballot measure that would ban state taxes on retirement accounts, according to tax experts.

California voters will decide the fate today of Proposition 40, known as the Billionaire Tax Act, which would impose a one-time tax of 5% on the total net worth of Californians worth over $1 billion. The so-called "billionaire tax" is one of the most widely watched ballot measures this election season, since it would be the first state tax on net worth and taps popular anger over the rising power and wealth of tech billionaires.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of the 2026 midterm elections

The tax requires a simple majority to pass. Since it would only tax the state's roughly 250 billionaires, many political experts initially predicted easy passage. Yet a recent poll by the Public Policy Institute of California shows it falling short, with 46% supporting and 52% against.

Supporters of the tax blame a $300 million ad blitz funded by tech billionaires and business groups opposing the measure. Many of the ads claim the ballot measure would allow the Legislature, with a two-thirds vote, to expand the tax to everyday Californians.

Yet tax experts cite another potential roadblock to the tax: Proposition 42. One of two ballot measures that would act as poison pills against the billionaire tax if they get more votes, Prop 42 calls for a permanent ban on any statewide tax on property of all Californians.

The ballot measure "Prohibits any new state tax (1) imposed on the ownership of personal property (all things people own other than real estate), or (2) that applies retroactively based on taxpayer's past activities."

The official California Voter Information Guide explains the "pro" argument by saying that Prop 42 protects Californians from double taxation by "prohibiting new and retroactive taxes on personal property, including retirement and savings."

The "con" argument, according to the voter guide, is that Prop 42 is "just another billionaire-funded trick." Propositions 41 and 42 were created and submitted by Building a Better California, a political action committee funded by tech executives and several California billionaires opposed to the billionaire tax.

Political and tax experts say voters' fears about protecting retirement accounts will be a powerful motivation to vote "yes" on Prop 42, thus nullifying the billionaire tax. As much as they may want to tax billionaires, their retirement accounts may be more important.

"Both Proposition 41, which prohibits new taxes from disregarding voter-approved spending limits, and Proposition 42, which prohibits new taxes on retirement accounts and personal property, could win majority support," said Jared Walczak of Walczak Policy Consulting.

#3004. Investors are doing just as well this year by shunning AI. How to invest if the tech trade keeps fizzling

A version of the S&P 500 without AI briefly pulled ahead of the traditional index this year and is now neck-and-neck as investors rotate into other areas of the market.

A proprietary Goldman Sachs index tracking the S&P 500 without stocks classified as "AI enablers" was up 12.4% through Oct. 22, edging ahead of the standard S&P 500's 12% gain. The Goldman index is now neck-and-neck with the S&P 500 as tech rallied in Monday trading.

Tech and AI-related companies make up about 45% of the S&P 500. That heavy weighting has helped power the index for years, but it's turned into a liability lately; the S&P 500 tech sector is down about 7% this month while other sectors have held up.

Investors have soured on debt and equity issued by companies like CoreWeave and Oracle. Shares of Meta Platforms sank almost 7% Thursday after it outlined more plans to spend heavily on capex and issue additional shares.

Companies supplying power and equipment for the buildout have been hit hard — names like GE Vernova, Bloom Energy, Vistra and Quanta Services all suffered steep declines during the recent selloff.

Goldman, which launched the measure earlier this year with S&P, uses the S&P 500 as its starting universe and excludes companies perceived to be enablers of AI before applying S&P 500 Index weighting methodology to the remaining universe of companies.

The ex-AI index lagged the S&P 500 for most of the year as investors continued to favor chipmakers, cloud-computing companies and other firms benefiting from heavy AI spending. However, that advantage has been narrowing and it disappeared last week.

Meanwhile, investors are rotating into defensive areas. ETFs like State Street Consumer Staples Select Sector SPDR ETF, State Street Health Select SPDR ETF and State Street Utilities Select Sector SPDR ETF are up 7% to 10% this month. Energy is ahead 10% too, though that's more related to the war in Iran.

Some analysts recommend sticking with non-AI companies for diversification and protection against more pain in tech. The "AI pendulum has swung back in favor of non-AI exposure over the last two weeks," Oppenheimer technical analyst Ari Wald said in a note on Oct. 24.

Two industries that Oppenheimer favors now are commercial services and insurance. The firm upgraded those industries to overweight last week, recommending Republic Services, Cintas, Verisk Analytics, Broadridge Financial Solutions, MetLife and Arthur J. Gallagher.

Berkshire Hathaway is also capitalizing on the rotation out of tech. Its Class B shares are up 8% this month, though it's still lagging behind the S&P 500 this year.

Berkshire owns a wide swath of operating companies, including insurance giant Geico and Burlington Northern Santa Fe railroad. It also has a $364 billion stock portfolio including companies like Apple, Coca-Cola, Alphabet and Bank of America. That diversification may help Berkshire continue to outperform if the tech sector drags down the S&P 500.

Trivector Research sees potential in "non-tech compounders." Healthcare giant Eli Lilly is one idea. The stock is up 22% on the strength of Lilly's weight-loss drugs. The company has more in the pipeline, including a next-generation obesity drug that Lilly plans to file for FDA approval early in 2027.

Viking Holdings, a cruise operator, is also proving durable. The company said on its August earnings call that 40% of its river capacity and 60% of its ocean capacity was already booked for 2027. "Based on recent events, we've seen our guests have proved to be resilient and are continuing to book 2027 and future seasons," the company told investors.

Viking stock is up 15% this year but it may still have upside. The average Wall Street target is $109, above recent prices around $82. Barclays recently upgraded the cruise operator to overweight with a $102 target, citing the company's "compelling risk/reward."
