#2999. Sullivan: What the 2026 midterms could mean for energy investors

Don't expect huge near-term moves in energy stocks off the results of the midterms. But there are some investing implications over the longer-term. Let me break it down.

Yes, it's entirely possible that one or both houses of Congress will shift to Democratic control. That's not my opinion. It's what many of the research reports, polls, and prediction markets are suggesting as of this writing.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of the 2026 midterm elections

For argument's sake, let's say that this time the polls are correct. The election ends with a "blue trickle," and Democrats win out with a slight edge in the House, Senate, or both. It won't be enough to meaningfully change, well, anything. If the election ends with just a slight lean to the Democratic side, it won't be enough for a veto-proof majority. Candidates can specify policies — like halting the growth of data centers — all they want, but there's a chasm between being elected and passing new laws.

Research I've read and conversations we've had on CNBC suggest that the next two years in Washington, D.C., will be flush with finger-pointing, investigations into the president and his family, and posturing ahead of the far-more-important 2028 presidential election.

S&P Global doesn't exactly agree with me, saying that Democratic control of either chamber would bring "tougher oversight of agency decisions" and could impact things such as tax incentives and "other policies underpinning long-term energy investments."

A Democratic Congress could be good for offshore wind, an energy source the president seems to hate with the white-hot passion of the sun. Speaking of the sun, a shift blue could also be positive for the continued growth of solar, perhaps via new tax incentives. Though both are growing rapidly.

If the Republicans maintain control over one or both houses, we keep doing more of the same, albeit with more urgency. S&P Global adds that "a continued Republican majority would give the administration more room to pursue expanded domestic production, faster permitting and environmental deregulation, though narrow margins could limit major legislation."

Any change in control of Congress is also unlikely to make a big difference around the Iran conflict. Once any new Congress begins in January, things will have either ramped up, stayed the same, or cooled down. A new Congress could try to use its authority under the War Powers Act. The team at Piper Sandler agrees, writing that "elections matter, but will likely not change the direction of things" as Trump focuses more on his own legacy. That's a polite way of saying that the President is going to do largely what he wants, Congress be darned.

On the state level, the midterms may matter a little bit more, particularly as they relate to energy-hungry data centers. In Texas, a change in the statehouse could bring a change in sentiment around data centers. Candidate Gina Hinojosa has a policy to "end data center madness" and supports a moratorium on new builds. Amy Acton also supports a "conditional moratorium" on new data center builds in Ohio. Vivek Ramaswamy is also calling for conditions on new data center construction.

The issue is a big deal in many states. Yet even if those candidates, or candidates with similar views on big new energy projects, win, they'd still have to enact meaningful change. Which could happen, of course. So let's say it does happen. New governors enter office and use their power to throttle or even stop new projects. Even if some of the big new projects are stalled or stopped, power demand, at a minimum, would stay at current levels. Which means demand for power production inputs such as natural gas, nuclear, and yes, even coal, would remain at current levels.

As far as power-hungry utility stocks go, Bank of America agrees with my election take above - mostly. Its team writes that it thinks the "2026 cycle matters for utility equities in far fewer states than the headline count suggests," but that committee appointments in each state will matter. The BofA team says that, regarding the election and utility stocks, five states matter most: Georgia, Ohio, Oregon, Pennsylvania, and Texas.

Texas is the "most consequential," as state environmental regulators have halted data center permits. That state's Commission on Environmental Quality (TCEQ) has actually stopped electronic permitting to make sure any permit is unrelated to data centers. To my point, that's already known information. It matters, but markets are already aware of it and have priced it in. Regardless, if you are investing in anything related to data centers, it wouldn't hurt to pay attention to Texas' election most of all.

There's a lot here. So take a breath, step back, and consider where we are right now.

The Strategic Petroleum Reserve is at a 40-year low.

We have two wars in some of the most important energy regions in the world.

The backlog for data centers - and thus the power needed to run them - is years long. Even if a number of them end up canceled, others will be built.

These days, it's hard to be certain of anything. But I am certain that overall energy demand isn't going down in America anytime soon. The only way to go is up. It's just a question of how much farther up.

That's my opinion. It could be wrong. And since I love hearing from you, I put out a poll on both X and LinkedIn. The results suggest you disagree with me.

Note → I am fully aware that the results of any poll on X or LinkedIn will be skewed by my type and makeup of followers on each platform - who lean heavily into both business, money and energy.

So if the elections won't matter in the near term for energy, what does?

Glad you asked.  Here's my ranking of what will matter for energy in the next year:

(Disagree?  Hit me up on X or LinkedIn and let me know why)

An end to either the Iran conflict or Russia's war on Ukraine would be massively positive for the world and send oil, natural gas, and refined products lower relatively quickly. Let's all hope for peace.

Lower interest rates would help many of the beaten-up stocks that rely on debt and consumer lending to prosper. Think residential solar panels.

The macro economy is going to do what it will, regardless of the U.S. elections, and American tax policy is largely unchangeable for the next couple of years.

Permitting reform is, of course, one factor that could be impacted by the election outcome, though it's unlikely to happen in the near term.

No, my friends, whatever the outcome of America's elections, it's safe to say the real political attention will be on Trump, more Trump, Trump's family, even more Trump, and then looking ahead to 2028's presidential vote.

If we could harness political infighting into some sort of power source, it may be better than nuclear!

I'm leaving for Europe this week to continue our five-year coverage of Europe's slow-rolling energy crisis. And while the word "crisis" is way overused in the media, I don't think it is here. Natural gas import prices are soaring. Storage levels are below recent averages. Interest rates are rising, and the public is already rage-filled about higher energy prices. It's a toxic combo. And I'll be in Europe later this week to talk about it.

First, from a macro markets perspective, I remain wary of tech and the stock market right now. As I posted on October 14, the markets "feel fragile."

Since writing that, the Nasdaq is down over 5%. A.I. isn't in a bubble, but not all companies are going to prosper. Some may go away. Watch the debt markets, particularly some A.I.-related high-yield (aka "junk") bonds. The State Street High Yield (JNK) ETF is prominently on my screens these days. Get to know the term "leveraged ETF," because those are in focus these days. The selling in many of these products can lead to selling in other products and stocks. If we get a slowdown in selling in those, some of the big tech stocks that have been selling off might stabilize.

Turning more specifically to why you are here: energy.

Earnings are (almost) behind us. ExxonMobil (XOM) and Chevron (CVX) posted big numbers last week. Marathon Petroleum (MPC) just reported, and those numbers were even bigger. The company earned $26.59 per share on $11.7 billion in EBITDA. That compares to EPS of $3.01 on earnings of $3.2 billion EBITDA one year ago.

ConocoPhillips (COP) caps things off later this week.

What now is that the world waits, watches, and focuses on Iran, the Strait of Hormuz, and Russia. What happens with those will determine the path of prices for oil and much more. Peace, and prices go down. New attacks, prices go higher. Higher prices mean many big-cap oil and gas stocks keep going higher. If Russia continues its brutal war against Ukraine, diesel fuel and jet fuel prices are going to remain volatile.

My take → Keep an eye on any headlines related to Russia selling diesel fuel to the United States. There is one ship on the way (see the RBI below), and let's see if more follow.

The continued risk around Iran and Russia pushed Goldman Sachs to raise its oil price forecast for the rest of this year. The firm is taking Brent crude to $105 and WTI crude to $94, and raising its average targets for next year to $88 for Brent (from $80) and $78 for WTI (from $73). Let's see if others follow.

While earnings have U.S. analysts swamped, Goldman's European team isn't too busy to update its "European Conviction List" of stocks. Some of the names it likes most are energy-related, including German power producer RWE (RWE-DE) and the better-known Siemens Energy (ENR-DE).

Concerns about AI, power, and rising interest rates have recently hit both stocks. RWE is down 4% this quarter, with Siemens Energy falling over 10%. Goldman remains bullish. The firm has a €212 price target on Siemens Energy and a €75 target on power producer RWE. That implies about a 30% return on RWE and a huge 70% upside on Siemens Energy.

Europe may have a world of energy problems, but that doesn't mean some of its stocks can't make you money. For more, tune in to my live CNBC coverage from Italy on Thursday, November 5.

We recently spoke with Toby Rice, CEO of natural gas giant EQT (EQT). His company just made a massive deal to buy Range Resources (RRC). We spoke with Toby about the deal, energy markets, and the importance of U.S. gas to Europe.

Another important interview on energy and AI this week was with Brian Nowak of Morgan Stanley. Brian focuses on tech firms such as Amazon, but his comments about some of the AI power players - the names we mention above - are worth a listen.

This week's Inside Line is with Christoph Halser, Gas & LNG analyst at Rystad Energy.

Could the one ship carrying Russian diesel fuel bound for America… actually skip America? It might.

The vessel we've had a close eye on for a week is moving slowly in the Atlantic Ocean. So slowly that some are now wondering if the ship will actually make it to New York. Could the destination change mid-voyage? It happens with ships all the time. A new buyer steps in to buy the cargo, and the ship changes course.

It does make you wonder… what American company would buy a load of Russian diesel right now? Putting money into Putin's pocket is not the PR that any company I know would want, but stranger things have happened.

The ship is supposed to dock in New York on November 7. Following the course of this ship right now is definitely random, but interesting.

OpenAI deepens Abu Dhabi foothold as Sam Altman inks deals spanning energy, government and investment

World's energy supply buffers have been eroded, ADNOC CEO says (Reuters)

U.S. Pushes to Restore Russian Gas Sales to Europe Via Ukraine (WSJ)

#2984. AI-generated 'slopaganda' in political ads puts internet platforms on alert

The midterm election cycle has been dominated by "slopaganda," or campaign ads featuring AI-generated clips and images, creating another challenge for Instagram, YouTube and X as they attempt to monitor their platforms.

The term slopaganda dates back to early last year, when a group of experts on artificial intelligence used it in a paper to describe a new phenomenon combining generative AI slop with propaganda and misinformation.

There's no comprehensive federal law governing the use or disclosure of AI in political ads. And while there are some 30 state laws with different requirements about AI use and disclosure, candidates don't always abide by them. On the leading internet sites run by Meta, Google and Elon Musk's SpaceX, users run into AI-generated ads and AI-generated posts shared by politicians, alongside authentic videos and clips.

More than $187 million has been spent on nearly 300 ads generated or enhanced by AI, according to an analysis released last week by the Wesleyan Media Project. That's almost certainly an undercount, the organization says, because so many ads don't contain disclaimers and because determining AI use is difficult in some instances. Over 90% of the spending came from Republican candidates, a Republican party committee or a Republican-leaning group, the report found, and most don't include disclosures.

CNBC found a number of AI-generated ads running online without disclosures, including a fake video of New York Democratic Gov. Kathy Hochul that was posted by her opponent, Republican challenger Bruce Blakeman.

In Texas, Republican senatorial candidate Ken Paxton created an AI-generated video showing Democratic opponent James Talarico making controversial statements, with a three-second disclosure flashing in the middle. It's being shared as organic content on YouTube and X by Paxton's campaign. "Organic content" refers to free, unpaid posts shared on a profile or channel.

The Blakeman and Paxton campaigns didn't immediately respond to requests for comment.

YouTube told CNBC that the company applies "prominent labels to realistic AI content."

"For organic content on YouTube, political candidates can request removal where their face appears to be altered or generated by AI," the company said. "For advertisers, we require disclosure when an election ad contains synthetic or digitally altered people or events to provide viewers with transparency."

YouTube parent Google also requires political ads to complete its election ads verification process and include "paid for by" disclosures.

X says advertisers must comply with applicable laws, putting the responsibility on the advertiser, not the platform. The company didn't respond to CNBC's request for comment.

Meta referred to its guidelines on political ads, including restrictions on their use in the week prior to the election. The company also mandates "paid for by" labels and maintains an ad library of political ads. In June, the company ramped up its labeling, adding a new "about this ad" tool for ads that Meta detects have been created with third-party AI tools.

One risk the tech platforms face with generative AI is being asked to create misleading images and videos. The Brennan Center for Justice tested ChatGPT, Gemini, Grok, Claude, Perplexity and DeepSeek for their willingness to facilitate deceptive content. The study from August found that many models may avoid generating content around familiar conspiracies, but can generally be manipulated into creating misleading material.

A retest of those platforms in October found that ChatGPT had made progress. It had started redirecting prompts for harmful election images into illustrations so they look like comics, or refusing to take action on them altogether.

A number of national politicians are trying to address the issue.

The Protect Elections from Deceptive AI Act, backed by senators from both parties, would prohibit knowingly distributing materially deceptive AI-generated video or audio about federal candidates. Other bills are being led by Democrats in Congress, including the AI Ads Act, which is looking to expand current bans on campaign misrepresentation to include generative AI content.

None of the proposed bills have advanced out of committee.

#2981. Federal Reserve Board requests public comment on two proposals related to establishing a regulatory framework for Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 2:30 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday requested public comment on two proposals related to establishing a regulatory framework for Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act.

The first proposal would require that Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers fully back their stablecoins with certain permissible reserve assets, such as short-term Treasury bills and certain other high-quality, liquid assets. The proposal would also establish standardized capital requirements to address certain credit and operational risks of payment stablecoin activities, as well as risk management standards, in accordance with the law. Separately, the proposal would introduce rules for Board-supervised firms that safekeep the assets backing payment stablecoins. It would also clarify the permissibility of stablecoin and related activities for Board-supervised banks.

The second proposal would establish a tailored application process for Board-supervised banks applying to issue payment stablecoins. These applicants would be required to submit a business plan and financial information, among other documents. The proposal would also create a process governing appeals, hearings, and final determinations for applications.

The comment period will close 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

#2980. As AI becomes more fractured, one name stands out: AMD

As Anthropic rushes to takes its shares public, the AI trade is showing cracks, and they are not confined to fringe players.

We recently wrote about CoreWeave, which fell 15.5% on Oct. 14 as yields on its 2032 notes touched 15%, and its default-swap spreads nearly doubled in October. IREN is down more than 20% this year. Last Thursday, the contagion reached chipmakers, which makes sense since much of CRWV and IREN's spending is going there. AMD dropped 8.1% as Meta's financing plans and a supply warning from Lam Research exacerbated data-center fears about the pace of AI spending.

Because when you screen for factors that demonstrably generate outperformance over time, the list narrows quickly. My screen: price above the 200-day moving average; revenue growing at least as fast as the S&P 500 (5%+ per annum); EPS growing at least as fast (7%+); rating upgrades; a PEG below 3; and ROE and ROIC of at least 12%. Of the names reporting in the next two weeks, AMD is effectively the only one that makes the list. Yes, you can (and arguably should, as our staples article described) position your portfolio with slightly less tech exposure than the broad indices provide, but we can't ignore the market's biggest and most transformative sector entirely.

Especially because the growth in this case doesn't just meet my criteria; it blows past them. Consensus for Q3 calls for EPS of $1.94 on revenue of $13.06 billion, versus $1.20 and $9.25 billion a year ago. That's YoY growth of nearly 62% and 41%, respectively. The recent drawdown came from a sector selloff, not anything AMD reported, but it shows that if some names in the space get hit, even the larger players are likely to feel it. On Oct. 6, Lisa Su said AMD would substantially increase supply in 2027 because AI orders keep outrunning capacity.

So how do you balance factors we know work for a fast-growing company against a backdrop of cracks in the AI trade? Options.

Owning the stock outright means absorbing a roughly 9% to 11% implied earnings-related move (day of). Earnings are about a week out, and over the past four quarters the smallest move over a similar window was more than 9%, and the largest move, after the company reported its Q1 26 results, was 38%! Of course, that kind of volatility means options aren't cheap, and that rich premium is also a reason not to simply buy calls.

A call spread risk reversal does both jobs: it reduces risk if the cracks widen, and the short options offset the elevated volatility we would otherwise pay for. The bottom chart shows that, by sacrificing about $1,700 of upside for $5,000 in outperformance during a severe downside move, and given how jittery this trade has been lately, this is a way to stay in it with considerably less risk. $5,000 less, specifically. Of course, selling that out will tie up some cash, but less so than simply buying the stock. And selling both the out and the call reduces long call purchase by 66%

#2978. Meta seeks to raise up to $25 billion in debt sale as AI spending jumps

Meta is seeking to raise between $20 billion and $25 billion in a bond sale on Tuesday as the company ramps up its spending on artificial intelligence infrastructure along with AI models and services.

The debt sale is expected to be two-thirds in U.S. dollars and one-third in euros, according to people familiar with the matter who asked not to be named because the details are private. Final pricing for the bond sale should come Tuesday afternoon, the sources said.

Meta declined to comment. Bloomberg News first reported on Meta's plans.

Meta's bond sale comes days after S&P Global Ratings revised the company's outlook from AA- stable to AA- negative, citing "uncertainty" about its hefty capex spending and negative free cash flow.

In its third-quarter earnings report last month, the Facebook parent revealed that its full-year capex will come in between $135 billion to $145 billion, representing a $5 billion increase to the low end of its previous guidance. Meta posted negative free cash flow of $2.12 billion for its latest quarter, joining Alphabet and Amazon in reporting a negative figure.

Meta previously raised $25 billion in an April bond sale, and said last week that it plans to raise $95 billion in an effort to fund its AI initiatives. Of that, Meta intends to first raise $35 billion in underwritten public equity offerings, and an additional $60 billion through an at-the-market program.

Meta's stock rose 2.7% on Tuesday after dropping in 12 of the prior 14 trading sessions and closing out October with its worst month in four years.

#2973. The next wave of luxury real estate: marina developments with yacht parking

Marinas are becoming the next big draw for luxury real estate, as the global wealthy search for homes to park their ever-larger yachts, according to developers.

With the number of yachts surging since Covid-19, real estate developers and investors are rushing to fill the shortage of boat slips and marina communities. There are now over 6,000 superyachts over 30 meters (98 feet) around the world, and another 200 delivered every year, according to SuperYacht Times.

The growth in the size and number of yachts has created a shortage of yacht slips. And investors are rushing to fill demand. Last year, Blackstone paid $5.6 billion for Safe Harbor Marinas, which then paid $1.5 billion in August for 65 marinas and storage sites purchased from MarineMax. European private equity firms are also on a marina buying spree to fill the shortage in the Mediterranean.

Real estate developers now see marinas as a powerful magnet for ultra-wealthy buyers, especially in South Florida. Billionaire Ken Griffin is building a megayacht marina in Miami with eight megayacht slips, including one for his 308-foot yacht.

The largest and most ambitious is Related Group's $2 billion new marina development in Fort Lauderdale. Slated to break ground next year and open in 2030, the project will include a St. Regis Hotel, condos, a beach club and slips for yachts up to 350 feet long.

"Here in South Florida is there's been a scarcity of yachting destinations and marinas and slips," said Related Group's Nick Pérez. "So when you're able to combine irreplaceable real estate and a marina, you just have a winning formula."

The 40-acre development will include a St. Regis hotel with 122 rooms, a 78-residence condo tower, 106 resort residences, as well as a deepwater marina that can hold 250 boats. It will also include a sports and wellness club with a padel and tennis court, yoga lawn, fitness center and pools. And it will have its own beach club.

Fort Lauderdale has long been the yachting capital of the U.S., with a vast infrastructure of yacht repair and renovation companies, along with crew, brokers, charter experts and shipbuilder offices. An estimated 2,000 superyachts pass through Fort Lauderdale each year, according to tourism officials. Many yacht owners are looking for real estate not only for themselves but also their crew.

Pérez said the new St. Regis development will not only appeal to the yachting community but also to a growing number of international buyers who are looking for a lower-cost alternative to Palm Beach and Miami. Several buyers from Mexico and Brazil have already purchased in the building.

He said up to 30% of the buyers at the project could be international.

"Fort Lauderdale is no longer T-shirt shops and flip-flops, it's now a luxury destination, with national retailers like Rolex, Patek, Cartier," Pérez said. "When you look at Miami and Palm Beach and the growth, now it's Fort Lauderdale's time to shine."

While boat slips are the main attraction, many buyers in Fort Lauderdale are pouring in from Southern California and the Northeast to take advantage of Florida's lower taxes.

"With the taxation situation in California, we're seeing a lot of interest from Newport and the Laguna area and San Diego," Pérez said.

#2971. Federal Reserve Board finalizes changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 9:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Wednesday finalized changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements. The two final rules are largely similar to the proposed rules from 2025. The Board also requested comment on a proposal to better capture differences in banks' business models to generate fee income.

The Board conducts stress tests to ensure that large banks are sufficiently capitalized and able to lend to households and businesses even in a severe recession. In December 2024, the Board announced it would modify the test to improve its resilience.

"The stress test is an essential component of our regulatory capital framework," said Vice Chair for Supervision Michelle W. Bowman. "Today's changes preserve its resilience by ensuring that it is transparent, granular, and risk-sensitive. The public will now have greater assurance that the risks banks take will be reflected appropriately in their stress test losses and their capital requirements."

The first final rule requires the Board to invite public input annually on the stress test scenarios and any material model changes. It also updates the framework that guides the design of the hypothetical scenarios and adopts the models that will be used for the 2027 stress test. In addition, the final rule makes several adjustments to the stress test calendar. The final rule also updates the global market shock component, which is a set of shocks to specific market components applied to banks with large trading books. These banks will now be tested against two global market shock components each year, and the Board will use the shock that produces the largest losses for each firm to calculate the firm's stress test results.

The second final rule requires the Board, when calculating stress capital buffer requirements, to average the results from the two most recent annual supervisory stress tests for firms subject to the stress test in both years. The Board will begin averaging stress capital buffer requirements in 2028 to ensure that only models incorporating public input are used in the calculation.

The proposal would request public comment on a revision to the Board's noninterest income model that would improve the stress test's ability to capture business model diversity across firms. If adopted, these adjustments would replace the current noninterest income model that projects each bank's fee income under stress. Comments are due 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

Together, the changes are likely to reduce year-over-year volatility in capital requirements by approximately 50 percent and are not expected to materially affect aggregate capital requirements.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call 202-452-2955.

#2969. Sen. Chris Murphy still hospitalized after suffering a stroke over the weekend

Sen. Chris Murphy remained hospitalized Tuesday after suffering a stroke over the weekend, his office confirmed.

"He's continuing to improve and heartened by the outpouring of well wishes," Josh Sorbe, Murphy's communications director, told CNBC in an emailed statement. "He early voted in Connecticut, and he encourages everyone to make their voices heard today by voting and sending a message in this defining election."

His office did not offer additional details when asked about his status or his expected recovery.

The Senate is due back in session Monday following Tuesday's midterm elections.

Murphy began experiencing stroke symptoms on Saturday while at his son's hockey game, his chief of staff Allison Herwitt said in a statement Sunday.

"He was taken to a local hospital, where it was discovered he had experienced a stroke. He was transferred to Johns Hopkins for further monitoring and is responding well to treatment," Herwitt said.

Herwitt said at the time that Murphy was "awake and alert."

Johns Hopkins Hospital's main campus is in Baltimore, though it has other locations in and around Washington.

Murphy is not up for reelection on Tuesday but had been traveling extensively this fall in support of Democratic candidates. Murphy came to the Senate in 2013 after a six-year stint in the U.S. House. He's also been mentioned as a potential 2028 presidential candidate.

#2965. Ripple strikes deal with Robinhood to expand its RLUSD digital dollar

Ripple has struck a deal with Robinhood to expand the reach of its digital dollar, RLUSD, strengthening trading and financing links between their institutional businesses and improving Robinhood's internal money management.

The partnership involves using RLUSD to settle institutional trades, automate Robinhood's treasury operations and expand access to digital asset trading and financing through Ripple Prime and Bitstamp, the global crypto exchange acquired by Robinhood last year.

It also helps meet Robinhood's ambition of becoming a global financial superapp, centralizing its customers' financial lives while expanding the underlying infrastructure that can serve both retail and institutional clients.

"Utility is what moves stablecoins forward. Working with Ripple, Bitstamp and Robinhood will have access to RLUSD for institutional settlement, Ripple Treasury for our operations and Ripple Prime for financing," Robinhood Crypto General Manager Johann Kerbrat said in a statement shared with CNBC. "That is what a connected, global financial system looks like."

Stablecoins have long been a workhorse of the crypto world, giving traders a way to move money among exchanges, buy and sell crypto assets, and park funds between trades without having to convert back into traditional currency. But as the crypto industry evolves beyond trading and markets move toward 24/7 liquidity, stablecoins are becoming essential financial infrastructure because of their ability to enable near-instant settlement and move dollar-denominated value at any time, regardless of traditional banking hours.

As firms look to do more with stablecoins beyond simply listing them on exchanges, stablecoin issuers are betting they'll favor a one-stop-shop approach that bundles the technology and services needed to move money, settle trades and manage cash.

"Stablecoins have evolved from a crypto trading tool into a critical piece of financial infrastructure," Ripple President Monica Long told CNBC. "The clearest evidence of this is where they're now being embedded — from corporate payments to capital markets. Our partnership with Robinhood reflects that evolution."

"This is about much more than trading," she added. "It's about fundamentally changing how money moves and is managed — giving institutions the ability to operate 24/7/365, unlocking liquidity and putting capital to work more efficiently."

Circle Internet Group, a Ripple competitor, is positioning itself for a similar future, where winning platforms, in addition to issuing stablecoins, also provide the infrastructure to put them to work. Earlier this year, for example, it launched the Circle Payments Network, connecting financial institutions and payment providers using its flagship USDC stablecoin and other digital assets for cross-border payments.

The network is Circle's answer to the 8-year-old Ripple Payments business, a service that helps businesses move money across borders, using any of several assets moving through that system, including USDC, Ripple-created XRP and, as of its debut in December 2024, RLUSD.

RLUSD's market cap has grown to more than $2.5 billion. Tether's USDT and Circle's USDC still dominate the total stablecoin market, which now exceeds $300 billion.

RLUSD will also be listed on Robinhood's trading platform, and is currently available to traders on Bitstamp as well as Robinhood Chain, the company's home-grown blockchain for tokenized stocks and other assets.

Robinhood, which reports third-quarter results for the three months ended in September after the market closes, rose about 2.5% in early trading Tuesday, snapping a three-day decline.

Correction: A previous headline misspelled RLUSD.

#2960. 2. Bundesliga: Clinical Nürnberg punish wasteful St. Pauli at the Millerntor

An unfortunate early own goal from Eric Smith gave Miroslav Klose's side the lead, but despite St. Pauli dominating large spells of the contest, the hosts struggled to make their opportunities count. Abdoulie Ceesay eventually restored parity in the second half, only for substitute Sigurd Haugen to put Nürnberg back in front before 17-year-old Marc Kornwachs Barjuan sealed the victory in the closing stages.

Nürnberg found themselves under pressure from the opening exchanges, with St. Pauli looking to make their home advantage count. Abdoulie Ceesay came close to opening the scoring inside two minutes, failing to make sufficient contact with a cross from the left, before Joel Chima Fujita sent an effort narrowly over the crossbar shortly afterwards.

Despite the hosts' promising start, it was Nürnberg who took the lead in the seventh minute through an unfortunate own goal. Julian Justvan drove towards the byline on the right before attempting to cut the ball back into the penalty area, where Eric Smith inadvertently diverted the delivery into his own net.

St. Pauli responded positively and came agonisingly close to equalising in the 17th minute when Louis Oppie struck the crossbar with a powerful effort following a corner. The hosts continued to dominate, with Connor Metcalfe wasting a particularly good opportunity in the 31st minute after finding himself through on goal, only to drag his low finish narrowly wide.

Branimir Hrgota and Ceesay also threatened as the half progressed, but Nürnberg goalkeeper Jan Reichert proved an important figure for the visitors. His best contribution came in stoppage time when he produced an excellent save to deny Ceesay from close range, ensuring Nürnberg maintained their fortunate advantage heading into the break.

St. Pauli continued where they left off after the interval, although Nürnberg initially looked more organised defensively. However, the hosts eventually found the breakthrough they deserved in the 58th minute. After winning possession high up the pitch, it was Arkadiusz Pyrka who collected the ball on the right before delivering a precise low cross into the six-yard box, where Ceesay was on hand to turn home and level the game at 1-1.

Despite the equaliser, it was Klose's substitutions that would ultimately prove decisive. Having introduced Sigurd Haugen in the 65th minute, the Nürnberg forward needed just six minutes to make his mark. A poor backpass from Oppie allowed Haugen to race through on goal and round Voll, before finishing from an incredibly tight angle to restore the visitors' advantage in the 71st minute.

St. Pauli attempted to respond but struggled to find a way through Nürnberg's increasingly organised defence. The visitors continued to frustrate the hosts, with several bookings arriving during a heated closing period as St. Pauli searched for another equaliser.

Nürnberg then put the result beyond doubt in the 90th minute when another substitute Marc Kornwachs Barjuan found the net via a stunning strike to make it 3-1, completing an excellent afternoon for Klose's side.

The final whistle confirmed a valuable away victory for Nürnberg, ending their four-match winless run in all competitions and strengthening their position in the promotion race. For St. Pauli, meanwhile, the defeat brought an end to their six-game unbeaten run, with their inability to convert a number of excellent first-half opportunities ultimately proving costly.

#2957. CrowdStrike launches AI kill switch that it says can stop internal agents that go rogue

CrowdStrike is betting that it has a solution to rogue artificial intelligence.

The cybersecurity vendor on Tuesday launched an AI kill switch designed to let security teams stop internal AI agents that go rogue, block them from relaunching and cut off their enterprise network access. The tool is built into CrowdStrike's Falcon Guardian platform, launched in September to secure AI agents. CrowdStrike also announced an integration with Anthropic's Claude Cowork.

"Having the ability to control and turn off, and make a decision and decide, I think that's really important for this next chapter of AI adoption," Chief Business Officer Daniel Bernard told CNBC.

A string of recent high-profile cyber incidents has added urgency to calls for some kind of emergency shutdown mechanism.

The Hugging Face breach orchestrated by rogue OpenAI agents in July was particularly alarming for businesses. Last week, Binance disclosed the largest crypto cyberattack on record and hackers have recently targeted hundreds of refrigerators and freezers at supermarkets in France.

Over the summer, U.S. representatives introduced bipartisan legislation that would require AI companies to build shutdown capabilities into their models.

Critics argue, however, that an emergency stop button is far too complicated to implement across multiple systems, data centers and complex AI models.

Bernard said the kill switch operates like a circuit breaker in systems where customers use its Falcon platform. The process can detect internal agent activity but requires a human to decide to cut the agent off and reinstate access.

"If you kill a process at the root, and we have that level of access at the root … it's very binary," Bernard said. "You either kill the AI and the agent, or you don't, and then you report on that."

The kill switch doesn't delete the agent's code but uses connected AI gateways to cut off access to the AI models that influence decisions, CrowdStrike said.

In the age of AI, businesses are moving quickly to adopt autonomous tools, often without the necessary safeguards and protections.

CrowdStrike believes its new tool will let companies stop AI agent 'misbehavior in its tracks, while leaving room for innovation.

The company also announced plans to acquire AI observability startup Fiddler AI and AI security testing firm Dreadnode.

CrowdStrike and its rivals are rapidly scooping up cybersecurity startups to keep up with increasing security demands. The need for AI security tools has also powered a broader wave of cybersecurity deal-making.

#2955. AI debt fears ease ahead of Oracle, Alphabet and Amazon updates. Here's what to watch.

Fears over artificial intelligence debt have eased after a bout of volatility. But more tests for AI debt are coming.

Oracle holds its Investor Day on Wednesday. And hyperscalers Alphabet and Amazon.com are scheduled to report quarterly results after the market close on Wednesday and Thursday, respectively.

Credit markets are on edge over a flood of AI debt, though fears have eased up a little lately. Meta Platforms' plans for a $95 billion stock offering, announced on Monday, helped bring down its credit-default swap spreads. Those spreads had risen last week when the company reported negative free cash flow in the third quarter. Other CDS spreads had moved higher too, though they have since backed off.

Investors had expected Meta to bring a large bond deal to market shortly after its earnings announcement, said Richard Cheng, head of Nuveen's investment-grade corporate sector team. The fact that it wasn't announced came as a relief.

"Just the absence of the bond deal itself is a direct catalyst for AI-related spread tightening," he said.

A credit spread is the difference in the yield between the corporate bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. When spreads tighten, it means investors are becoming more confident about the bond issuer.

The market action over the last few days shows that investors are recognizing that "credit fears may have been overblown," said CNBC contributor Paul Meeks, head of technology research at Freedom Capital Markets.

"I expect that these better feelings, maybe not good but better, may continue as companies announce their quarters over the next few weeks."

Still, spreads on CDS remain wider over the past year due to a surge of debt issuance and free cash flows turning negative across the AI space. Meta's CDS spread, for instance, is still up 177% year to date, while Amazon's spread has gained 129%.

"It's not necessarily fundamentals," said CreditSights analyst Jordan Chalfin. "It's more, I'd say, 80% driven by technicals and sentiment."

Alphabet and Amazon pose the next big hurdle for tech bonds as they update their capex plans and free cash flow forecasts. Microsoft's earnings are also due Wednesday, but the company has yet to issue any bonds to pay for its AI spending.

Both Amazon and Alphabet reported negative free cash flow in the second quarter and analysts expect that trend to continue.

Analysts expect Alphabet to report free cash flow of -$2.3 billion while spending $55.2 billion on capital expenditures in the third quarter, according to FactSet consensus estimates. Amazon is expected to report -$9.9 billion in free cash flow against capex of $58.7 billion.

S&P Global Ratings estimated in an August report that the hyperscalers will have negative free operating cash flow in 2026 and 2027.

More critical will be their outlooks, Cheng says. "The singular focus on the investors' minds is really what the capex guidance trajectory looks like across the hyperscaler space."

Meeks believes elevated capex is a sign of strong AI demand. The hyperscalers have "decided that with all the evidence they have — and they have more than you and me — that this is worthwhile spending, and not just worthwhile; it's required," he said.

CreditSights' Chalfin doesn't expect the hyperscalers to announce major new bond issuance around their earnings reports. Oracle's updates may be more critical for credit markets. The company has issued $117 billion in long-term debt and is clinging to an investment-grade credit rating of BBB-, the lowest level of investment grade above junk, according to S&P.

"It's the most important IG tech credit, just given how it's a bit more stressed relative to the A-rated names," he said. "It's trading more like a high-yield credit, even though it's IG, and so there's just a lot of investor concern on that one."

One wrinkle to the AI debt story: balance sheets don't reveal the whole picture. The companies are using complicated off-balance sheet arrangements for data centers and chip financing, and they aren't fully transparent, S&P Global said in a September report.

"There are commitments they are entering into, for example, into data center leases or purchase commitments on chips that aren't necessarily treated as debt," Mark Habib, digital infrastructure sector lead at S&P Global Ratings, said on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" Monday.

S&P is trying to provide the market with more visibility on those commitments "to give a fuller picture of what kinds of obligations they're actually entering into," he added.

Oracle aside, hyperscaler credit ratings are all comfortably above A levels — reflecting the fact that they have broadly diversified revenue streams, ample coverage for interest payments, and capacity to add leverage if they choose.

Nuveen's Cheng isn't concerned, pointing out that while there has been some credit repricing, there is no credit crisis.

"They continue to have exceptional credit profiles, strong cash generation liquidity," he said of the largest hyperscalers.

"The market is really not concerned about credit quality at this point," Cheng added. "It's really just the trajectory of the capex needs and the supply of debt to fund that. Both of those are pointing in one direction — and that's up."

Correction: This story has been revised to reflect that Oracle's investment-grade credit rating is BBB-, according to S&P. A previous version mischaracterized the company's credit rating.

#2951. AMD has been crushed into earnings. Here’s one way to trade it

Advanced Micro Devices has been under significant pressure leading up to earnings after the close. The stock has been caught in the swings of the AI debate all year, but this most recent leg lower was triggered by a wave of disappointing tech earnings.

A record drop in Alphabet last Thursday was followed by weak results from Amazon, as well as AMD's long-standing CPU rival, Intel. Intel guided fourth-quarter revenue well below consensus and expects the PC slowdown to last through at least the first half of next year. That sent Intel's stock down 29% on Friday, its worst day since the 1970s. Caught in the fallout of Intel and the broader tech sector, AMD fell 10.9% that day and slid another 7.5% Monday.

For the past week, AMD has traded on other companies' news. Now its own numbers get a vote.  AMD's stock reactions have been mixed in recent quarters, sensitive to guidance and high investor expectations. It fell 17% after Q4 2025 results, when a beat and a guide above estimates still underwhelmed some analysts. It rose 18.6% after Q1 2026 results, when it guided about 6.5% above expectations. It fell 7% after Q2 2026 results, when a guide above consensus still fell short of the $14 billion some analysts wanted. That may make direction hard to call, but options let investors build a trade that doesn't have to pick a side.

Based on Monday's close, I'm selling the November 4 425/415 put spread and the 510/520 call spread for a credit of roughly $3.65.

AMD's 30-day implied volatility is elevated going into earnings, closing Monday higher than on about 81% of days over the past year. This means sellers are collecting richer premium than usual. To capture that premium while limiting time beyond the earnings announcement, the trade uses the November 4 weeklies. These contracts imply a move of about 9% through Wednesday, in line with AMD's 8.4% average close-to-close move over its last 10 reports. The short strikes sit at the edges of the expected move, with long strikes $10 away to limit capital at risk going into earnings. This isn't a bet that options are mispricing the move or a directional call. It's a neutral play that aims to profit from the elevated premium that tends to come out after the print.

The trade reaches its maximum profit of $365 if AMD closes between $425 and $510 on Wednesday. Breakevens are $421.35 and $513.65, about 9.7% below and 10.0% above Monday's close, just outside the expected move. The maximum loss of $635 comes if AMD closes below $415 or above $520, roughly 11% in either direction. That has happened in 11 of its last 40 reports, and AMD has had moves as large as 19% in its last four, emphasizing the importance of capping the risk with the long wings.

If AMD finishes between $415 and $425, the short put can be assigned, creating an obligation to buy 100 shares per spread at $425. After the credit, the cost basis is $421.35, or roughly 35% below the Oct. 6 closing high. For investors who like AMD's long-term story, that's a way to end up owning the stock at a discount. A finish between $510 and $520 can assign the short call and leave a short stock position. For investors looking to avoid either risk, consider managing the position before Wednesday's close.

#2947. Federal Reserve Board announces enforcement action against American Express Company to address, among other things, the firm’s failure to sufficiently detect and report certain suspicious activity related to money laundering

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 4:30 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday announced an enforcement action against American Express Company to address, among other things, the firm's failure to sufficiently detect and report certain suspicious activity related to money laundering.

The Board also identified significant deficiencies in how American Express Company's enterprise-wide anti-money laundering program was implemented, in particular at the firm's subsidiary national bank. The Office of the Comptroller of the Currency, the primary federal regulator of the firm's national bank, has issued a separate enforcement action against the bank.

The Board's action will help ensure that the firm operates in compliance with all U.S. laws and regulations.
